\lx@nobreakspace\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}\sum_{i\in J_{v}^{k},j\in J_{w}^{\ell}}\xi_{i,j}v_{i}^{2}{w_{j}}^{2}\leq\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}2^{-2(k+\ell)}\sum_{i\in J_{v}^{k},j\in J_{w}^{\ell}}\xi_{i,j}.

Now, for any
k\in I_{v}
and
\ell\in I_{w}
,

(4.14)

\lx@nobreakspace 2^{2(k+1)}\alpha_{v}^{k}\geq|J_{v}^{k}|\geq r_{k}2^{2k},\ 2^{2(\ell+1)}\alpha_{w}^{\ell}\geq|J_{w}^{\ell}|\geq r_{\ell}2^{2\ell}.

One can check that
k\mapsto k^{-2}2^{2k}
on
[2,+\infty)
is increasing, and as a consequence
k\mapsto r_{k}2^{2k}
as well. This implies that for any
k\in I_{v}
,
|J_{v}^{k}|\geq r_{k_{0}}2^{2k_{0}}\geq\delta\varepsilon^{-2}
. Note that as
w\in\mathcal{D}_{M/\sqrt{np}}
,
\alpha_{w}^{\ell}=0
for
\ell<\ell_{0}
. Therefore, for any
\ell\in I_{w}
,
|J_{w}^{\ell}|\geq r_{\ell_{0}}2^{2\ell_{0}}\geq\delta np/M^{2}
.
Now, for fixed sets
S,T\subset[n]
and
\eta\geq 3p
(
3
is arbitrary, any numerical constant strictly greater than
e
works), we claim that

(4.15)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}\xi_{i,j}>2(p+\eta)|S|\cdot|T|\Big)\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}|S|\cdot|T|\Lambda_{p}^{*}(\eta)}\leq e^{-c\eta|S|\cdot|T|\log(\eta/p)},

where
c>0
is a numerical constant. Indeed, we can write
\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}\xi_{i,j}\leq 2\sum_{(i,j)\in E}\xi_{i,j}
,
where
E:=\big((S\cap T)^{2}\cap\{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}:i\leq j\}\big)\cup(S\setminus T\times T)\cup(S\times T\setminus S)
. One can check that
(\xi_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in E}
is a family of independent Bernoulli random variables, and that
(1/2)|S|\cdot|T|\leq|E|\leq|S|\cdot|T|
.
Therefore the first inequality in (
4.15
) follows from Chernoff’s inequality. To derive the second inequality we use
Lemma
4.5
(iii).

Next let
s,t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\delta\varepsilon^{-2}\leq s
and
t\geq\delta np/M^{2}
.
As for any
\ell\in[n]
, there are at most
(en/\ell)^{\ell}
subsets of
[n]
of size
\ell
, we get from (
4.15
) by a union bound, for any
\eta\geq 3p
,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|S|=s,|T|=t}\sum_{i\in Sj\in T}\xi_{i,j}>2(p+\eta)st\Big)\leq e^{2(s\vee t)\log\frac{{e}n}{s\vee t}}e^{-c\eta st\log(\eta/p)}.

Since
M\geq 1
and
\log(M^{2}/\delta)\leq\gamma\log(1/p)
, we have

\lx@nobreakspace\frac{\log(\delta/p)}{\log(M^{2}/(\delta p))}\geq\frac{\log(\delta/(M^{2}p))}{\log(M^{2}/(\delta p))}\geq\frac{1-\gamma}{1+\gamma}.

We deduce that there exists
\gamma^{\prime}>0
depending on
\gamma
such that for any
\eta\geq\max(\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{s\wedge t},\delta)
,

\lx@nobreakspace c\eta(s\wedge t)\log(\eta/p)-2\log\frac{{e}n}{s\vee t}\geq c\eta(s\wedge t)\log(\eta/p)-2\log\Big(\frac{{e}M^{2}}{\delta p}\Big)\geq\frac{c}{2}\eta(s\wedge t)\log(\delta/p).

Again, as
M\geq 1
and
\log(M^{2}/\delta)\leq\gamma\log(1/p)
, we have
\log(\delta/p)/\log(1/p)\geq(1-\gamma)>0
. Fix
r>0
.
At the price of enlarging
\gamma^{\prime}>0
, we get for any
\eta\geq\max\big(\frac{\gamma^{\prime}rnp}{st},\delta\big)
,

\lx@nobreakspace\frac{c}{2}\eta(s\vee t)\cdot(s\wedge t)\log(\delta/p)=\frac{c}{2}\eta st\log(\delta/p)\geq rnp\log(1/p).

Thus we have shown that there exists a constant
\gamma^{\prime}>0
depending on
\gamma
such that

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|S|=s,|T|=t}\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}\xi_{i,j}>2(p+\eta_{s,t})st\Big)\leq e^{-rnp\log(1/p)},

where
\eta_{s,t}=\max\big(\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{s\wedge t},\frac{\gamma^{\prime}rnp}{st},\delta,3p\big)
.
Using another union bound we obtain

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigcup_{t\geq\delta np/M^{2}\atop s\geq\delta\varepsilon^{-2}}\Big\{\sup_{|S|=s,|T|=t}\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}\xi_{i,j}>2(p+\eta_{s,t})st\Big\}\bigg)\leq n^{2}e^{-rnp\log(1/p)}.

Let
\mathcal{E}
denote the event on the left hand side above. Recall that for any
k\in I_{v}
,
|J_{v}^{k}|\geq\delta\varepsilon^{-2}
and any
\ell\in I_{w}
,
|J_{w}^{\ell}|\geq\delta np/M^{2}
. For such
k,\ell
, set
\eta_{k,\ell}
to be a short hand for
\eta_{|J_{v}^{k}|,|J_{w}^{\ell}|}
. On
\mathcal{E}^{c}
, using (
4.13
) we get

\displaystyle\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}\sum_{i\in J_{v}^{k},j\in J_{w}^{\ell}}\xi_{i,j}v_{i}^{2}{w_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}2^{1-2(k+\ell)}|J_{v}^{k}|.|J_{w}^{\ell}|(p+\eta_{k,\ell}).

To complete the proof we need to bound the right hand side of the above. To this end, we use the bound
\eta_{k,\ell}\leq\gamma^{\prime}/(|J_{v}^{k}|\wedge|J_{w}^{\ell}|)+\gamma^{\prime}rnp/(|J_{v}^{k}|.|J_{w}^{\ell}|)+\delta+3p
, and compute the contributions of each term.
The upper bounds on
|J_{v}^{k}|
and
|J_{w}^{\ell}|
from (
4.14
), and
\sum_{k}\alpha_{v}^{k}=\sum_{\ell}\alpha_{w}^{\ell}=1
yield

\lx@nobreakspace\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}2^{-2(k+\ell)}|J_{v}^{k}|.|J_{w}^{\ell}|\leq 16,\quad\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}2^{-2(k+\ell)}\leq 2^{-2(k_{0}+\ell_{0}-2)}\leq 144\frac{\varepsilon^{2}M^{2}}{np},

and

\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}2^{-2(k+\ell)}|J_{w}^{\ell}|\vee|J_{v}^{k}|\leq\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}2^{-2(k+\ell)}(|J_{w}^{\ell}|+|J_{v}^{k}|)\\
\leq\Big(\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}2^{-2k}\Big)\Big(\sum_{\ell}4\alpha_{w}^{\ell}\Big)+\Big(\sum_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}2^{-2\ell}\Big)\Big(\sum_{k}4\alpha_{v}^{k}\Big)\leq 128\varepsilon^{2}.

Using all the estimates above and the fact that
\delta\geq\max(\varepsilon^{2},3p)
, we get

\lx@nobreakspace\sum_{(k,\ell)\in I_{v}\times I_{w}}\sum_{i\in J_{v}^{k},j\in J_{w}^{\ell}}\xi_{i,j}v_{i}^{2}{w_{j}}^{2}\leq 32(\delta+4p)+288\gamma^{\prime}\big(\varepsilon^{2}+r(\varepsilon M)^{2}\big)\leq\gamma^{\prime\prime}(\delta+r(\varepsilon M)^{2}),

where
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is constant depending on
\gamma
. Combining the above inequality with (
4.12
) and choosing
r=\delta/(\varepsilon M)^{2}
, this concludes the proof.
∎

Equipped with Lemma
4.3
, we can now give a proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Let
\widehat{X}^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}\circ\Xi^{\prime}
be an independent copy of
\widehat{X}
. For any
(i,j)\in[n]^{2}
,
i\leq j
, let
\widehat{X}_{(i,j)}^{\prime}
be the matrix with the same entries as
\widehat{X}
except that the
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
coefficients are replaced by
\widehat{X}_{i,j}^{\prime}
. Define the random variable
V_{+}
as

\lx@nobreakspace V_{+}=\sum_{i\leq j}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\big[\big(f(\widehat{X})-f(\widehat{X}_{(i,j)}^{\prime})\big)_{+}^{2}\big],

where
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
means that the expectation is with respect to
\widehat{X}^{\prime}
.
For any
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
we have by (
4.8
)

f(\widehat{X})-f(\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{(i,j)})\leq\langle\zeta(\widehat{X}),\widehat{X}-\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{(i,j)}\rangle=(\zeta(\widehat{X})_{i,j}+\zeta(\widehat{X})_{j,i}{\bf 1}_{i\neq j})\big(\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}-\xi_{i,j}^{\prime}G^{\prime}_{i,j}\big).

Therefore,

(4.16)

\lx@nobreakspace V_{+}\leq 2\sum_{i,j\in[n]}(\zeta(\widehat{X})_{i,j})^{2}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\big[(\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}-\xi^{\prime}_{i,j}G^{\prime}_{i,j})^{2}\big].

Using the bound
|\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}-\xi^{\prime}_{i,j}G^{\prime}_{i,j}|\leq 2
and the fact that
\zeta(\widehat{X})\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{\varepsilon,M}
, one obtains
V_{+}\leq 8\|\zeta(\widehat{X})\|_{2}^{2}\leq 8k
almost surely. Using Lemma
4.3
, we will show that
V_{+}
is actually much smaller with overwhelming probability. Coming back at (
4.16
) and writing
\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}-\xi^{\prime}_{i,j}G^{\prime}_{i,j}=\xi_{i,j}(G_{i,j}-G^{\prime}_{i,j})+(\xi_{i,j}-\xi_{i,j}^{\prime})G^{\prime}_{i,j}
, we get

\displaystyle\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[(\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}-\xi_{i,j}^{\prime}G^{\prime}_{i,j})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\big[\big(\xi_{i,j}(G_{i,j}-G^{\prime}_{i,j})\big)^{2}\big]+2\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\big[\big((\xi_{i,j}-\xi^{\prime}_{i,j})G^{\prime}_{i,j}\big)^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq 8\xi_{i,j}+2\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[(\xi_{i,j}-\xi_{i,j}^{\prime})^{2}]\leq 10\xi_{i,j}+2p.

where in the last step we used
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[(\xi_{i,j}-\xi_{i,j}^{\prime})^{2}]\leq\xi_{i,j}+p
. Using the fact that
\zeta(\widehat{X})\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon,M}^{k}
, and as a consequence
\|\zeta(\widehat{X})\|_{2}^{2}\leq k
, we get from (
4.16
)

\lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace V_{+}\leq 20k\sup_{v\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon},w\in\mathcal{D}_{M}/\sqrt{np}}\lx@nobreakspace\sum_{i,j\in[n]}v_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}\xi_{i,j}+4kp.

By Lemma
4.3
, there exists
\gamma^{\prime}\geq 1
depending on
\gamma
such that for any
\delta\geq\max(\varepsilon^{2},3p)
and
\log(M^{2}/\delta)\leq\gamma\log(1/p)
,

(4.17)

\mathbb{P}\big(V_{+}\geq 24k\gamma^{\prime}\delta\big)\leq n^{2}e^{-\delta np\frac{\log(1/p)}{(\varepsilon M)^{2}}}.

Assume
\eta:=\varepsilon M/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}\leq e^{-1}
and take
\delta=\max(\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)},\varepsilon^{2})
. Since
\varepsilon^{2}\geq 3p
and
\log(M^{2}/\varepsilon^{2})\leq\gamma\log(1/p)
, we can apply the above inequality (
4.17
). As we assumed
np\log(1/p)/M^{2}\geq 4\log n
and
\delta\geq\varepsilon^{2}
, this yields

(4.18)

\mathbb{P}\big(V_{+}\geq 24k\gamma^{\prime}\delta\big)\leq e^{-\frac{\delta}{2\eta^{2}}np}.

Let
E^{c}
denote the event on the left-hand side and set
q_{0}:=2\log(1/\mathbb{P}(E^{c}))/\log(1/(3\gamma^{\prime}\delta))
.
Applying Lemma
4.4
to
f/\sqrt{8k}
(
V_{+}\leq 8k
almost surely), it follows that there exists a constant
\gamma^{\prime\prime}>0
depending on
\gamma
and
k
such that

(4.19)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\big(f(\widehat{X})-\mathbb{E}f(\widehat{X})>t\big)\leq e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}\delta}},

for any
2\leq t^{2}/(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\delta)\leq q_{0}
. From (
4.18
) and
\gamma^{\prime}\geq 1
, we have
q_{0}\geq\delta np/[\eta^{2}\log(1/\delta)]
.
As in addition
\delta\leq 1
, (
4.19
) holds in particular when
2\gamma^{\prime\prime}\leq t^{2}\leq\gamma^{\prime\prime}\delta^{2}np/[\eta^{2}\log(1/\delta)]
.
Finally, we observe that as
\eta\leq e^{-1}
,

\lx@nobreakspace\frac{\delta^{2}}{\eta^{2}\log(1/\delta)}\geq\frac{\log(1/\eta)}{\log\Big(\frac{1}{\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)}}\Big)}\geq 1,

which ends the proof.
∎

The second step towards proving Proposition
4.1
is the following result which says that the deviations of the quadratic form induced by
X
over vectors whose support is of size much smaller than
np
are negligible at the exponential scale
np
. We will use this result repetitively in the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
1.4
to identify the structure of the top eigenvector.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
X=G\circ\Xi/\sqrt{np}
,
G
and
\Xi
are independent symmetric random matrices where
(G_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
are independent centered and bounded by
1
and
\Xi
has i.i.d.
\dBer(p)
entries on and above the diagonal. There exist numerical constants
p_{0},C>0
such that if
p\leq p_{0}
then for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
t\geq\varepsilon\sqrt{C\log(1/(\varepsilon^{2}p))/\log(1/p)}
,

(4.20)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{v,w\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\langle v,Xw\rangle\geq t\Big)\leq e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{C}np\log(1/p)},\,\text{ where }\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}:=\{u\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:|\mathrm{supp}(u)|\leq\varepsilon^{2}np\}.

Proof.

Fix
v,w\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. By Chernoff’s inequality, we have for any
\theta\geq 0
,

(4.21)

\mathbb{P}\big(\langle v,(G\circ\Xi)w\rangle\geq t\sqrt{np}\big)\leq e^{-\theta npt}\prod_{i<j}\mathbb{E}\Big(e^{2\theta\sqrt{np}\lx@nobreakspace v_{i}w_{j}G_{i,j}\xi_{i,j}}\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(e^{\theta\sqrt{np}v_{i}w_{i}G_{i,i}\xi_{i,i}}\Big).

We claim that there exists
p_{0}>0
such that if
Z
is a centered random variable bounded by
1
and
\zeta
an independent Bernoulli random variable with parameter
p\leq p_{0}
, then for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(4.22)

\log\mathbb{E}\big(e^{sZ\zeta}\big)\leq\frac{s^{2}}{\log(1/p)}.

Indeed, by Lemma
4.5
there exists
p_{0}>0
such that for any
p\leq p_{0}
and for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Lambda_{p}(\theta)\leq\theta^{2}/(2\log(1/p))
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{\zeta}
denote the expectation with respect to
\zeta
. Then,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}_{\zeta}\big(e^{sZ\zeta}\big)\leq e^{\frac{s^{2}Z^{2}}{2\log(1/p)}+psZ}\leq e^{\frac{s^{2}}{2\log(1/p)}+psZ}.

By Hoeffding’s lemma (see
[
24
, Lemma 2.2]
) we have
\mathbb{E}\big(e^{spZ}\big)\leq\exp(s^{2}p^{2}/2)
. Therefore integrating the above inequality with respect to
Z
and using that
p^{2}\leq 1/\log(1/p)
for any
p\in(0,1)
, we get
\mathbb{E}\big(e^{sZ\zeta}\big)\leq\exp(s^{2}/\log(1/p))
,
which proves the claim (
4.22
). Now, by (
4.21
)–(
4.22
) and the fact that
v,w\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
,

(4.23)

\log\mathbb{P}\big(\langle v,(G\circ\Xi)w\rangle\geq t\sqrt{np}\big)\leq{-np\sup_{\theta\geq 0}\Big\{\theta t-\frac{2\theta^{2}}{\log(1/p)})\Big\}}=-\frac{t^{2}}{8}np\log(1/p).

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a
1/4
-net of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
for the
\ell^{2}
-norm and
m=\lfloor\varepsilon^{2}np\rfloor
.
Note that there are at most
m(en/m)^{m}
subsets
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
such that
|\mathcal{J}|\leq m
. Since
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
is the union of the corresponding unit balls
\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}
, it follows from
[
3
, Corollary 4.1.15]
that one can find such a net
\mathcal{N}
with
\#\mathcal{N}\leq m(en/m)^{m}12^{m}\leq(n/m)^{3m}
, for
p
sufficiently small. Then, using a similar argument as in
[
49
, Lemma 4.4.1]
, we find

(4.24)

\sup_{v,w\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\langle v,(G\circ\Xi)w\rangle\leq 2\sup_{v,w\in\mathcal{N}}\langle v,(G\circ\Xi)w\rangle.

Using (
4.23
), (
4.24
), the bound on
\#\mathcal{N}
, and a union bound, we get

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{v,w\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\langle v,(G\circ\Xi)w\rangle\geq t\sqrt{np}\Big)\leq\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}p}\Big)^{3\varepsilon^{2}np}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{32}np\log(1/p)}\leq e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{64}np\log(1/p)},

for any
t^{2}\geq 192\varepsilon^{2}\log(1/(\varepsilon^{2}p))/\log(1/p)
. This completes the proof.
∎

It remains to prove Proposition
4.1
. The proof will use Proposition
4.2
and will involve careful choices of certain parameters.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

For any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, write
w=\widehat{w}+\widecheck{w}
, where
\widecheck{w}_{i}=w_{i}{\bf 1}_{|w_{i}|>M/\sqrt{np}}
for any
i\in[n]
, where
M\geq 1
is some constant to be determined below.
For
\zeta(K)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\theta_{\ell}w_{\ell}w_{\ell}^{\sf T}\in\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{k}
, with
\varepsilon
as in (
4.4
), we let
\zeta_{2}(K):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\theta_{\ell}\widecheck{w}_{\ell}{\widecheck{w}_{\ell}}^{\sf T}
and
\zeta_{1}(K):=\zeta(K)-\zeta_{2}(K)
. Because of the assumption (
4.3
) we can write

f(K^{\prime})=\sup_{K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}}\{f(K)+\langle\zeta(K),K^{\prime}-K\rangle\},\ \forall K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Define

f_{1}(K^{\prime}):=\sup_{K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}}\{f(K)-\langle\zeta_{2}(K),K\rangle+\langle\zeta_{1}(K),K^{\prime}-K\rangle\}\text{ and }f_{2}(K^{\prime}):=\sup_{K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}}|\langle\zeta_{2}(K),K^{\prime}\rangle|,

for
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
(note that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
depend implicitly on
\varepsilon
and
M
).

Now fix
t_{0}=\sqrt{\pi C_{0}k^{2}}
for some large constant
C_{0}<+\infty
, to be specified below. Assume
t_{0}/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}\leq t\leq 1/t_{0}
, where
p\leq p_{0}
and
p_{0}\in(0,1)
is some small parameter (depending on
t_{0}
) to be chosen later. Set
M=t_{0}/t
for the rest of the proof.
Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
from (
4.20
) we see that
\widecheck{w}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}_{1/M}
. Further
|\theta_{\ell}|\leq 1
for
\ell\in[k]
. Therefore,
f_{2}(K^{\prime})\leq k\sup_{u,v\in\mathcal{S}_{1/M}}\lx@nobreakspace\langle u,K^{\prime}v\rangle
for
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. By our choice of
M
, if
p_{0}
is sufficiently small, we have
(1/M)\sqrt{C\log(M^{2}/p)/\log(1/p)}\leq t/4
, where
C
is as in Lemma
4.6
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.6
, for any
s\geq t/4
,

(4.25)

\mathbb{P}\big(f_{2}(X)>s\big)\leq e^{-\frac{s^{2}np}{Ck^{2}}\log(1/p)}.

Integrating the above inequality yields
\mathbb{E}f_{2}(X)\leq\frac{t}{4}+\sqrt{\pi Ck^{2}/\log(1/p)}
.
By taking
C_{0}
(and hence
t_{0}
) large enough we obtain
\mathbb{E}f_{2}(X)\leq t/3
. Since
|f-f_{1}|\leq f_{2}
, this implies that

(4.26)

\mathbb{E}f(X)\leq\mathbb{E}f_{1}(X)+t/3.

On the other hand, note that by construction
\zeta_{1}(K)\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon,M}^{k}
for any
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon,M}^{k}
is defined in (
4.7
).
Set
\delta=\varepsilon/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}
. Assuming that
np\geq 4\log n
,
t_{0}/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}\leq t\leq 1/t_{0}
, and
\log(t^{2}\log(1/p))/[t\sqrt{\log(1/p)}]\leq\varepsilon
, one can check that provided
t_{0}
is sufficiently large, and
p_{0}
small enough depending on
t_{0}
, all the assumptions on parameters
M,\varepsilon
, and
\eta
needed to apply Proposition
4.2
are satisfied. Therefore, noting that
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
is increasing if
x\in(0,e^{-1}]
, we deduce from Proposition
4.2
that there exists some
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}>0
, depending only on
k
, such that if
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}/np\leq t^{2}\leq 1/{\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}}
,
then

(4.27)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\big(f_{1}(X)-\mathbb{E}f_{1}(X)>t/3\big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}np}{\gamma_{0}\max\big((\delta/t)\log(\frac{t}{\delta}\big),\varepsilon^{2}\big)}\Big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}np}{\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}\Big),

where in the last step we used that
\varepsilon^{2}\geq(\delta/t)\log(t/\delta)
, which is a consequence of the upper bound on
\varepsilon
given in (
4.4
). Since
np\geq\log(1/p)
, this implies that (
4.27
) continues to hold for all
t
satisfying (
4.4
) with
\gamma_{0}:=\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\vee t_{0}^{2}
. Thus
combining (
4.25
), applied for
s=t/3
, (
4.26
), and (
4.27
), and using a union bound, we deduce that, for any
t
and
\varepsilon
as in (
4.4
),

\mathbb{P}\big(f(X)-\mathbb{E}f(X)>t\big)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}np}{\gamma_{0}^{\prime}}[\varepsilon^{-2}\wedge\log(1/p)]\Big),

where
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}
is a positive constant depending on
k
. Since
\varepsilon\geq t_{0}^{-1}\log(t_{0}^{2})/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}
(use
t_{0}/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}\leq t\leq t_{0}^{-1}
), this ends the proof.
∎

5.
Large deviation upper bound for matrices with bounded entries

The goal of this section is to prove the large deviation upper bound of Theorem
1.4
under the additional assumption that the entries of
G
are bounded. Throughout this section we fix
R>0
such that for any
i,j\in[n]
,
|G_{i,j}|\leq R
.

5.1.
Block decomposition

Let
{\bm{u}}
be an eigenvector of
X
associated to
\lambda_{X}
with unit norm and denote by
k_{\text{top}}
its multiplicity. For any
\varepsilon>0
we want to find a random subset
J
with size at most
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
with overwhelming probability, such that

(5.1)

J\supset\{i\in[n]:|{\bm{u}}_{i}|\geq\varepsilon\}\text{ and }\lambda_{X^{(J)}}<\lambda_{X},

where we remind the reader that
X^{(J)}
is the submatrix of
X
spanned by the rows and columns in
J^{c}
. To construct this set, we will use the following lemma, which says that one can decrease the top eigenvalue of a matrix by removing certain rows and columns. As we could not locate the proof of this result in the literature, we include it below.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
Y
be a real symmetric matrix of size
n
and
k
be the multiplicity of
\lambda_{Y}
. Then, there exists a subset
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
of size
k
such that
\lambda_{Y^{(\mathcal{J})}\lx@nobreakspace}<\lambda_{Y}
.

Proof.

Let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
be a collection of linearly independent eigenvectors associated to
\lambda_{Y}
. It follows that there exists
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
with
|\mathcal{J}|=k
such that the matrix
(w_{j}(i))_{i\in\mathcal{J},j\in[k]}
is invertible, where
w_{j}(i)
denotes the
i
-th entry of
w_{j}
. We claim that
\lambda_{Y^{(\mathcal{J})}}<\lambda_{Y}
. Note that it always holds that
\lambda_{Y^{(\mathcal{J})}}\leq\lambda_{Y}
. Arguing by contradiction, assume that the equality holds. This implies that there exists an eigenvector
w
of
Y
supported on
\mathcal{J}^{c}
.
This follows from the fact if
\widetilde{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
is a unit vector such that
\langle\widetilde{w},Y^{(\mathcal{J})}\widetilde{w}\rangle=\lambda_{Y}
then
\langle\widetilde{w},Y^{(\mathcal{J})}\widetilde{w}\rangle=\langle w,Yw\rangle=\sup_{\|v\|^{2}=1}\langle v,Yv\rangle
,
where
w
is the extension of
\widetilde{w}
to
[n]
by setting to zero its entries in
\mathcal{J}
. This equality implies that
w
, as an optimiser of the quadratic form defined by
Y
on the sphere, should satisfy the critical point equation, and therefore should be an eigenvector of
Y
.
Now, we show that
w,w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
are linearly independent. If
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}
are scalars such that
w=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}w_{j}
, then as
w
is supported on
\mathcal{J}^{c}
, we have in particular for any
i\in\mathcal{J}
,
0=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}w_{j}(i)
.
Since we chose
\mathcal{J}
such that
(w_{j}(i))_{i\in\mathcal{J},j\in[k]}
is invertible, these equalities imply that
\theta_{1}=\cdots=\theta_{k}=0
. As
k
is the multiplicity of
\lambda_{Y}
we reach a contradiction.
∎

Define now
J\subset[n]
as the subset

(5.2)

J=\{i\in[n]:|{\bm{u}}_{i}|\geq\varepsilon\}\cup J^{\prime},

where
J^{\prime}
is the subset obtained from Lemma
5.1
such that
\#J^{\prime}=k_{\text{top}}
. Decompose the top eigenvector and the matrix
X
as

(5.3)

\lx@nobreakspace{\bm{u}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\\
\lx@nobreakspace{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix},\quad X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{J}&\widecheck{X}_{J}^{\sf T}\\
\widecheck{X}_{J}&X^{(J)}\end{pmatrix},

where
{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
,
{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{J^{c}}
. Note that by construction
{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
, and therefore by Chebychev’s inequality

(5.4)

k_{\text{top}}\leq\#J\leq\varepsilon^{-2}+k_{\text{top}}.

Moreover, by Lemma
5.1
,
\lambda_{X^{(J)}}<\lambda_{X}
almost surely.

5.2.
Multiplicity of the top eigenvalue

We will now show that with high probability,
J
is of size only of order
\varepsilon^{-2}
. To prove this, we will rely on the fact that on a large deviation event of the top eigenvalue, its multiplicity can be at most of order
1
with high probability.
More precisely, we will show the following result.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\delta>0
.

(5.5)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(k_{\text{top}}\geq\varepsilon^{-2},\lambda_{X}\geq 2+\delta\big)=-\infty.

As a consequence, for any
\delta>0
, and with
J
as in (
5.2
),

(5.6)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\#J\geq 2\varepsilon^{-2},\lambda_{X}\geq 2+\delta\big)=-\infty.

To prove Lemma
5.2
, we will show that with high probability, there are at most
O(1)
eigenvalues greater than
2
. This fact is a consequence of the following concentration inequality. Denote for any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
by
\mathcal{N}(I)
the number of eigenvalues of
X
in
I
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\delta>0
. There exists
n_{\delta,R}
depending on
\delta
and
R
such that for any
n\geq n_{\delta,R}
and
k\geq 1
,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{N}\big([2+\delta,+\infty)\big)\geq k\Big)\leq e^{-c\frac{k\delta^{2}np}{R^{2}}},

where
c>0
is a numerical constant.

Proof.

Define
f(x):=\frac{1}{\delta}(x-2)_{+}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. As
f(x)\geq 1
for
x\geq 2+\delta
, we have

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{N}\big([2+\delta,+\infty))\geq k\big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(\lambda_{i})\geq k\Big),

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
are the eigenvalues of
X
in a non increasing order. Note that as
f
is non decreasing

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(\lambda_{i})\Big)\leq k\mathbb{E}f(\lambda_{1})\leq k\Big(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\Big((\lambda_{1}-2)_{+}{\bf 1}_{\lambda_{1}\geq 2+\delta/4}\Big)\Big)\leq k\Big(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{4}{\delta^{2}}\mathbb{E}[(\lambda_{1}-2)_{+}^{2}]\Big)\leq k/2,

for all large
n
, where the last inequality follows from the expectation bound
\mathbb{E}\lambda_{1}\leq 2(1+o(1))
(see
[
15
, Theorem 2.7]
), and the variance bound
\mathrm{Var}(\lambda_{1})=o(1)
where the latter is a consequence of (
4.2
), applied for
f(X)=\lambda_{1}(X)
, and an integration by parts.
This allows us to write

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{N}\big([2+\delta,+\infty))\geq k\big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(f(\lambda_{i})-\mathbb{E}f(\lambda_{i})\big)\geq k/2\Big).

Finally using
[
6
, Proposition 3.2]
we get the claim.
∎

The proof of Lemma
5.2
is now immediate.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

For any
\delta>0
, we have
\{\lambda_{X}\geq 2+\delta\}\subset\{\mathcal{N}([2+\delta,+\infty))\geq k_{\text{top}}\}
. Therefore

\mathbb{P}\big(k_{\text{top}}\geq\varepsilon^{-2},\lambda_{X}\geq 2+\delta\big)\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{N}\big([2+\delta,+\infty))\geq\varepsilon^{-2}\big).

Using Lemma
5.3
, we get the first claim (
5.5
). The second claim follows from (
5.4
).
∎

5.3.
Large deviations of the maximal degree

In this section we show that for any random subset
\bar{J}\subset[n]
of bounded size, the large deviation of the operator norm of
\widecheck{X}_{\bar{J}}
is dominated by the deviation of the maximal degree among the vertices in
\bar{J}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\bar{J}\subset[n]
be a random subset of bounded size.
For any
t>0
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{X}_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}\geq\max_{i\in\bar{J}}\|X_{i}\|^{2}+t\big)=-\infty.

Assume for the moment that Lemma
5.4
holds. It tells us in particular that the large deviation upper tail of
\|\widecheck{X}_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}
can be read off from the one of the maximal degree. Now, since
np\gg\log n
, the large deviations of the maximal degree are given by the ones of the degree of an individual vertex, which has for rate function
h_{L}
according to Lemma
3.4
.
Thus, Lemmas
5.4
and
3.4
together with the lower semicontinuity of
h_{L}
gives the following large deviation result.

Proposition 5.5
.

For any
\bar{J}\subset[n]
a random subset of bounded size and
t>1
,

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{X}_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}\geq t\big)\leq-h_{L}(t).

It remains to prove Lemma
5.4
. To this end, we first show that for a given vector
v
with bounded support,
\|Xv\|^{2}
is dominated by the mean of the degrees of the vertices in the support of
v
weighted by the square of the entries of
v
. This amounts to say that in the underlying random graph
\Xi
, for any finite set of vertices, their neighbourhoods are essentially disjoint at the exponential scale
np
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
be a subset of bounded size. For any
v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
t>0
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|Xv\|^{2}\geq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}\|X_{i}\|^{2}v_{i}^{2}+t\big)=-\infty.

Proof.

Observe

\displaystyle\|Xv\|^{2}=\big\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}v_{i}\lx@nobreakspace X_{i}\big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}v_{i}^{2}\|X_{i}\|^{2}+\sum_{i\neq j\in\mathcal{J}}v_{i}v_{j}\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}v_{i}^{2}\|X_{i}\|^{2}+\#\mathcal{I}\max_{i\neq j}|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|.

Since
\#\mathcal{J}
is bounded, it suffices to show that for any
t>0
,

(5.7)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\max_{i\neq j\in[n]}|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|>t\big)=-\infty.

Let
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
. As
|G_{k,\ell}|\leq R
for any
k,\ell
, we have
|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|\leq R^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{i,k}\xi_{j,k}/np
. As
(\xi_{i,k}\xi_{j,k})_{k\notin\{i,j\}}
are i.i.d. Bernoulli of parameter
p^{2}
, we obtain by Chernoff’s inequality and Lemma
4.5
(iii), that for any
t>0
and for
n
large enough,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{k\notin\{i,j\}}\xi_{i,k}\xi_{j,k}>tnp\big)\leq e^{-ctnp\log(t/p)},

where
c
is a positive numerical constant.
Using a union bound and the fact that
np\gg\log n
, this yields the claim (
5.7
).
∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
5.4
.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
such that
\#\bar{J}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}
almost surely.
Since
np\gg\log n
and

\lx@nobreakspace\log\#\big\{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]:\#\mathcal{J}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\big\}=O(\log n),

it suffices to show the statement for a deterministic subset
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
such that
\#\mathcal{J}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}
. By Lemma
5.6
, we know that for any
v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
t>0
,

(5.8)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|^{2}\geq\max_{i\in\mathcal{J}}\|X_{i}\|^{2}+t\big)=-\infty.

It remains to perform a net argument. Let
\eta\in(0,1)
and
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
a
\eta
-net for the
\ell^{2}
norm of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
. By
[
3
, Corollary 4.1.15]
, there exists such a net of size at most
(3/\eta)^{1/\varepsilon}
. Besides by
[
49
, Lemma 4.4.1]
,

\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\sup_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|.

Using (
5.8
), this gives for any
t>0
and
\eta\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{I}}\|^{2}\geq(1-\eta)^{-2}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\|X_{i}\|^{2}+t)\big)=-\infty.

Using the exponential tightness of the maximum degree guaranteed by Lemma
3.4
and a union bound, this ends the proof.
∎

5.4.
Localisation of the top eigenvector

We show that on the upper tail large deviation event of the top eigenvalue, the top eigenvector must localise in the sense that the
\ell^{2}
weight of the entries of order at least
\varepsilon
is lower bounded by a constant. More precisely, we prove that
{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}
defined in (
5.3
) has a non-trivial
\ell^{2}
norm at the large deviation scale.

Lemma 5.7
.

For any
\lambda>2
, there exists
\eta_{\lambda}>0
increasing in
\lambda
such that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\eta_{\lambda},\ \lambda_{X}>\lambda\big)<-I(\lambda).

To prove Lemma
5.7
we first show that a large value of the inner product
\langle v,Xw\rangle
cannot be achieved by using delocalised unit vectors. This result uses in a
crucial way
the fact that the entries are bounded. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
from (
4.1
).

Lemma 5.8
.

For any
t>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{v,w\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}}\lx@nobreakspace\langle v,Xw\rangle>2+t\Big)=-\infty.

Proof.

Define for any
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
,
f(K):=\sup_{v,w\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}}\langle v,Kw\rangle
.
Clearly
f
is a convex function. Note that for any
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
,
\langle v,Kw\rangle=\langle K,V\rangle
where
V=\frac{1}{2}(vw^{\sf T}+wv^{\sf T})
. Since
4V=(v+w)(v+w)^{\sf T}-(v-w)(v-w)^{\sf T}
, we deduce that
V
belongs to the set of flat matrices of rank at most
2
,
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
defined in (
4.1
). Since
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
is compact, the supremum defining
f(K)
is achieved for any
K
, and it follows that
\partial f(K)\cap\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\neq\emptyset
. It is then not difficult to show that there exists a measurable choice of sub differential that belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
. That is, there exists
\zeta:\mathcal{H}_{n}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{n}
measurable such that
\zeta(K)\in\partial f(K)\cap\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
for any
K\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
(see Lemma
B.1
and its proof). As a consequence, for all
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
,

\lx@nobreakspace f(K)-f(K^{\prime})\leq\langle\zeta(K),K-K^{\prime}\rangle.

It follows from Lemma
4.1
that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(f(X)-\mathbb{E}f(X)>t\big)=-\infty.

Besides, as
np\gg\log n
, we know from
[
15
, Theorem 2.7]
that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{v,w\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}}\lx@nobreakspace\langle v,Xw\rangle\leq\mathbb{E}\|X\|=2(1+o(1))
.
This ends the proof of the claim.
∎

We are now ready to give a proof of Lemma
5.7
.

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

Expanding
\lambda_{X}=\langle({\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}+{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}),X({\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}+{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon})\rangle
, on the event
\{\#J\leq 2\varepsilon^{-2}\}
, we get

\lambda_{X}=2\langle\widecheck{X}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+\langle{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon},X^{(J)}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},\widecheck{X}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle\leq 2\|\widecheck{X}_{J}\|.\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|+\sup_{w\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}}\langle w,Xw\rangle+o(1),

where the last inequality follows from the fact that the entries of
G
are bounded. Thus, for any
t,r>0
, and all large
n
,

\{\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq t/(4r),\ \lambda_{X}>2+t,\ \#J\leq 2\varepsilon^{-2}\}\subset\{\|\widecheck{X}_{J}\|>r\}\cup\{\sup_{w\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}}\langle w,Xw\rangle\geq 2+t/4\}.

By Lemma
5.5
, it follows that

(5.9)

\lx@nobreakspace\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{X}_{J}\|>2+t\big)\leq-h_{L}((2+t)^{2})<-h_{L}\Big(\frac{2+t}{m(2+t)}\Big)=I(2+t),

where we used that for any
\lambda>2
,
\lambda m(\lambda)>1
and that
h_{L}
is increasing on
[1,+\infty)
.
Thus, taking
r=t+2
, we get using Lemmas
5.8
and
5.2
, and (
5.9
) that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\eta_{t},\lambda_{X}>2+t\big)<-I(2+t),

where
\eta_{t}=t/(4(t+2))
for any
t>0
. Finally, one can check that
\eta_{t}
is increasing in
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
∎

5.5.
Concentration of the resolvent

The goal of this section is to prove the following concentration result of the resolvent.

Proposition 5.9
.

For any
\lambda>2
,
t>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\Big(\langle\widecheck{X}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},\lx@nobreakspace(\lambda_{X}-X^{(J)})^{-1}\widecheck{X}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle-m(\lambda_{X})\|\widecheck{X}_{J}\|^{2}\cdot\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}>t,\lambda_{X}\geq\lambda\Big)<-I(\lambda).

To prove Proposition
5.9
, we will take advantage of the fact that for a deterministic symmetric matrix
Y
, if there exists
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
such that
\lambda_{Y^{(\mathcal{J})}}<\lambda_{Y}
, then the top eigenvalue of
Y
is the solution of an equation involving an optimization problem on
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
described in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
Y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
such that
\lambda_{Y^{(\mathcal{J})}}<\lambda_{Y}
. Then,

(5.10)

\lambda_{Y}=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}(\langle\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}v,(\lambda_{Y}-Y^{(\mathcal{J})})^{-1}\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle+\langle v,Y_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle\big).

Moreover, the supremum is achieved for any vector
v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
which can be extended to a top eigenvector of
Y
.

Proof.

Using the same computation as in (
2.3
) one can check that if
v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
admits an extension as a top eigenvector of
Y
, then

\lambda_{Y}=\langle\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}v,(\lambda_{Y}-Y^{(\mathcal{J})})^{-1}\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle+\langle v,Y_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle.

As
\lambda_{Y^{(\mathcal{J})}}<\lambda_{Y}
, any top eigenvector of
Y
has a non-zero projection on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
. Therefore, there exists some vector
v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
which can be extended to a top eigenvector, and consequently the supremum in (
5.10
) is at least as large as
\lambda_{Y}
. It remains to prove the other direction.

By a compactness argument, the supremum is achieved and let
v_{*}
be a maximiser. It should satisfy the critical point equation which reads
\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\sf T}(\lambda_{Y}-Y^{(\mathcal{J})})^{-1}\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}v_{*}+Y_{\mathcal{J}}v_{*}=\theta v_{*}
for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. Denoting
w_{*}=(\lambda_{Y}-Y^{(\mathcal{J})})^{-1}\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}v_{*}
, we get the equations

\lx@nobreakspace\begin{cases}Y_{\mathcal{J}}v_{*}+\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\sf T}w_{*}=\theta v_{*}\\
\widecheck{Y}_{\mathcal{J}}v_{*}+Y^{(\mathcal{J})}w_{*}=\lambda_{Y}w_{*}.\end{cases}

If
u_{*}^{\sf T}=(v_{*}^{\sf T},w_{*}^{\sf T})
, then we find
\langle u_{*},Yu_{*}\rangle=\theta+\lambda_{Y}\|w_{*}\|^{2}
. The inequality
\langle u_{*},Yu_{*}\rangle\leq\lambda_{Y}\|u_{*}\|^{2}
, as
\|v_{*}\|^{2}=1
, yields
\theta\leq\lambda_{Y}
. Since
\theta
is the value of the supremum, this ends the proof.
∎

To prove Proposition
5.9
we need to set up a few more notation.
Fix
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
a subset of bounded size,
\lambda>2
, and a realisation of
\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}
and
X_{\mathcal{J}}
. For
K\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}^{\lambda}
(recall the relevant notation from Section
1.3
) we set

\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K):=\langle\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v,(\lambda-K)^{-1}\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle+\langle v,X_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle,\lx@nobreakspace\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}},\text{ and }\varphi_{\lambda}(K):=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}\lx@nobreakspace\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K).

Observe that since
\lambda_{X^{(J)}}<\lambda_{X}
by definition of
J
, Lemma
5.10
yields that on the event where
J=\mathcal{J}
,
\varphi_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})=\varphi_{\lambda_{X},\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})
, where
\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}:={\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}/\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|
. Therefore, to prove Proposition
5.9
, it suffices to show that the upper tail of
\varphi_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})
concentrates uniformly in
\lambda
and
\mathcal{J}
around its expectation.
By
[
17
, Exercise I.I.15]
, we know that for any given
w\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
,
K\mapsto\langle w,(\lambda-K)^{-1}w\rangle
is convex on
\mathcal{H}^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
. In order to show that
\varphi_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})
concentrates with a large deviation speed much larger than
np
, we need to extend it suitably on the whole space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
. To this end,
define for any
r,\varepsilon>0
the set
E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}
of matrices
K
such that there exists a “flat” subdifferential of
\varphi_{\lambda}
at
K
, that is

\lx@nobreakspace E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}:=\big\{K\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}^{\lambda}:\exists v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}},\varphi_{\lambda}(K)=\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K),\nabla\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K)\in r\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{1}\big\},

where
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
4.1
).
For any
K\in E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}
, we set

\Theta(K):=\{\nabla\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K)\in r\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{1}:v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}\text{ and }\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K)=\varphi_{\lambda}(K)\}.

Now extend
{\varphi_{\lambda}}_{|E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
by setting

(5.11)

\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(K^{\prime}):=\sup_{K\in E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}}\sup_{\zeta\in\Theta(K)}\big\{\varphi_{\lambda}(K)+\langle\zeta,K^{\prime}-K\rangle\big\},\,\forall K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}.

Note that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}
extends
{\varphi_{\lambda}}_{|E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}}
in such a way that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}
is convex and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}\leq{\varphi}_{\lambda}
pointwise on the subset
\mathcal{H}^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
.

To prove Proposition
5.9
, in a first step, we upper bound the expectation of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})
given
\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}
and
X_{\mathcal{J}}
. We need to define the following event. Fix
C\geq 1
and
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
, and set

(5.12)

\Omega_{C}:=\big\{\#\{(i,j)\in[n]\times\mathcal{J}:\xi_{i,j}=1\}\leq Cnp\text{ and }\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}\leq C\big\}.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
C\geq 1
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, and
\lambda>2+2\delta
. Denote
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
to be the expectation with respect to
X^{(\mathcal{J})}
. Then

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\big[\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\big]\leq m(\lambda)\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+o(1),\qquad\text{a.s.\penalty\ on }\Omega_{C}.

The proof of Proposition
5.11
is postponed to Section
7
. It uses an isotropic local law, concentration bounds, and a net argument. We also need the following exponential tightness result.

Lemma 5.12
.

Under the same setup of Theorem
1.4

\lim_{M\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\|X\|>M)=-\infty.

Lemma
5.12
was essentially proved in
[
10
]
. See
[
10
, Theorem 1.7]
.
It was not stated in the form that we need. Nevertheless, one can check that the same proof yields Lemma
5.12
. So we do not repeat it here. Let us point to the reader that Lemma
5.12
does not need the entries of
G
to be uniformly bounded, and therefore it will also be used later in Section
6
when we consider the unbounded case. We are now ready to give a proof of Proposition
5.9
.

Proof of Proposition
5.9
.

By definition (see (
5.11
))
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}
is convex on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
and coincides with
\varphi_{\lambda}
on
E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}
.
Since
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
is compact and
\Theta(K)\subset r\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
for any
K\in E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}
, we deduce from (
5.11
) that
r\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\cap\partial\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(K^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
for any
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
.
Therefore there exists
\zeta:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
a measurable selection for the multivalued mapping
\partial\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)\cap r\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
, that is,
\zeta(K)\in\partial\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(K)\cap r\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
for any
K\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
(see Lemma
B.1
). Thus,

\lx@nobreakspace\widetilde{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(K)-\widetilde{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(K^{\prime})\leq\langle\zeta(K),K-K^{\prime}\rangle,\qquad\forall K,K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}},\

Applying Proposition
4.1
to
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}/r
, we get that for any sufficiently small
t>0
and
n
large enough

(5.13)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{J}}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>t\big)\leq 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}np}{\gamma_{0}(r\varepsilon)^{2}}},

where
\gamma_{0}>0
is a numerical constant. Using Proposition
5.11
, we obtain for any
C,t>0
,

(5.14)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\Omega_{C}\cap\big\{\widetilde{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>m(\lambda)\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+t\big\}\big)=-\infty,

Next we claim that

(5.15)

\lim_{C\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{C}^{c})=-\infty.

Indeed, by Lemma
5.12
,
\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|
is exponentially tight. Moreover, using Binomial tail estimates and the fact that
\mathcal{J}
is a set of bounded size it yields,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{C\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\#\{(i,j)\in[n]\times\mathcal{J}:\xi_{i,j}=1\}>Cnp\big)=-\infty.

Thus the claim (
5.15
) follows. Combining (
5.15
) and (
5.14
), we get for any
t>0
and
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
of bounded size,

(5.16)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>m(\lambda)\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+t\big)=-\infty.

It remains now to perform a union bound on
\mathcal{J}
and a net argument on
\lambda
.
We first perform a net argument over
\lambda
. For
\delta>0

we show that

(5.17)

\Gamma_{\delta,r}^{\lambda,\mathcal{J}}:=\{\lambda\leq\lambda_{X}\leq\lambda+\delta,\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}>1/r,J=\mathcal{J}\}\subset\{\varphi_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\leq\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda+\delta}^{\varepsilon}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})+\delta r\}.

On the event
J=\mathcal{J}
, we have on the one hand by Lemma
5.10
that
\varphi_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})=\varphi_{\lambda_{X},\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})
, where
\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}={\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}/\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|
, and on the other hand by (
2.3
),

\nabla\varphi_{\lambda_{X},\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})=\frac{1}{\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}{{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}}^{\sf T}.

Therefore, on the event
\Gamma_{\delta,r}^{\lambda,\mathcal{J}}
,
X^{(\mathcal{J})}\in E_{\lambda_{X}}^{\varepsilon,r}
. Observe that this is equivalent to say that on the event
\Gamma_{\delta,r}^{\lambda,\mathcal{J}}
,
K=X^{(\mathcal{J})}+(\lambda+\delta)-\lambda_{X}\in E_{\lambda+\delta}^{\varepsilon,r}
. Moreover, on this event
we have
\nabla\varphi_{\lambda_{X},\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})=\nabla\varphi_{\lambda+\delta,\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}}(K)\in\Theta(K)
and
\varphi_{\lambda+\delta}(K)=\varphi_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})
. Hence, on
\Gamma_{\delta,r}^{\lambda,\mathcal{J}}
we have

\lx@nobreakspace\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda+\delta}^{\varepsilon}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\geq\varphi_{\lambda+\delta}(K)+\langle X^{(\mathcal{J})}-K,\nabla\varphi_{\lambda+\delta,\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}}(K)\rangle=\varphi_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{({\mathcal{J}})})+\langle X^{(\mathcal{J})}-K,\nabla\varphi_{\lambda_{X},\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\varepsilon}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\rangle.

Using that on
\Gamma_{\delta,r}^{\lambda,\mathcal{J}}
,
\lambda_{X}\geq\lambda
, this yields

\lx@nobreakspace\widetilde{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda+\delta}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\geq\varphi_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})-(\lambda+\delta-\lambda_{X})\|{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}/\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\geq\varphi_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})-\delta r.

This yields (
5.17
). Now, if we take
r=1/\eta_{\lambda}^{2}
where
\eta_{\lambda}
is as in Lemma
5.7
, then we get using (
5.16
) and the fact that
m
is non increasing, that for any
t>0
and
0<\delta\leq t\eta_{\lambda}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big({\varphi}_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>m(\lambda_{X})\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+2t,\lambda\leq\lambda_{X}\leq\lambda+\delta,J=\mathcal{J}\big)

\displaystyle<-I(\lambda).

As the above estimate is true for any
t>0
,
\lambda>2
and
\eta_{\lambda}
is increasing in
\lambda
, we deduce using a net argument and the exponential tightness of
\lambda_{X}
given by Lemma
5.12
, that for any
\lambda>2
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big({\varphi}_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>m(\lambda_{X})\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+t,\lambda_{X}\geq\lambda,J=\mathcal{J}\big)<-I(\lambda).

Finally, as there are at most
e^{O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)}
subsets
\mathcal{J}
such that
|\mathcal{J}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-2}
and for any such subset
\varphi_{\lambda_{X}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\leq\langle\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},\lx@nobreakspace(\lambda_{X}-X^{(\mathcal{J})})^{-1}\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+2R\varepsilon^{-2}/\sqrt{np}
, we conclude the proof by using a union bound, Lemma
5.2
and the fact that
np\gg\log n
.
∎

5.6.
Proofs of the large deviation upper bound in the bounded case and of Theorem
1.7

We begin with the proof of Theorem
1.7
. By (
2.3
), we have

\lx@nobreakspace\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},X_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+\langle\widecheck{X}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},\lx@nobreakspace(\lambda_{X}-X^{(J)})^{-1}\widecheck{X}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle=\lambda_{X}\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}.

Using Proposition
5.9
and the fact that
\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},X_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle=o(1)
with overwhelming probability by Lemma
5.2
, we get that for any
t>0
, and
\lambda>2
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\lambda_{X}\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}>m(\lambda_{X})\|\widecheck{X}_{J}\|^{2}\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}+t,\lambda_{X}\geq\lambda\big)<-I(\lambda).

Using that
\lambda_{X}
is exponentially tight,
\lambda\mapsto\lambda/m(\lambda)
is increasing and Lemma
5.7
, we get for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\lambda/m(\lambda)>\|\widecheck{X}_{J}\|^{2}+t,\lambda_{X}\geq\lambda\big)<-I(\lambda).

Combining the above estimate with Lemma
5.4
, this ends the proof of the first claim. The second claim is an immediately consequence of Lemma
5.7
, and as we know that the large deviation lower bound holds with rate function
I
, the third claim follows.

We finally give the proof of large deviation upper bound in the bounded case. Let
\lambda>2
. Theorem
1.7
shows that for any
t>0
, with overwhelming probability, on the large deviation event
\{\lambda_{X}\geq\lambda\}
, there exists a vertex with degree at least
\lambda/m(\lambda)-t
. Since the large deviation of the maximal degree has rate function
h_{L}
by Lemma
3.4
, this ends the proof.
∎

6.
Large deviation upper bound for the general case

In this section we prove that the upper tail large deviation event of the top eigenvalue in the unbounded case is dominated by the emergence of either a clique of vertices with large edge weights or by one vertex having a large vertex weight and a high degree.

6.1.
Truncation and decoupling

We will build upon the large deviation principle for the top eigenvalue in the bounded case. We start by defining an appropriate decomposition of
X
into
A+B
, where
A
is a sparse Wigner matrix with bounded entries, and then show that we can replace
A
by a sparse Wigner matrix with bounded entries
independent
of
B
. In order to carry out these arguments, we first perform a reduction of Theorem
1.4
in the case where the distributions of the entries of
G
satisfy the following additional assumption.

Assumption 6.1

(Density and extended support)
.

The laws of the entries of
G
have densities with respect to the Lebesgue measure and have extended supports, where a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
is said to have an extended support if
\inf\mathrm{supp}(\mu)=-\infty
and
\sup\mathrm{supp}(\mu)=+\infty
.

Lemma 6.2
.

If the large deviation upper bound of Theorem
1.4
holds for any sparse Wigner matrix satisfying Assumptions
1.2
and
6.1
then it continues to hold for any sparse Wigner matrices satisfying only Assumption
1.2
.

Proof.

Let
X,G
, and
\Xi
be as in Theorem
1.4
.
Let
\Gamma
be a symmetric random matrix, independent of
G
and
\Xi
, with i.i.d. random variables variables on and above the diagonal such that the common law of those random variables has a symmetric density (around zero) with respect to the Lebesgue measure, with tails lighter than a Gaussian and of unit variance, and has an extended support.
Define for any
\varepsilon>0
,
G^{\varepsilon}:=G+\varepsilon\Gamma
, and
X^{\varepsilon}:=G^{\varepsilon}\circ\Xi/\sqrt{np}
. Clearly
G^{\varepsilon}
satisfy Assumptions
1.2
and
6.1

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Note that
\mathrm{Var}(G^{\varepsilon}_{1,2})=1+\varepsilon^{2}
and that the entries of
\Gamma
having a tail lighter than the Gaussian means that (
1.2
) continues to hold for
(G^{\varepsilon})_{i,j}
with the same
\alpha
and
\beta
, for any
\varepsilon>0
. Hence applying Theorem
1.4
to
X^{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}}
gives for any
t>2\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}}

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\lambda_{X^{\varepsilon}}>t\big)\leq-I_{\varepsilon}(t),

where

\lx@nobreakspace I_{\varepsilon}(t):=\min\Big\{\widehat{I}_{\varepsilon}(t_{\varepsilon}),\frac{1}{4\beta_{\varepsilon}m(t_{\varepsilon})^{2}}\Big\},\ \widehat{I}_{\varepsilon}(t_{\varepsilon}):=\inf\big\{\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha_{\varepsilon}}+h_{L_{\varepsilon}}(s):r+m(t_{\varepsilon})s=t_{\varepsilon},r\geq 0,s\geq 1\big\},

with
t_{\varepsilon}:=t/\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}}
,
\alpha_{\varepsilon}:=\alpha/(1+\varepsilon^{2})
,
\beta_{\varepsilon}:=\beta/(1+\varepsilon^{2})
, and
L_{\varepsilon}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}[\exp(\theta(G^{\varepsilon}_{1,2})^{2}/(1+\varepsilon^{2}))]
.

Upon observing that
(G^{\varepsilon})_{1,2}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon^{2})(G_{1,2}^{2}+\Gamma_{1,2}^{2})
and using the fact that the lighter than a Gaussian tail assumption of
\Gamma_{1,2}
implies that
\mathbb{E}[\exp(\theta\Gamma_{1,2}^{2})]<\infty
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, we derive that
\mathcal{D}_{L}\subset\mathcal{D}_{L_{\varepsilon}}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. It also follows by the dominated convergence theorem that
L_{\varepsilon}
converges to
L
pointwise on
\mathcal{D}_{L}
, as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. As
L_{\varepsilon}
is increasing on
\mathbb{R}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, Dini’s theorem further implies that
L_{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly to
L
on compact subsets of
\mathcal{D}_{L}
. Therefore

(6.1)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}h_{L_{\varepsilon}}(x)\geq\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{D}_{L}}\{\theta x-L_{\varepsilon}(\theta)+1\}=h_{L}(x).

On the other hand, by Jensen’s inequality and the fact that
\Gamma_{1,2}
is centered, we have
L_{\varepsilon}(\theta)\geq L(\theta/(1+\varepsilon^{2}))
for any
\theta\geq 0
which in turn gives

(6.2)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}h_{L_{\varepsilon}}(x)\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}h_{L}(x(1+\varepsilon^{2}))=h_{L}(x),\quad\text{ for }x\geq 1,

where the last step also uses that
h_{L}
is continuous. The pointwise convergence of
h_{L_{\varepsilon}}
to
h_{L}
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, given by (
6.1
)-(
6.2
), together with the fact that
h_{L_{\varepsilon}}
is non decreasing on
[1,+\infty)
(recall Lemma
3.5
(e)), imply that the convergence is uniform on compact subsets of
[1,+\infty)
. One can check that this entails that
I_{\varepsilon}(t)\to I(t)
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, for any
t>2
. Besides
|\lambda_{X^{\varepsilon}}-\lambda_{X}|\leq\varepsilon\|\Gamma\circ\Xi/\sqrt{np}\|
, so that by Lemma
5.12
, it follows that
(\lambda_{X^{\varepsilon}})_{\varepsilon>0}
, is an exponentially good approximation of
\lambda_{X}
(see
[
30
, Definition 4.2.14]
). Since
I
is lower semicontinuous, we deduce from
[
30
, Theorem 4.2.16]
that the large deviation upper bound of Theorem
1.4
holds for
X
.
∎

Equipped with Lemma
6.2
from now on we assume that the entries of
G
satisfy Assumptions
1.2
and
6.1
. With these additional assumptions, we define a truncation of the entries of
G
in a way that preserve their zero mean property. This will be crucial in our decoupling argument in order to guarantee the resulting decoupled sparse matrices to be independent. To achieve this, we rely on the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mu
be a centered probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
that is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and with an extended support. There exist continuous functions
a,b:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\int_{-a(R)}^{b(R)}xd\mu(x)=0,\ \forall R>0,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad a(R),b(R)\to+\infty\text{ as }R\to+\infty.

Proof.

Let
a>0
. Define
F_{a}:b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\int_{-a}^{b}xd\mu(x)
. Note that
F_{a}
is continuous since
\mu
is absolutely continuous. Further
F_{a}(0)<0
,
\lim_{b\to+\infty}F_{a}(b)>0
by the assumptions that
\mu
is centered and has an extended support. By the mean value theorem, there exists
b=b(a)>0
such that
F_{a}(b)=0
. Moreover, one can check that
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto b(a)
is continuous and using the fact that
\mu
is centered and has an extended support,
b(a)\to+\infty
as
a\to+\infty
.
∎

Let
\mu_{i,j}
denote the law of
G_{i,j}
for
i,j\in[n]
,
a_{i,j}
,
b_{i,j}
be the functions given by Lemma
6.3
for
\mu_{i,j}
, and
I_{i,j}(R):=[-a_{i,j}(R),b_{i,j}(R)]
. For any
R>0
, define
\widetilde{A}^{R}
to be the matrix with its entries given by

(6.3)

\widetilde{A}_{i,j}^{R}:=G_{i,j}{\bf 1}_{G_{i,j}\in I_{i,j}(R)},\lx@nobreakspace\forall i,j\in[n],

and
A^{R}:=\widetilde{A}^{R}\circ\Xi/\sqrt{np}
. Further set
\widetilde{B}:=G-\widetilde{A}^{R}
and
B^{R}:=\widetilde{B}^{R}\circ\Xi/\sqrt{np}
. To ease the notation, when there is no risk of confusion, we will drop the
R
-dependence from these notation and this convention will be adopted for most of the results and in their proofs that follow.

Next we argue that we can replace
A^{R}
by a symmetric matrix
H^{R}
independent of
B^{R}
, such that
H^{R}:=(\widetilde{H}^{R}\circ\Xi^{\prime})/\sqrt{np}
, where
\widetilde{H}^{R}
and
\Xi^{\prime}
are independent symmetric matrices,
\Xi^{\prime}:=(\xi^{\prime}_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]}
has same law as
\Xi
, the variables
(\widetilde{H}^{R}_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
are independent and
\widetilde{H}^{R}_{i,j}
is distributed according to the law of
G_{i,j}
conditioned on
G_{i,j}\in I_{i,j}(R)
.
Let
E:=\{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}:G_{i,j}\in I_{i,j}(R)]\}
and define
\widehat{A}^{R}
to be the matrix with entries

(6.4)

\lx@nobreakspace\widehat{A}^{R}_{i,j}:={\bf 1}_{(i,j)\in E}A^{R}_{i,j}+{\bf 1}_{(i,j)\notin E}H^{R}_{i,j},\quad\forall i,j\in[n].

One can check that
\widehat{A}
is independent of
B^{R}
and that it has the same law as
H^{R}
. Here we take the advantage of our special truncation of the entries.
Observe that we could have done the same construction with a simpler truncation, e.g. choosing
I_{i,j}
to be symmetric, and then recenter the truncated entries, but for such a truncation, unless the distribution of
G
is symmetric around zero, the independence does not hold
.
With the above notation we have the following result.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
X^{R}=\widehat{A}^{R}+B^{R}
, where
\widehat{A}
is defined in (
6.4
).
For any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|X^{R}-X\|>t\big)=-\infty.

Proof.

We can write
\widehat{A}^{R}-A^{R}=(\widetilde{H}\circ\Xi^{\prime\prime})/\sqrt{np}
, where
\Xi^{\prime\prime}=(\xi^{\prime\prime}_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]}
such that
\xi^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}=\xi^{\prime}_{i,j}{\bf 1}_{G_{i,j}\notin I_{i,j}(R)}
. Note that
\widetilde{H}^{R}
and
\Xi^{\prime\prime}
are independent. Since
\xi_{i,j}^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\dBer(q)

with
q=o_{R}(1)p
, the exponential tightness result of Lemma
5.12
yields that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widetilde{H}^{R}\circ\Xi^{\prime\prime}\|>t\sqrt{np}\big)=-\infty,

which ends the proof of the claim.
∎

Proposition
6.4
allows us to focus on proving a large deviation upper bound for
\lambda_{Z^{R}}
where
Z^{R}=H^{R}+B^{R}
, for which one can take advantage of the independence between
H^{R}
and
B^{R}
. Indeed,
Z^{R}
has the same law as
X^{R}
by construction and by Weyl’s inequality,
|\lambda_{X^{R}}-\lambda_{X}|\leq\|X^{R}-X\|
. Thus Proposition
6.4
yields that
(\lambda_{Z^{R}})_{R>0}
is an exponentially good approximation of
\lambda_{X}
, and by
[
30
, Theorem 4.2.16]
we can retrieve the large deviation upper bound rate function of
\lambda_{X}
as the limit of the ones for
\lambda_{Z^{R}}
as
R\to+\infty
. Since
\|Z^{R}\|\leq\|H^{R}\|+\|B^{R}\|
, using Lemma
5.12
we note that
\|Z^{R}\|
is exponentially tight for any
R>0
. Moreover, by the exponential tightness of
\|X\|
and Proposition
6.4
, we deduce that
\|Z^{R}\|
,
R\to+\infty
are uniformly exponentially tight. More precisely we have the following result.

Lemma 6.5

(Exponential tightness)
.

For any
R>0
,

(6.5)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{t\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty\lx@nobreakspace}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\|Z^{R}\|>t)=-\infty.

Further,

(6.6)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{t\to+\infty}\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty\lx@nobreakspace}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\|Z^{R}\|>t)=-\infty.

6.2.
Block matrix decomposition

Let
{\bm{u}}
be a measurable choice of a unit eigenvector of
Z^{R}
associated to the top eigenvalue
\lambda_{Z^{R}}
.
Similarly as in the bounded case, we will decompose the matrix
Z^{R}
in blocks according to a certain set
J
of vertices where the top eigenvector
{\bm{u}}
localises. Unlike the bounded case, we choose a different localisation threshold since we are expecting to see a new large deviation scenario: the emergence of a clique of sub entropic size. We incorporate as well for technical reasons to the set
J
vertices with large degree with respect to the network
B^{R}
. More precisely, define for any
\varepsilon>0
,

(6.7)

\lx@nobreakspace J:=\big\{j\in[n]:\|B^{R}_{j}\|\geq\delta\big\}\cup\big\{i\in[n]:|{\bm{u}}_{i}|\geq\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/p)}{np}}\big\},

where
B^{R}_{j}
denotes
the
j^{\text{th}}
-column of
B^{R}
, and
\delta
is chosen to be any function of
\varepsilon
such that
\delta=o(\varepsilon)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Observe that by Chebychev’s inequality we have

(6.8)

\lx@nobreakspace\#\big\{i\in[n]:|{\bm{u}}_{i}|\geq\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/p)}{np}}\big\}\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}np}{\log(1/p)}.

As we will see, adding the indices
j
for which
\|B^{R}_{j}\|
is atypically large only increases the size of
J
by an additive constant with overwhelming probability. This yields the following lemma.

Lemma 6.6
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
R
large enough,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\#J>2\varepsilon^{2}np/\log(1/p)\big)=-\infty.

A consequence of the above result is that the set
J
carries
no entropy
at the large deviation scale
np
, meaning that
\log
of the number of possible realisations of
J
is bounded by

(6.9)

\log\#\big\{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]:\#\mathcal{J}\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}np/\log(1/p)\big\}=O(\varepsilon^{2}np).

The above estimate follows readily from the fact that there are at most
(en/m)^{m}
subsets of
[n]
with size
m
. This estimate is the motivation behind our choice of the localisation threshold
\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{\log(1/p)/np}
. In particular, (
6.9
) will allow us to derive estimates with
J
replaced by a fixed deterministic subset
\mathcal{J}
and then take union bounds over possible realisations of
J
.

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Since
np\gg\log(1/p)
, by (
6.8
) it suffices to prove that for any
\delta>0
and
R
large enough,

\lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{m\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\#\big\{j\in[n]:\|B_{j}\|\geq\delta\big\}\geq m\big)=-\infty.

As the number of subsets of
[n]
of size
m
is at most
e^{O(m\log n)}
and
np\gg\log n
,
it is sufficient to prove that for any
\delta>0
, and
R
large enough,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{m\to+\infty}\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\forall j\in[m],\ \|B_{j}\|\geq\delta\big)=-\infty.

Using a similar argument as in (
3.1
), we deduce that for
\theta
small enough, independent of
(i,j)
,
\log\mathbb{E}(e^{2\theta\widetilde{B}_{i,j}^{2}\xi_{i,j}})=o_{R}(1)p
.
With this choice of
\theta
, Chernoff’s inequality and (
3.1
) give

(6.10)

\lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace\log\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|B_{j}\|^{2}>\delta^{2}m\big)\leq-(\theta\delta^{2}-o_{R}(1))mnp,

which ends the proof of the claim.
∎

With our choice of subset
J
defined in (
6.7
), we split the top eigenvector
{\bm{u}}
into two parts
{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
and
{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\bm{J}}^{c}}
such that
{\bm{u}}^{\sf T}=({{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}}^{\sf T},{{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}}^{\sf T})
. This decomposition of the space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
entails the following block decomposition of
Z^{R}
:

(6.11)

Z^{R}=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{J}&\widecheck{Z}_{J}^{\sf T}\\
\widecheck{Z}_{J}&Z^{(J)}\end{pmatrix},

where we refer the reader yet again to Section
1.3
for the notational conventions.

6.3.
Localisation of the top eigenvector

We now move on to prove that on the upper tail large deviation event of the top eigenvalue, any top eigenvector of
Z^{R}
must localise in the sense that the
\ell^{2}
-weight of of the sub vector consisting of entries that are much greater than
\sqrt{\log(1/p)/np}
is bounded from below by a constant depending on the deviation of the top eigenvalue.

Lemma 6.7
.

For any
\lambda>2
,

\lim_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\eta,\lambda_{Z^{R}}\geq\lambda)=-\infty.

Similarly as in the bounded case, this localisation result builds on the fact that the supremum of
\langle w,Z^{R}w\rangle
when
w
is a delocalised unit vector, meaning that
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{\log(1/p)/np}
, cannot exceed
2
with overwhelming probability. This is the content of the following lemma. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
from (
4.1
).

Lemma 6.8
.

For
\varepsilon>0
set
M_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{\log(1/p)}
. Then for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\langle w,Z^{R}w\rangle>2+t\Big)=-\infty.

Postponing the proof of Lemma
6.8
for later we first show how it yields Lemma
6.7
.

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

Expanding
\lambda_{Z}=\langle{\bm{u}},Z{\bm{u}}\rangle
, we can write
\lambda_{Z}=\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},Z_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+2\langle{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon},\widecheck{Z}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+\langle{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon},Z^{(J)}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle
.
As a consequence,

\lx@nobreakspace\lambda_{Z}\leq\|Z\|\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}+2\|Z\|\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|+\sup_{w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\lx@nobreakspace\langle w,Zw\rangle.

Thus if
\lambda>2
,
r\geq 1
and
\eta\in(0,1)
, we have

\lx@nobreakspace\big\{\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\eta,\lambda_{Z}\geq\lambda\big\}\subset\{\lambda-3r\eta\leq\sup_{w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\lx@nobreakspace\langle w,Zw\rangle\big\}\cup\big\{\|Z\|\geq r\big\}.

Combining Lemmas
6.5
and
6.8
we get the claim.
∎

It now remains to prove Lemma
6.8
. Since
H^{R}
has bounded entries, by Lemma
5.8
we already know that the statement holds for
H^{R}
instead of
Z^{R}
. Thus, it suffices to show that the supremum of
\langle w,B^{R}w\rangle
where
w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}}
is negligible with overwhelming probability. We prove the following stronger statement which will be used later in this section.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any
t>0
and
M_{\varepsilon}
as in Lemma
6.8
,

(6.12)

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\to 0\atop\varepsilon^{\prime}/\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{v\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}},w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\langle v,B^{R}w\rangle>t\Big)=-\infty.

This results says that a large deviation of the operator norm of
B
should come from a localised structure: In order to see an atypically large inner product
\langle v,B^{R}w\rangle
, either
v
should have entries that are of order
1
or
w
should put weight on a set of vertices of size much smaller than
np/\log(1/p)
.

As we will use a concentration argument, we need to regularize the distribution of
\widetilde{B}^{R}
in a first step by adding an independent centered random symmetric matrix
\widetilde{C}^{R}
whose entries have a small variance such that the distribution of
\widetilde{B}^{R}+\widetilde{C}^{R}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. In the following lemma we prove the existence of such a matrix
\widetilde{C}^{R}
.

Lemma 6.10
.

For
R
large enough, independent of
n
, there exists
\widetilde{C}^{R}
a centered symmetric random matrix independent of
\widetilde{B}^{R}
such that

(a)

(\widetilde{C}^{R}_{i,i})_{i\in[n]}
and
(\widetilde{C}^{R}_{i,j})_{i<j}
are independent families of i.i.d. random variables.

(b)

For any
(i,j)\in[n]^{2}
,
\mathbb{E}[(\widetilde{C}^{R}_{i,j})^{2}]=o_{R}(1)
.

(c)

The distribution of
\widetilde{B}^{R}+\widetilde{C}^{R}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt norm and with a constant
c_{R}
independent of
n
.

Proof.

As log-Sobolev inequalities tensorise (see
[
42
, Corollary 5.7]
) it suffices to show that for
(i,j)\in\{(1,1),(1,2)\}
, there exists a centered random variable
\widetilde{C}^{R}_{i,j}
with the property (b)
such that the distribution of
\widetilde{B}^{R}_{i,j}+\widetilde{C}^{R}_{i,j}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{R}
. Recall from (
6.3
) that
\widetilde{B}_{i,j}^{R}=G_{i,j}{\bf 1}\{G_{i,j}\notin[-a_{i,j}(R),b_{i,j}(R)]\}
. Let
c_{i,j}:=\min(a_{i,j}(R),b_{i,j}(R))/4
. Define
\beta_{i,j}=\alpha
if
i=j
, and
\beta_{i,j}=\beta
otherwise, and fix
\beta^{\prime}_{i,j}>\beta_{i,j}
. Now, for
R
large enough set
V_{i,j}
to be a symmetric function on
\mathbb{R}
defined as

\lx@nobreakspace V_{i,j}(x):=\begin{cases}\frac{c_{i,j}x^{2}}{2}&\text{ if }|x|\leq c_{i,j},\\
\frac{x^{2}}{2\beta_{i,j}^{\prime}}+k_{i,j}|x|+\ell_{i,j}&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
\ell_{i,j}\leq 0\leq k_{i,j}
are chosen so that
V_{i,j}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Since
V_{i,j}^{\prime}
is monotonically increasing the function
V_{i,j}
is convex.
Define the probability measure
\mu_{i,j}(dx)\propto\exp(-V_{i,j}(x))dx
on
\mathbb{R}
. Clearly
\mu_{i,j}
has zero mean and one can easily check that
\int x^{2}d\mu_{i,j}(x)=o_{R}(1)
since
c_{i,j}\to+\infty
as
R\to+\infty
. We will show that
\mu_{i,j}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality, and that if
\upsilon_{i,j}
is the distribution of
\widetilde{B}^{R}_{i,j}
then
\upsilon_{i,j}\ast\mu_{i,j}
also satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality.

As for
R
large enough
V^{\prime}_{i,j}(z+h)-V_{i,j}^{\prime}(z-h)\geq 2h/\beta^{\prime}_{i,j}
for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
h\geq 0
, it follows that
V_{i,j}
is strongly convex with convexity parameter bounded from below by
1/\beta^{\prime}_{i,j}
. By
[
20
, Proposition 3.1]
we deduce that
\mu_{i,j}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality with constant
2\beta^{\prime}_{i,j}
. Now, from
[
50
, Theorem 2.1]
and Jensen’s inequality, we know that a sufficient condition for
\upsilon_{i,j}\ast\mu_{i,j}
to satisfy a log-Sobolev inequality is that for some
\lambda>1
,

(6.13)

\int\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda\beta_{i,j}^{\prime}}{2}|V_{i,j}^{\prime}(x)-V_{i,j}^{\prime}(x-z)|^{2}\Big)d\upsilon_{x}(z)d\mu_{i,j}(x)<+\infty,

where
\upsilon_{x}(dz)\propto e^{-V_{i,j}(x-z)}d\upsilon_{i,j}(z)
. In order to prove (
6.13
) we note that
\mathrm{supp}(\upsilon_{i,j})\subset\{0\}\cup[-a_{i,j}(R),b_{i,j}(R)]^{c}
. Since
c_{i,j}<\min(a_{i,j}(R),b_{i,j}(R))/2
, if
z\notin[-b_{i,j}(R),a_{i,j}(R)]
, then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
x
and
x-z
cannot be both in
[-c_{i,j},c_{i,j}]
. As a consequence, one can check that for any
z\in[-a_{i,j}(R),b_{i,j}(R)]^{c}\cup\{0\}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
|V^{\prime}_{i,j}(x)-V^{\prime}_{i,j}(x-z)|\leq|z|/\beta^{\prime}_{i,j}+\kappa_{i,j}
, where
\kappa_{i,j}>0
depends on
R
. Moreover,
x\mapsto V_{i,j}(x)-x^{2}/(2\beta_{i,j}^{\prime})-k_{i,j}|x|
is uniformly bounded. Thus, by (
6.13
), it suffices to show that there exists
\lambda>1
such that

(6.14)

\lx@nobreakspace\int\frac{\exp\big(\frac{1}{2\beta_{i,j}^{\prime}}(\lambda z^{2}-(x-z)^{2}-x^{2}))}{\int\exp\big(-\frac{(x-z^{\prime})^{2}}{2\beta_{i,j}^{\prime}}-k_{i,j}|x-z^{\prime}|\big)d\upsilon_{i,j}(z^{\prime})}d\upsilon_{i,j}(z)dx<+\infty.

Arguing as in the proof of
[
50
, Theorem 2.1]
, we can localize the integral in the denominator on a interval
[-L,L]
such that
\upsilon_{i,j}([-L,L])\geq 1/2
, and lower bound up to a multiplicative constant the integrand by
\exp(-x^{2}/(2\beta_{i,j}^{\prime})-C|x|)
, where
C>0
depend on
L
. Using the bound
(x-z)^{2}\geq[t/(1-t)]x^{2}-tz^{2}
for
x,z\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in(0,1)
, we get that (
6.14
) is implied by the condition that there exists
\lambda>1
and
t\in(0,1)
such that

(6.15)

\lx@nobreakspace\int\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2\beta_{i,j}^{\prime}}\big((\lambda+t)z^{2}-\frac{t}{1-t}x^{2}\big)+C|x|\Big)d\upsilon_{i,j}(z)dx<+\infty.

Since
\int\exp(z^{2}/(2\beta^{\prime\prime}))d\upsilon(z)<+\infty
for any
\beta^{\prime\prime}>\beta_{i,j}
(by (
1.2
)) we finally obtain that there indeed exist
\lambda>1
and
t\in(0,1)
such that (
6.15
) holds. This completes the proof. ∎

With Lemma
6.10
now at our disposal we can give a proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Let
\widetilde{C}^{R}
be a symmetric random matrix independent of
\widetilde{B}^{R}
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
6.10
and define
\widetilde{T}^{R}:=\widetilde{B}^{R}+\widetilde{C}^{R}
. For any realisation of the graph
\Xi
and
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
define the function

\lx@nobreakspace f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(K):=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}},w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\langle v,(K\circ\Xi)w\rangle,\ \forall K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

We will prove that for any
\theta\geq 0
,

(6.16)

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\to 0\atop\varepsilon^{\prime}/\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\theta\sqrt{np}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})}\big]\leq 0

Using
\mathbb{E}\widetilde{C}^{R}=0
and Jensen’s inequality we have for any
\theta\geq 0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\theta\sqrt{np}f_{\Xi}^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\widetilde{B}^{R})}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\theta\sqrt{np}f_{\Xi}^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{R})}\big].

Therefore the above inequality together with (
6.16
) and Chernoff’s inequality yield (
6.12
). So we only need to prove (
6.16
).
To this end, fixing a realisation of
\Xi
for now, we can write for any
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
,

\lx@nobreakspace f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(K)-f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(K^{\prime})\leq L_{\Xi}\|K-K^{\prime}\|_{2},\text{ where }L_{\Xi}^{2}:=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}},w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\sum_{i,j}\xi_{i,j}v_{i}^{2}w_{j}^{2}.

Since, by Lemma
6.10
, for
R
large enough
\widetilde{T}^{R}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality with some constant
c_{R}>0
, we deduce by
[
42
, Eqn. (5.8)]
that for any
\theta\geq 0
,

(6.17)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}\big[e^{\theta\sqrt{np}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})}\big]\leq e^{\frac{np\theta^{2}L_{\Xi}^{2}}{2c_{R}}+\theta\sqrt{np}\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})}.

On the other hand, we know by Lemma
4.3
that for any
\delta^{2}\geq\max({\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2},3p)
and any
n
large enough

(6.18)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\big(L_{\Xi}>\delta)\leq e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2(\varepsilon^{\prime}M_{\varepsilon})^{2}}np\log(1/p)}=e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2(\varepsilon^{\prime}/\varepsilon)^{2}}np}.

If
\varepsilon=o(1)
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}/\varepsilon=o(1)
, then we can find
\delta=o(1)
such that
\delta\gg\varepsilon^{\prime}/\varepsilon
and
\delta^{2}\geq\max({\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2},3p)
, and fix this choice of
\delta
for the rest of the proof.
Now, observe that the function
\Xi\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})
is convex and that for any symmetric matrices
\Xi=(\xi_{i,j})_{i,j}
,
\Xi^{\prime}=(\xi^{\prime}_{i,j})_{i,j}
,

\displaystyle\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})-\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{R})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}\|\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ(\Xi-\Xi^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}\|\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ(\Xi-\Xi^{\prime})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i,j}\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{T}^{R}_{i,j})^{2}(\xi_{i,j}-\xi_{i,j}^{\prime})^{2}},

implying that it is
\sigma_{R}
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, where
\sigma^{2}_{R}:=\max(\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{T}^{R}_{1,1})^{2}),\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{T}^{R}_{1,2})^{2})
. Therefore using
[
24
, Theorem 8.6]
, we get that for any
\theta\geq 0
,

(6.19)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}\Big(e^{\theta\sqrt{np}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})-\mathbb{E}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})\big)}\Big)\leq\exp\Big(\frac{\theta^{2}\sigma_{R}^{2}np}{8}\Big).

Note that
\sigma_{R}\to 0
as
R\to+\infty
. Thus, combining (
6.17
), (
6.19
), and (
6.18
) together with the fact that
L_{\Xi}\leq 1
a.s., we deduce that in order to prove (
6.16
) it suffices to show that

(6.20)

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\sup_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{np}}\mathbb{E}f_{\Xi}^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{R})=0.

Turning to do that using Seginer’s theorem
[
47
, Theorem 1.1]
and a symmetrization argument
[
36
, Section 4]
(see also
[
41
, Theorem 3.5]
), we know that

(6.21)

\mathbb{E}f^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Xi}(\widetilde{T}^{R})\leq\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}\|\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi\|\lesssim\mathbb{E}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|.

Using again the sub-Gaussian concentration property of
\widetilde{T}^{R}
, ensured by Lemma
6.10
, together with the fact that
\Xi\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|
is a convex
\sigma_{R}
-Lipschitz function with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt norm we find that for any
t>0
and
i\in[n]
,

(6.22)

\mathbb{P}\big(\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|-\mathbb{E}\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|>t\big)\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|-\mathbb{E}\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|>t/2\big)\\
+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}\big(\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|-\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{T}^{R}}\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|>t/2\big)\big]\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{8(c_{R}\vee\sigma_{R}^{2})}\Big).

It is a classical fact that a sub-Gaussian tail such as (
6.22
) bound entails

(6.23)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in[n]}\big(\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|-\mathbb{E}\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|\big)=O\big(\sqrt{(c_{R}\vee\sigma_{R}^{2}\log n})\big).

On the other hand, using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get
\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\big[\|(\widetilde{T}^{R}\circ\Xi)_{i}\|\big]\leq\sigma_{R}\sqrt{np}
.
This observation together with (
6.21
) and (
6.23
)
gives the claim (
6.20
) as
\lim_{R\to+\infty}\sigma_{R}=0
. ∎

6.4.
Exponential equivalent of the eigenvalue-eigenvector equation

Recall (
2.5
) and the definition of
J
from (
6.7
). The goal of this section is to find an exponential equivalent of the equation in (
2.5
) involving terms with tractable large deviation behaviours. More precisely, we will show the following proposition.

Proposition 6.11
.

For any
t,\varepsilon>0
,
R\geq 1
and
\lambda>2
, define the event

(6.24)

\lx@nobreakspace\Omega_{\varepsilon,R,t}:=\Big\{\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},Z_{{J}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+m(\lambda)\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{J}}{\bm{u}}_{k}^{2}(\|(\widecheck{H}_{J})_{k}\|^{2}-1)_{+}+\|\widecheck{B}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\Big)\geq\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}/m(\lambda)-t\Big\},

where
\mathfrak{J}:=\{k\in J:|{\bm{u}}_{k}|\geq\varepsilon\}
. Then, for any
\delta>0
such that
\lambda\geq 2+\delta
,

(6.25)

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\Omega_{\varepsilon,R,t}^{c}\cap\Theta_{\lambda,\delta}\big)=-\infty,\,\text{ where }\Theta_{\lambda,\delta}:=\{\lambda_{H^{(J)}}\leq\lambda-\delta\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_{Z}\}.

Proposition
6.11
shows that the large deviation upper tail event of
\lambda_{Z}
, intersected with the event that
\lambda_{Z}
is away from
{\rm Spec}(H^{(J)})
, enforces an inequality, at the large deviation scale, involving
\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},Z_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle
, which one can think as the ‘clique contribution’, the ‘degrees’
\|(\widecheck{H}_{J})_{k}\|^{2}
in the network
H
, and
\|\widecheck{B}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
. We will see later that although the last term is non negligible it corresponds to a sub optimal scenario and thus will ultimately disappear from the final expression for the rate function.

The proof of Proposition
6.11
