require several ingredients. Proofs of those are carried out over the next few subsections.

6.4.1.
B^{(J)}
is spectrally negligible over completely delocalised vectors

In a first step, using that
J^{c}
does not contain any high degree vertices with respect to the network
B
, we show that
\|B^{(J)}w\|=o(1)
when
w
is a completely delocalised unit vector, that is
\|w\|_{\infty}\ll\sqrt{\log(1/p)/np}
.

Lemma 6.12
.

For any
t>0
and
\varepsilon>0
, with
M_{\varepsilon}
as in Lemma
6.8
,

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\|B^{(J)}w\|>t\big)=-\infty.

Proof.

Let
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{J^{c}}
. Write
w^{\prime}=x+y
with
x_{i}:=w^{\prime}_{i}{\bf 1}\{|w_{i}^{\prime}|>\varepsilon^{\prime}\}
, for any
i\in J^{c}
,
where
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
is a parameter to be chosen later. Note that for any
w\in\mathbb{B}^{J^{c}}
, we have by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\lx@nobreakspace\langle x,B^{(J)}w\rangle\leq\sum_{i\in J^{c}}|x_{i}|\|B_{i}\|\leq\|x\|_{1}\max_{i\in J^{c}}\|B_{i}\|.

Since
\|x\|_{2}\leq 1
and for any
i\in J^{c}
we have
|x_{i}|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}
, we deduce that
\|x\|_{1}\leq 1/\varepsilon^{\prime}
. By construction (see (
6.7
)),
J^{c}
does not contain any vertex of high degree with respect to the network
B
. In particular
\max_{i\notin J}\|B_{i}\|\leq\delta
. Thus
\langle x,B^{(J)}w\rangle\leq\delta/\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
So, we have proven that

\lx@nobreakspace\sup_{w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}}\|B^{(J)}w\|^{2}\leq\delta/\varepsilon^{\prime}+\sup\big\{\langle y,Bw\rangle:y\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}},w\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}\big\}.

Since
\delta=o(\varepsilon)
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we can find
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
such that
\delta\ll\varepsilon^{\prime}\ll\varepsilon
.
Applying Lemma
6.9
with these choices the claim follows.
∎

6.4.2.
Concentration of the resolvent

The most important step to move from (
2.5
) to Proposition
6.11
is the following concentration bound.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
t,\delta,\lambda
, and
\Theta_{\lambda,\delta}
be as in Proposition
6.11
. Set
\mathbb{P}_{\Theta_{\lambda,\delta}}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(\cdot\cap\Theta_{\lambda,\delta})
. Then

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}_{\Theta_{\lambda,\delta}}\Big(\langle{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon},(\lambda_{Z^{R}}-H^{(J)})^{-1}{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle-m(\lambda_{Z^{R}})\|{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}>t\Big)=-\infty.

The proof of Lemma
6.13
builds on the idea developed in Section
4
that convex functions with flat gradients of sparse Wigner matrices with bounded entries satisfy improved sub-Gaussian upper tail bounds. Observe that in the lemma above we have restricted our concentration estimate to the event where there is a gap in the spectrum, in the sense that
\lambda_{H^{(J)}}+\delta\leq\lambda_{Z}
for some
\delta>0
. Since the bounded case is settled we can work on this event as we can compute the probability of the complement event
\{\lambda_{H^{(J)}}>\lambda-\delta\}
.

Turning to prove Lemma
6.13
we first show that if
Y
is a sparse Wigner matrix, in the sense of Definition
1.1
, with bounded entries, then
f_{\lambda,x}(Y):=\langle x,(\lambda-Y)^{-1}x\rangle
(note that the domain of
f_{\lambda,x}
is
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\lambda}
) concentrates when
x
belongs to a set of ‘good’ directions, to be defined, and that the concentration can be made uniform in these directions if one considers vectors close to a given low dimensional subspace.
To this end, define for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varepsilon,r>0
and
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\lambda}

(6.26)

\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}(K):=\big\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:\|(\lambda-K)^{-1}x\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon,\ \|(\lambda-K)^{-1}x\|_{2}\leq r\big\}.

Since
\nabla_{K}f_{\lambda,x}(K)=(\lambda-K)^{-1}x((\lambda-K)^{-1}x)^{\sf T}
, the set
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}(K)
consists of directions in which the gradient at
K
of the map
K^{\prime}\mapsto f_{\lambda,x}(K^{\prime})
is flat, in the sense that it belongs to
r^{2}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon/r}^{1}
(see (
4.1
) for a definition).
Finally, denote by
\mathfrak{S}
the set of subspaces
\mathcal{W}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(6.27)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W})\leq np/\log(1/p)\text{ and }\ \|x\|_{1}/\|x\|_{2}\leq np/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}=:a_{n},\,\forall x\in\mathcal{W}.

With this notation we have the following proposition.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
\lambda,r>0
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
be such that
\lambda>2+\delta
. Let
Y
be a sparse Wigner matrix with bounded entries. Fix
\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}
. For
\lambda^{\prime},t,\eta>0
and
K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{n}
define

F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(K):=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(K)}\big(f_{\lambda^{\prime},x}(K)-m(\lambda^{\prime})\|x\|^{2}\big)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(K):=\{x\in\mathcal{V}_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon,r}(K):d(x,\mathcal{W})\leq\eta\},

where
d(y,E):=\inf_{w\in E}\|y-w\|
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, for any
t>0
and
\eta>0
small enough

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda}F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(Y)>t,\lambda_{Y}<\lambda-\delta\Big)=-\infty.

Let us add here that the probability bound being uniform over
\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}
will be important later when we apply Proposition
6.14
to a random minor of
H
.

To prove Proposition
6.14

we first show that for a given
\lambda>2
and
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}(Y)
, the probability that
f_{\lambda,x}(Y)
is greater than
m(\lambda)\|x\|^{2}
decreases at the exponential scale faster than
np
. To state and prove this concentration result we need to regularise
f_{\lambda,x}
which in turn requires some more notation. Recall (
6.26
) and for any
\delta,\varepsilon,r>0
define
E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}:=\big\{K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}:x\in\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}(K)\big\}
.
In words
E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
is the set of symmetric matrices for which
x
is a ‘good’ direction for the resolvent at
\lambda
.
Extend
f_{\lambda,x}
to the whole space
\mathcal{H}_{n}
by setting

(6.28)

\widetilde{f}^{\varepsilon,r}_{\lambda,x}(K):=\sup_{K^{\prime}\in E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}}\big\{f_{\lambda,x}(K^{\prime})+\langle\nabla f_{\lambda,x}(K^{\prime}),K-K^{\prime}\rangle\big\},\ \forall K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

By
[
17
, Exercise I.I.15]
, we know that
f_{\lambda,x}
is convex on its domain. Therefore,
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
coincides with
f_{\lambda,x}
on
E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
is by construction convex and
r^{2}
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt norm on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
since
\|\nabla f_{\lambda,x}(K^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq r^{2}
for any
K^{\prime}\in E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
. Note that although not explicitly mentioned,
E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
and
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
both depend implicitly on
\delta
.

Applying the concentration inequality for functions with flat gradients of Proposition
4.1
and using the isotropic local law we get the following lemma. Its proof is deferred to Section
7
.
Recall the definition of
a_{n}
from (
6.27
).

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\delta>0
and
\lambda>2+\delta
. Assume
Y
is a sparse Wigner matrix with bounded entries. Then for any
t,r>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\|x\|_{1}\leq a_{n}\atop x\in 2\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathbb{P}\big(\widetilde{f}^{\varepsilon,r}_{\lambda,x}(Y)-m(\lambda)\|x\|^{2}>t\big)=-\infty.

Combining Lemma
6.15
together with a net argument on the vectors in
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}(Y)
close to a low dimensional subspace
\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}
, we get the following proposition. We draw the attention of the reader to the fact that
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}(Y)
being a set of a random vectors the net argument will not be completely straightforward.

Proposition 6.16
.

With the same setting and notation of Proposition
6.14
for any
t>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathbb{P}\big(F_{\lambda,\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(Y)>t,\lambda_{Y}<\lambda-\delta\big)=-\infty.

Proof.

Starting from Lemma
6.15
and fixing
\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}
we first perform a net argument on
x\in\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{W}}:=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Without loss of generality assume that
\delta\in(0,1)
. Since
\|(\lambda-K)^{-1}\|\leq 1/\delta
for any
K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}
, we have for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
,

(6.29)

\lx@nobreakspace E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}\subset E_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon+s^{\prime},r+s^{\prime}}\text{ and }\ \lx@nobreakspace|f_{\lambda,x}(K)-f_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}(K)|\leq 2s^{\prime},\ \forall K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n},

with
s^{\prime}=\delta^{-1}\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{2}
. For any
\eta\in(0,1)
let
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}(\mathcal{W})
be an
\eta
-net for the
\ell^{2}
-norm of
2\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{W}}
. We claim that the two stability estimates of (
6.29
) yield for any
K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}
,

(6.30)

\lx@nobreakspace F_{\lambda,\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(K)\leq\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}(\mathcal{W})}\big(\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon+s,r+s}(K)-m(\lambda)\|x^{\prime}\|^{2}\big)+12s,

where
s=2\delta^{-1}\eta
.
Indeed, if
K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}
and
x\in\mathcal{G}_{\lambda,\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(K)
, then this means that
K\in E_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}
and that there exists
x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
such that
\|x-x^{\prime\prime}\|\leq\eta
. Since
\|x\|\leq 1
this entails that
\|x^{\prime\prime}\|\leq 2
as
\eta\leq 1
, and we deduce that there exists
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}(\mathcal{W})
such that
\|x-x^{\prime}\|\leq 2\eta
. Using (
6.29
) we get

\lx@nobreakspace f_{\lambda,x}(K)\leq f_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}(K)+4\delta^{-1}\eta.

Moreover,
K\in E_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon+s,r+s}
(by (
6.29
) again) which yields
f_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}(K)=\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon+s,r+s}(K)
. Noting that
m(\lambda)\leq 1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, this ends the proof of the claim (
6.30
).

By
[
3
, Corollary 4.1.15]
we know that for any
\eta\in(0,1)
we can find such a set
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}(\mathcal{W})
with

\lx@nobreakspace\log|\mathcal{N}_{\eta}(\mathcal{W})|\leq\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W})\log(3/\eta)=o(np),

where we used the fact that
\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W})\leq np/\log(1/p)
.
So using a union bound and Lemma
6.15
, we get for any
t>0
, and
\eta>0
small enough

(6.31)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}(\mathcal{W})}\big(\widetilde{f}^{\varepsilon+s,r+s}_{\lambda,x^{\prime}}(Y)-m(\lambda)\|x^{\prime}\|^{2}\big)>t)=-\infty.

Combining this with (
6.30
) ends the proof.
∎

Finally we perform a net argument on
\lambda
to obtain the uniform concentration result of Proposition
6.14
.

Proof of Proposition
6.14
.

Fix
\lambda>2+\delta
and
K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
be the standard
\varepsilon
-net of the interval
(\lambda,\lambda+\varepsilon^{-1})
. Pick
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\geq\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
such that
0\leq\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon
.
Since
\|(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-K)^{-1}-(\lambda_{2}^{\prime}-K)^{-1}\|\leq q:=\delta^{-2}\varepsilon
we have the inclusion

\lx@nobreakspace\mathcal{V}_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon,r}(K)\subset\mathcal{V}_{\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon+q,r+q}(K),

and as a consequence
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(K)\subset\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2},\eta}^{\varepsilon+q,r+q,\mathcal{W}}(K)
. Therefore using the facts that
\lambda^{\prime}\mapsto f_{\lambda^{\prime},x}(K)
is non increasing on
(\lambda,+\infty)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and that
m
is
\delta^{-2}
-Lipschitz on
(2+\delta,+\infty)
we have

\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\in(\lambda,\lambda+\varepsilon^{-1})}F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(Y)\leq\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}}F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon+q,r+q,\mathcal{W}}(Y)+q,

on the event
Y\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}
. Using Proposition
6.16
, a union bound, and the fact that
|\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}|=O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
we get for any
t>0
and
\eta
small enough

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\in(\lambda,\lambda+\varepsilon^{-1})}F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(Y)>t,Y\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}\big)\\
\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}}F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon+q,r+q,\mathcal{W}}(Y)>t,Y\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}\big)=-\infty.

On the other hand, observe that on the event
Y\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-\delta}_{n}
we have

\lx@nobreakspace\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda+\varepsilon^{-1}}F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(Y)\leq\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda+\varepsilon^{-1}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}f_{\lambda^{\prime},x}(Y)\leq\varepsilon.

The last two observations end the proof.
∎

We can now give a proof of Lemma
6.13
.

Proof of Lemma
6.13
.

Recall that
a_{n}=np/\sqrt{\log(1/p)}
and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}:=\{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]:|\mathcal{J}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}np/\log(1/p)\}
.
Define for any
t,r,\varepsilon,\eta>0
,
\mathcal{J}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
, and
\mathcal{W}
a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathcal{F}_{t,\mathcal{W}}^{\mathcal{J},\varepsilon,\eta}:=\Big\{\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda}F_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}^{\varepsilon,r,\mathcal{W}}(H^{(\mathcal{J})})>t,\lambda-\delta>\lambda_{H^{(\mathcal{J})}}\Big\}.

Using that
\log|\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}|=O(\varepsilon^{2}np)
and applying Proposition
6.14
to
H^{(\mathcal{J})}/\sigma_{R}
, where
\sigma_{R}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}H_{1,2}^{2}=1+o_{R}(1)
and
\mathcal{J}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
, we get that for
\eta
small enough and
R
large enough

(6.32)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{J}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}\sup_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{J}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{t,\mathcal{W}}^{\mathcal{J},\varepsilon,\eta})=-\infty,

where
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{J}}
is the set of subspaces
\mathcal{W}
of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
satisfying (
6.27
).
Now define the event
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}:=\{\sum_{i\in[n],j\in\mathcal{J}}(\xi_{i,j}+\xi_{i,j}^{\prime})\leq a_{n}^{2}\}
. Using a binomial tail bound together with the fact that
np|\mathcal{J}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}a_{n}^{2}
for any
\mathcal{J}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
and
a_{n}^{2}\gg np
, we obtain that for
\varepsilon
small enough

(6.33)

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{J}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}^{c})=-\infty.

On the event
{G}_{\mathcal{J}}
,
\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}}
has at most
a_{n}^{2}
non zero entries. It follows that for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
|\mathrm{supp}(\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}}v)|\leq a_{n}^{2}
and therefore
\|\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|_{1}\leq a_{n}\|\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|_{2}
. Besides
{\rm rank}(\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}})\leq|\mathcal{J}|\leq np/\log(1/p)
for
\varepsilon
small enough. Thus the subspace
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{J}}:=\mathrm{ran}(\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{J}}
on
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}
. As the tail estimate (
6.32
) is uniform in
\mathcal{W}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{J}}
and
(H^{(\mathcal{J})},\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}})
are independent,
the estimate (
6.33
) together with Lemma
6.6
, and a union bound yield for any
t>0
and
\eta
small enough

(6.34)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{t,\mathcal{W}_{J}}^{J,\varepsilon,\eta})\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{J}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{t,\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{J}}}^{\mathcal{J},\varepsilon,\eta})+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log|\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}|=-\infty.

Recall that
{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}=\widecheck{Z}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\lx@nobreakspace+B^{(J)}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}
, where by definition
{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}
and fix
\kappa\geq 1
. When
\lambda_{Z}>\lambda_{H^{(J)}}
obviously one has
\lambda_{Z}\notin{\rm Spec}(H^{(J)})
. Hence, on the event where
\|{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\kappa
, by (
2.5
) we have
\kappa^{-1}{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{V}_{\lambda_{Z}}^{\varepsilon,1}(H^{(J)})
all large
n
(recall (
6.26
)). Therefore, upon denoting

(6.35)

\Gamma_{t,\varepsilon}:=\{f_{\lambda_{Z},{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}}(H^{(J)})-m(\lambda_{Z})\|{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\leq t,\lambda_{Z}>\lambda_{H^{(J)}}\}

and

(6.36)

\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\eta}^{\kappa}:=\{\|\widecheck{Z}_{J}\|\leq\kappa/2,\ \|B^{({J})}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\eta\},

we deduce from (
6.34
) and the fact that on
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\eta}^{\kappa}
, for
\eta\in(0,1/2]
, we have
\|{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\kappa
and
d({\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon},\mathcal{W}_{J})\leq\eta
, that for any
t>0
,
\kappa\geq 1
and
\eta>0
small enough,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}_{\Theta_{\lambda,\delta}}(\Gamma_{t,\varepsilon}^{c}\cap\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\eta}^{\kappa})=-\infty.

Finally using Lemmas
6.5
and
6.12
(as
{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{D}_{M_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{np}}
) we get the claim.
∎

6.4.3.
Exponential equivalent of
\|\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|^{2}

We now move to the analysis of the large deviation upper tail of
\|{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\|
, where
{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}=\widecheck{Z}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}+B^{(J)}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}
. As
\|B^{(J)}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\|=o(1)
by Lemma
6.12
, it boils down to understand the deviations of
\|\widecheck{Z}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|
.

Proposition 6.17
.

Fix
t\in(0,1)
. Let
\Upsilon_{t,\varepsilon}:=\Upsilon_{t,\varepsilon}({\bm{J}},{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon})
, where for any
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
and
v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}

\Upsilon_{t,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{J},v):=\big\{\|\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}}{v}\|^{2}\leq\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{v}}v_{k}^{2}(\|\widecheck{H}_{\mathcal{J}}^{(k)}\|^{2}-1)_{+}+\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}{v}\|^{2}+\|{v}\|^{2}+t\big\},\text{ and }\mathcal{I}_{v}:=\{k:|v_{k}|\geq\varepsilon\}.

Then

(6.37)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\Upsilon_{t,\varepsilon}(J,{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon})^{c})=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}}\mathbb{P}(\Upsilon_{t,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{J},v)^{c})=-\infty.

Proof.

Observe that the first equality in (
6.37
) follows from Lemma
6.6
, a union bound, and a net argument. So we only prove the second equality. Let
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
and
v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
To ease the notation, write
\widecheck{Z}
instead of
\widecheck{Z}_{\mathcal{J}}
, and similarly for
\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
and
\widecheck{H}_{\mathcal{J}}
.
We first prove that with overwhelming probability
\widecheck{H}v
and
\widecheck{B}v
are almost orthogonal. Since
H
and
B
are independent, and
\widetilde{H}
has entries bounded by some
\kappa_{R}>0
, we deduce by (
4.23
) that

(6.38)

\mathbb{P}\big(\langle\widecheck{B}v,\widecheck{H}v\rangle>t\|\widecheck{B}v\|\big)\leq\sup_{w\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}}\mathbb{P}\big(\langle\widecheck{H}v,w\rangle\geq t\big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{ct^{2}}{\kappa_{R}^{2}}np\log(1/p)\Big),

for any
t>0
, where
c>0
is a numerical constant.
Besides, we can write for any
t,s>0
,

(6.39)

\mathbb{P}\big(\langle\widecheck{B}v,\widecheck{H}v\rangle\geq s\big)\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\langle\widecheck{B}v,\widecheck{H}v\rangle>(s/t)\|\widecheck{B}v\|\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{B}\|\geq t\big).

Using (
6.38
) and the fact that
\|B\|
is exponentially tight, by Lemma
5.12
, we obtain from (
6.39
) by first letting
n\to+\infty
and then letting
t\to+\infty
, that for any
s>0
,

(6.40)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\langle\widecheck{B}v,\widecheck{H}v\rangle\geq s\big)=-\infty.

For
\varepsilon>0
write
v=x^{\varepsilon}+y^{\varepsilon}
where
x_{i}^{\varepsilon}:=v_{i}{\bf 1}_{|v_{i}|>\varepsilon}

for any
i\in\mathcal{J}
. Note that
x^{\varepsilon}
and
y^{\varepsilon}
have disjoint supports. This yields that
\widecheck{H}x^{\varepsilon}
and
\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}
are independent. Thus the same argument as for (
6.40
) gives

(6.41)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\langle\widecheck{H}x^{\varepsilon},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle>s\big)=-\infty,\qquad\text{ for any }s>0.

Since Lemma
5.6
tells us that
\|\widecheck{H}x^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
is dominated by the weighted sum of the degrees of the vertices in the support of
x^{\varepsilon}
, it remains to show that
\|\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
concentrates at the exponential scale, that is for any
s>0
and
R
large enough

(6.42)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}>s+\|y^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\big)=-\infty.

Writing for any
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
,
y^{\prime}=v^{\prime}+w^{\prime}
with
v^{\prime}_{i}:=y^{\prime}_{i}{\bf 1}_{|y^{\prime}_{i}|>\varepsilon^{-1}/\sqrt{np}}
for any
i\notin\mathcal{J}
, we
get the inequality

(6.43)

\lx@nobreakspace\|\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\|=\sup_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}}\langle y^{\prime},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle\leq\sup_{v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\langle v^{\prime},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle+\sup_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{-1}/\sqrt{np}}}\langle w^{\prime},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle,

where
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
is as in (
4.20
). By (
4.23
) and a union bound there exists a constant
C<+\infty
such that for any
t>CR\varepsilon
,

(6.44)

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\langle v^{\prime},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle>t\|y^{\varepsilon}\|\Big)=-\infty.

On the other hand, using a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.8
and the fact that
\|y^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
, we deduce from Proposition
4.2
that for any
t>0
,
\varepsilon
small enough and
n
large enough,

(6.45)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{-1}/\sqrt{np}}}\langle w^{\prime},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\sup_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{-1}/\sqrt{np}}}\langle w^{\prime},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle>t\Big)\leq e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{\gamma_{0}\varepsilon^{2}}np},

where
\gamma_{0}
is a numerical constant. Besides
\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{H}_{1,2})^{2}=1+o_{R}(1)
, which implies that

(6.46)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}\sup_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon^{-1}/\sqrt{np}}}\langle w^{\prime},\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\rangle\leq\mathbb{E}\|\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\big(\mathbb{E}\|\widecheck{H}y^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\leq\|y^{\varepsilon}\|(1+o_{R}(1)).

Combining (
6.43
)–(
6.46
) we get the claim (
6.42
). This finally ends the proof.
∎

6.4.4.
Proof of Proposition
6.11

Fix
t>0
and
\eta>0
small. Recall the definition of
\Upsilon_{t,\varepsilon}
from Proposition
6.17
. Recall also (
6.35
)-(
6.36
).
By (
2.5
) on
\Gamma_{t,\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,t\eta}^{\eta^{-1}}
,

\lambda_{Z}\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\leq\lx@nobreakspace\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},Z_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+m(\lambda_{Z})\|{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}+2t\leq\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},Z_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+m(\lambda_{Z})\|\widecheck{Z}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}+5t,

where the last step follows from the fact that
\|{\bm{x}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\leq\|\widecheck{Z}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}+2\|Z\|.\|B^{(J)}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\|+\|B^{(J)}{\bm{w}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
. This together with the fact that
m
is non increasing on
[2,+\infty)
, that
\lambda^{\prime}-m(\lambda^{\prime})=1/m(\lambda^{\prime})
and
m(\lambda^{\prime})\leq 1
for
\lambda^{\prime}\geq 2
yield that on
\widehat{\Theta}:=\Theta_{\lambda,\delta}\cap\Upsilon_{t,\varepsilon}\cap\Gamma_{t,\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,t\eta}^{\eta^{-1}}
,

\lx@nobreakspace\frac{\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}}{m(\lambda)}\leq\lx@nobreakspace\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},Z_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle+m(\lambda)\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{J}}{\bm{u}}^{2}_{k}(\|\widecheck{H}_{J}^{(k)}\|^{2}-1)_{+}+\|\widecheck{B}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\Big)+6t,

where
\mathfrak{J}:=\{k\in J:|{\bm{u}}_{k}|\geq\varepsilon\}
.
Now combining Lemmas
6.5
,
6.12
, and
6.13
, and Proposition
6.17
we find the desired bound on
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\Theta}^{c})
. This completes the proof.
∎

6.5.
Large deviation estimates

In this section we analyze the large deviations of the random variables appearing in Proposition
6.11
,
\langle{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon},Z_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\rangle
,
\|\widecheck{H}_{J}^{(k)}\|^{2}
, and
\|\widecheck{B}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
. Since up to fixing
J
and
{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}
these random variables are independent, it is sufficient to compute their large deviation rate functions separately.

6.5.1.
Large deviations of
\langle v,Z^{R}v\rangle

In this short section we prove the following tail bound estimate.

Proposition 6.18
.

For any
t,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\log\mathbb{P}\Big(\langle v,Z^{R}v\rangle>t\sqrt{\beta+\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\beta\big)\|v\|_{4}^{4}+\varepsilon^{\prime}}\Big)\leq-\frac{t^{2}}{4}.

The extra parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
in the above proposition is only meant to give some security in the case where
\beta=0
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.4
it suffices to prove the same tail bound for
X
instead of
Z
.
Let
v\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. For any
\theta\geq 0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}(e^{\theta np\langle v,Xv\rangle})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big(e^{\theta\sqrt{np}v_{i}^{2}\xi_{i,i}G_{i,i}}\big)\prod_{i<j}\mathbb{E}\big(e^{2\theta\sqrt{np}\lx@nobreakspace v_{i}v_{j}\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}}\big).

Let
M>0
. Note that if
|\lambda|>M
then
\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\lambda\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}})\leq(\beta/2+o_{M}(1))\lambda^{2}
, for any
i\neq j
and
\mathbb{E}(e^{|\lambda|\xi_{i,i}G_{i,i}})\leq(\alpha/2+o_{M}(1))\lambda^{2}
.
If
|\lambda|\leq M
using the concavity of the
\log
and the fact that
\mathbb{E}G_{i,j}=0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\lambda\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}})=\log(1-p+p\mathbb{E}e^{\lambda G_{i,j}})\leq p\mathbb{E}(e^{\lambda G_{i,j}}-1-\lambda G_{i,j})\leq\lambda^{2}p\mathbb{E}(G_{i,j}^{2}e^{\lambda G_{i,j}})\leq\lambda^{2}pe^{O(M^{2})},

where in the second last step we used
e^{x}-1-x\leq x^{2}e^{x}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and in the last step we used that
G_{i,j}
is a sub-Gaussian random variable. Thus, for
n
large enough,
i\neq j\in[n]
, and any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lx@nobreakspace\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\lambda\xi_{i,j}G_{i,j}})\leq(\beta/2+o_{M}(1))\lambda^{2}\quad\lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace\text{ and }\quad\log\mathbb{E}(e^{|\lambda|\xi_{i,i}G_{i,i}})\leq(\alpha/2+o_{M}(1))\lambda^{2}.

As
v\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we find

\displaystyle\lx@nobreakspace\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\theta np\langle v,Xv\rangle})

\displaystyle\leq np\Big(\beta\sum_{i\neq j}v_{i}^{2}v_{j}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}^{4}+o_{M}(1)\Big)\theta^{2}=np\Big(\beta+\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\beta\big)\|v\|_{4}^{4}+o_{M}(1)\Big)\theta^{2}.

Using Chernoff’s inequality and then taking a
\limsup
over
n
first followed by a
\limsup
over
M
we get the claim.
∎

6.5.2.
Tail estimate of
\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|^{2}

We show the following lemma.

Lemma 6.19
.

For any
t>0
,

(6.47)

\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\sup_{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|^{2}>t\big)\leq-\frac{t}{2\beta}.

We start first by proving this tail bound in the case where
\mathcal{J}
is of bounded size.

Lemma 6.20
.

For any
t>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\sup_{\mathcal{I}\subset[n]\atop\#\mathcal{I}\leq m}\sup_{u\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{I}}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{I}}u\|^{2}>t\big)\leq-\frac{t}{2\beta}.

Proof.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
u\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
|\mathcal{I}|\leq m
.
For any
i\notin\mathcal{I}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
define

\lx@nobreakspace\Phi_{i}(\theta):=\log\mathbb{E}\big(e^{\theta np\big(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}B_{i,j}u_{j}\big)^{2}}\big)=\log\mathbb{E}\big(e^{\sqrt{2\theta}\Gamma\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}\widetilde{B}_{i,j}\xi_{i,j}u_{j}}\big),

where
\Gamma
is an independent standard Gaussian. Observe that it suffices to show that
for any
\theta<1/(2\beta)
,

\sup_{i\notin\mathcal{I}}\Phi_{i}(\theta)=o_{R}(1)p
uniformly in
u\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{I}
such that
|\mathcal{I}|\leq m
(one then uses Chernoff’s inequality).
Fix
\theta<1/(2\beta)
. Let
r,s>1
such that
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{s}=1
and
s\theta<1/(2\beta)
. Recall that
\widetilde{B}_{i,j}=G_{i,j}{\bf 1}_{G_{i,j}\notin I_{i,j}(R)}
. Using independence and Hölder’s inequality,

\lx@nobreakspace\Phi_{i}(\theta)\leq\log\mathbb{E}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{I}}(1-pq+pq^{\frac{1}{r}}e^{\frac{1}{s}\Lambda_{1,2}(\sqrt{2s^{2}\theta}\Gamma u_{j})}),

where
q=\mathbb{P}(G_{1,2}\notin I_{1,2}(R))=o_{R}(1)
.
Expanding the product and using that
\log(a+x)\leq x
for
a\in(0,1]
and
x\geq 0
we get

(6.48)

\displaystyle\lx@nobreakspace\Phi_{i}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E\subset\mathcal{I}\atop|E|\geq 1}(1-pq)^{|\mathcal{I}\setminus E|}(pq^{\frac{1}{r}})^{|E|}\mathbb{E}\big(e^{\frac{1}{s}\sum_{j\in E}\Lambda_{1,2}(\sqrt{2s^{2}\theta}\Gamma u_{j})}\big).

Let
\sigma^{2}:=\sup_{\theta\neq 0}\Lambda_{1,2}(\theta)/\theta^{2}
. Note that for any
M>0
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
, we can write
\Lambda_{1,2}(\zeta)\leq\sigma^{2}M^{2}+(\beta/2)(1+o_{M}(1))\zeta^{2}
. Therefore, for any
E\subset\mathcal{I}
,
E\neq\emptyset
, as
\|u\|\leq 1
,

\lx@nobreakspace\frac{1}{s}\sum_{j\in E}\Lambda_{1,2}(\sqrt{2s^{2}\theta}\Gamma u_{j})\leq\sigma^{2}M^{2}\frac{|E|}{s}+\beta\theta s\Gamma^{2}(1+o_{M}(1)),

which yields for
M
large enough

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}\big(e^{\frac{1}{s}\sum_{j\in E}\Lambda(\sqrt{2s^{2}\theta}\Gamma u_{j})}\big)\leq\frac{e^{\frac{\sigma^{2}M^{2}}{s}|E|}}{\sqrt{1-2\beta\theta s(1+o_{M}(1))}}\leq C^{|E|},

where
C
is some positive constant. Coming back to (
6.48
), we find denoting
m^{\prime}=|\mathcal{I}|
,

\displaystyle\lx@nobreakspace\Phi_{i}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E\subset\mathcal{I}\atop|E|\geq 1}(1-pq)^{|\mathcal{I}\setminus E|}(Cpq^{\frac{1}{r}})^{|E|}=(1-pq+Cpq^{\frac{1}{r}})^{m^{\prime}}-(1-pq)^{m^{\prime}}\leq Cm^{\prime}(1+Cpq^{\frac{1}{r}})^{m^{\prime}-1}pq^{\frac{1}{r}}.

We deduce that
\sup_{i\notin\mathcal{I}}\Phi_{i}(\theta)=o_{R}(1)p
uniformly in
|\mathcal{I}|\leq m
. This completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to give a proof of Lemma
6.19
.

Proof of Lemma
6.19
.

Let
\eta>0
and write
v=v^{\eta}+{v}_{\eta}
, where
v^{\eta}(i)=v_{i}{\bf 1}_{|v_{i}|>\eta}
,
for any
i\in\mathcal{J}
.
By Lemma
6.20
, we know that for any
t,\eta>0
,

(6.49)

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\sup_{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v^{\eta}\|>t\|v^{\eta}\|\big)\leq-\frac{t^{2}}{2\beta}.

We claim that similarly for any
t>0
,

(6.50)

\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\sup_{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}{v}_{\eta}\|>t\|{v}_{\eta}\|\big)\leq-\frac{t^{2}}{2\beta}.

Assume for the moment that the above claim is true. Observe that if we denote by
\hat{v}^{\eta}=v^{\eta}/\|v^{\eta}\|
and
\hat{v}_{\eta}=v_{\eta}/\|v_{\eta}\|
, then

(6.51)

\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|\leq\lx@nobreakspace\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v^{\eta}\|+\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v_{\eta}\|=\|v^{\eta}\|\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\hat{v}^{\eta}\|+\|v_{\eta}\|\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\hat{v}_{\eta}\|\leq\sqrt{\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\hat{v}^{\eta}\|^{2}+\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\hat{v}_{\eta}\|^{2}}.

Since
v^{\eta}
and
v_{\eta}
have disjoint supports,
\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\hat{v}^{\eta}
and
\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\hat{v}_{\eta}
are independent.
Using (
6.49
)–(
6.50
) and the contraction principle (see also Proposition
C.1
), (
6.47
) follows from (
6.51
).
Hence it remains to show (
6.50
). For any
z\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
we can write
z=x+y
where
x_{i}=z_{i}{\bf 1}_{|z_{i}|>M/\sqrt{np}}
, with
M=\sqrt{\log(1/p)/\eta}
.
This decomposition gives the bound

\lx@nobreakspace\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v_{\eta}\|\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{1/M^{2}}}\langle x,\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v_{\eta}\rangle+\sup_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{M/\sqrt{np}},y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\eta}}\langle y,{B}y^{\prime}\rangle.

By Lemma
6.9
, we know that for any
\delta>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{M/\sqrt{np}},y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\eta}}\langle y,{B}y^{\prime}\rangle>\delta\big)=-\infty.

Thus, it suffices to show that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\sup_{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{1/M^{2}}}\langle x,\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v_{\eta}\rangle>t\big)\leq-\frac{t^{2}}{2\beta}.

Using yet another net argument and a union bound we can reduce ourselves to prove that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\sup_{\mathcal{J}\subset[n]}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}^{c}},x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{J}}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\langle x,\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}x^{\prime}\rangle>t\big)\leq-\frac{t^{2}}{2\beta}.

Since the entries of
\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
are centered i.i.d. random variables, and for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{E}(e^{\lambda\widetilde{B}_{1,2}})\leq 1+\mathbb{E}(e^{\lambda G_{1,2}})\leq 2\mathbb{E}(e^{\lambda G_{1,2}})
repeating the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
6.18
we obtain the above bound. We omit the details. ∎

6.5.3.
Tail estimate of a degree in the network
H

In the next lemma, we give the large deviation upper tail rate function of the
\ell^{2}
-norm of a column of
H
.

Lemma 6.21
.

For any
k\in[n]
and
t>1
,

\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|{H}_{k}\|^{2}>t\big)\leq-h_{L}(t).

Proof.

For any
\theta\geq 0
define
\lx@nobreakspace\Lambda_{n,R}(\theta):=(np)^{-1}\mathbb{E}(\exp(np\theta\|{H}_{k}\|^{2}))
.
Using the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
3.4
, it follows that

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{n\to+\infty}\Lambda_{n,R}(\theta)=L_{R}(\theta)-1,

where
L_{R}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}(e^{\theta\widetilde{H}_{1,2}^{2}})
. Since
\lim_{R\to+\infty}L_{R}(\theta)=L(\theta)
the proof follows upon using Chernoff’s inequality by first letting
n\to+\infty
, followed by letting
R\to+\infty
, and then optimising over
\theta
.
∎

6.6.
Variational principle of the upper bound upper tail large deviation

In this section, we show that the large deviation upper tail rate function of
\lambda_{X}
can be expressed as the solution of a certain variational problem that we now describe.
Define for any
\lambda>2
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq 1
and
\mu>0

(6.52)

\Phi_{k,\lambda}(\mu):=\inf\Big\{\frac{r^{2}}{4}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{L}(d_{i})+\frac{t}{2\beta}:H_{\lambda}\big(r,t,\kappa,(d_{i})_{i\leq k}\big)\geq\mu,(r,t,\kappa,(d_{i})_{i\leq k})\in\mathcal{D}_{k}\Big\},

where
\mathcal{D}_{k}:=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\times[0,1]\times[1,+\infty)^{k}
, and
H_{\lambda}
is defined as

\lx@nobreakspace H_{\lambda}(r,t,\kappa,(d_{i})_{i\leq k}):=r\sqrt{\beta+\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\beta\big)\kappa^{2}}+m(\lambda)\kappa\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}(d_{i}-1)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}+m(\lambda)t.

The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 6.22
.

Fis any
\lambda>2
. Set
\Phi_{\lambda}:=\inf_{k\geq 1}\Phi_{k,\lambda}
. For any
t>0
we have

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\lambda_{X}\geq\lambda)\leq-\Phi_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda)-t),

Proof.

Let
\lambda>2
. By Proposition
6.4
it suffices to show that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\lambda_{Z^{R}}\geq\lambda)\leq-\Phi_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda)-t).

Observe that as
h_{L}
is finite everywhere (see Lemma
3.5
)
\Phi_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda))<+\infty
. By Lemma
6.7
there exists a
\chi(\lambda)>0
such that

(6.53)

\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}\leq\chi(\lambda),\lambda_{Z^{R}}\geq\lambda)\leq-\Phi_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda)).

For any
t>0
, define
\widehat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,R,t}:=\Omega_{\varepsilon,R,t\chi(\lambda)}
(recall (
6.24
))
Using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and dividing by
\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
, we obtain that on
{\widehat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,R,t}}\cap\{\|{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}>\chi(\lambda)\}

(6.54)

\lx@nobreakspace 1/m(\lambda)\leq\lx@nobreakspace\langle{\bm{\hat{}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}},Z_{J}{\bm{\hat{}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}}\rangle+m(\lambda)\|{\bm{\hat{}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}}\|_{4}^{2}\big(\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{J}}(\|(\widecheck{H}_{J})_{k}\|^{2}-1)_{+}^{2}\big)^{1/2}+m(\lambda)\|\widecheck{B}_{J}{\bm{\hat{}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}+t,

where
{\bm{\hat{}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}}:={\bm{v}^{\varepsilon}/\|{\bm{v}^{\varepsilon}}\|}
.
It is not clear so far that the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality used here is sharp. Since the two scenarios that we believe to lead the large deviation event are on the one hand a planted clique with diverging size, corresponding to
\|{\bm{\hat{}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}}\|_{4}=0
, and on the other a large degree vertex, corresponding to
\|{\bm{\hat{}}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}}\|_{4}=1
, we are at least not loosing any information when of one these two strategies are at play. To compute an upper bound on the probability of
\widehat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,R,t}
, we will first fix
J
,
\mathfrak{J}
and
\hat{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}
, and later take union bounds. To this end, let
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
,
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{J}
such that
|\mathcal{J}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}np/\log(1/p)
and
|\mathcal{I}|=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-2}\rfloor
, and
v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
. Define

\lx@nobreakspace Z_{R,\varepsilon}^{(1)}:=\frac{\langle v,Z_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle}{\sqrt{\beta+\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\beta\big)\kappa_{v}^{2}}},\ Z_{R,\varepsilon}^{(2)}:=\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}(\|(\widecheck{H}_{\mathcal{J}})_{k}\|^{2}-1)_{+}^{2}\Big)^{1/2},\ Z_{R,\varepsilon}^{(3)}:=\|\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|^{2},

where
\kappa_{v}:=\|v\|_{4}^{2}
.
Since
\|(\widecheck{H}_{\mathcal{J}})_{k}\|^{2}
,
k\in\mathcal{I}
are independent, and
h_{L}
is an increasing good rate function on
[1,+\infty)
, using Proposition
6.21
and a generalisation of the contraction principle (cf. Proposition
C.1
) we get

(6.55)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(Z^{(2)}_{R,\varepsilon}>t\big)\leq-\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-2}\rfloor}(t)\leq-K(t),

where
K_{\ell}(t):=\inf\{\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}h_{L}(d_{k}):\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}(d_{k}-1)^{2}\geq t,\forall 1\leq k\leq\ell,d_{k}\geq 1\}
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}

and
K:=\inf_{\ell\geq 1}K_{\ell}
.

As
Z_{\mathcal{J}}
,
(\widecheck{H}_{\mathcal{J}})_{k}
,
k\in\mathcal{I}
, and
\widecheck{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
are independent so are
Z_{R,\varepsilon}^{(k)}
,
k=1,2,3
. Therefore, by Propositions
6.18
,
6.19
, and (
6.55
), and Proposition
C.1
(applied with the positive part of
Z_{R,\varepsilon}^{(1)}
), we deduce that for any
\mu>0
,

\limsup_{R\to+\infty\atop\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(T_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{R,\varepsilon}({v})>\mu\big)\leq-\Phi_{\lambda}(\mu),

where

T_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{R,\varepsilon}({v}):=Z_{R,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\sqrt{\beta+\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\beta\big)\kappa_{v}^{2}}+m(\lambda)(\kappa_{v}Z_{2,R}+Z_{3,R})
.
Since
|\mathcal{J}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}np/\log(1/p)
, the map
\mathcal{I}\mapsto T_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{R,\varepsilon}(v)
is non decreasing function in the cardinality of
\mathcal{I}
, and
np\gg\log n
, using a net argument and a union bound we derive that for any
\mu>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{R\to+\infty\atop\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|\mathcal{J}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}np/\log(1/p)\atop|\mathcal{I}|\leq\varepsilon^{-2}}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}T_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{R}(v)>\mu\Big)\\
\leq\limsup_{R\to+\infty\atop\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}T_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{R,\varepsilon}(v)>\mu)\leq-\Phi_{\lambda}(\mu).

Combining the above estimate together with (
6.53
) and Lemma
6.6
, upon using (
6.54
) we get that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,R,t})\leq-\Phi_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda)-t).

By Proposition
6.11
for any
\delta>0
such that
\lambda>2+\delta
and
t>0
we further deduce that

(6.56)

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\lambda_{H^{(J)}}\leq\lambda-\delta\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_{Z^{R}})\leq-\Phi_{\lambda}\big(1/m(\lambda)-t\big).

Besides, using the result of Theorem
1.4
in the bounded case, and a union bound on
J
, we have for any
R,\varepsilon>0
,

(6.57)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\lambda_{H^{(J)}}>\lambda-\delta\big)\leq-I_{R}((\lambda-\delta)/\sigma_{R}),

where
I_{R}(\mu):=h_{L_{R}}(\mu/m(\mu))
,
\sigma_{R}^{2}=\mathbb{E}(H_{1,2}^{2})
, and
L_{R}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}(e^{\theta H_{1,2}^{2}/\sigma_{R}^{2}})
for any
\mu\geq 2
and
\theta\geq 0
. One can check that

\lx@nobreakspace\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{R\to+\infty}I_{R}((\lambda-\delta)/\sigma_{R})=h_{L}(\lambda/m(\lambda)).

Since
\Phi_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda)-t)\leq\Phi_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda))\leq\Phi_{1,\lambda}(1/m(\lambda))\leq h_{L}(\lambda/m(\lambda))
, the proof is complete by putting together (
6.56
)–(
6.57
).
∎

6.7.
Analysis of the optimisation problem

The goal of this section is to solve the optimisation problem given by the function
\Phi_{\lambda}
. We prove the following result.

Proposition 6.23
.

For any
\lambda>2
, and
0<\mu\leq 1/m(\lambda)
,

\lx@nobreakspace\Phi_{\lambda}(\mu)=\min\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{4\beta},\inf\big\{\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha}+h_{L}(1+d):r+m(\lambda)s\geq\mu:r,d\geq 0\big\}\Big).

This result is the last piece of the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
1.4
. Indeed, Proposition
6.23
yields in particular that
\Phi_{\lambda}
is lower semicontinuous as it is the minimum of two lower semicontinuous functions. Combining Propositions
6.23
and
6.22
, we get the claimed upper bound rate function
I
defined in Definition
1.3
. In a first step we start by optimising on the degrees
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
, i.e. the minimum is achieved by
\Phi_{1,\lambda}
(recall (
6.52
)), and show that the minimum is achieved in dimension 1, which accounts for the fact that the large deviation event is dominated by the emergence of at most atypically large degree.

Lemma 6.24
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\geq 0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace\inf\big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{L}(d_{i}):\sum_{i=1}^{k}(d_{i}-1)^{2}\geq s^{2},d_{i}\geq 1,1\leq i\leq k\big\}=h_{L}(1+s).

Proof.

First we claim that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\geq 0
,

(6.58)

\lx@nobreakspace\inf\big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{L}(d_{i}):\sum_{i=1}^{k}(d_{i}-1)^{2}\geq s^{2},d_{i}\geq 1,1\leq i\leq k\big\}=\inf_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\ell h_{L}\Big(1+\frac{s}{\sqrt{\ell}}\Big).

Since
h_{L}
is a good rate function, the infimum above is achieved at some vector
d^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
d^{*}=(d_{1}^{*},\ldots,d_{\ell}^{*},1,\ldots,1)
where
\ell\leq k
and
d_{i}^{*}>1
for any
i\leq\ell
. As
h_{L}(1)=0
the vector
(d_{1}^{*},\ldots,d_{\ell}^{*})
is a minimizer of the optimization problem (
6.58
) in dimension
\ell
which lies in the interior of the domain
[1,+\infty)^{\ell}
. By the multiplier’s rule (e.g.
[
27
, Theorem 9.1]
), there exists
(\gamma,\theta)\neq(0,0)
such that
\gamma\in\{0,1\}
,
\theta\leq 0
,

\theta\big(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}(d_{i}^{*}-1)^{2}-s^{2}\big)=0,\quad\text{ and }\quad\gamma h_{L}^{\prime}(d_{i}^{*})+2\theta(d_{i}^{*}-1)=0,\,i\in[\ell].

If
\gamma=0
, then as
\theta\neq 0
,
d^{*}_{i}=1
for any
i
, contradicting the definition of
d^{*}
. Therefore,
\gamma=1
. If
\theta=0
then
h_{L}^{\prime}(d^{*}_{i})=0
, which entails by Lemma
3.5
(e) that
d^{*}_{i}=1
for any
i
which is again contradictory. Thus
\theta\neq 0
. As a consequence
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}(d_{i}^{*}-1)^{2}=s^{2}
.
By Lemma
3.5
(e) the equation
h_{L}^{\prime}(d)=-2\theta(d-1)
can only have at most one solution strictly greater than
1
. Since
d^{*}_{i}>1
for
i\in[\ell]
, it follows that
d^{*}_{i}=d_{j}^{*}
for
i,j\in[\ell]
. As
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}(d_{i}^{*}-1)^{2}=s^{2}
, we deduce that
d^{*}_{i}=1+s/\sqrt{\ell}
, yielding the claim (
6.58
).

By (
6.58
) it now suffices to show that

(6.59)

\inf_{\ell\geq 1}\ell h_{L}\Big(1+\frac{s}{\sqrt{\ell}}\Big)=h_{L}(1+s).

This is straightforward. Indeed, fixing
\ell\geq 1
and denoting
f(s):=\ell h_{L}(1+s/\sqrt{\ell})-h_{L}(1+s)
,
s\geq 0
, we observe that
f(0)=0
, and by concavity of
h_{L}^{\prime}
and the fact that
h_{L}^{\prime}(1)=0
we have
f^{\prime}(s)=\sqrt{\ell}h_{L}^{\prime}(1+s/\sqrt{\ell})-h_{L}^{\prime}(1+s)\geq 0
. This yields (
6.58
) completing the proof.
∎

In the next step, we optimize on
\kappa
and show that the infimum is achieved either for
\kappa=1
or
\kappa=0
, corresponding respectively to the high degree scenario and the planted clique of diverging size.

Lemma 6.25
.

Define for any
\mu\geq 0
,

\widehat{\Phi}_{\lambda}(\mu):=\inf\big\{\frac{r^{2}}{4}+h_{L}(1+s):r\sqrt{\beta+\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\beta\big)\kappa^{2}}+m(\lambda)\kappa s\geq\mu,r,s\geq 0,\kappa\in[0,1]\big\}.

Then,

\lx@nobreakspace\widehat{\Phi}_{\lambda}(\mu)=\min\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{4\beta},\inf\big\{\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha}+h_{L}(1+s):r+m(\lambda)s\geq\mu:r,s\geq 0\big\}\Big).

Proof.

First observe that if
\beta\leq\alpha/2
then the function
\kappa\mapsto r\sqrt{\beta+(\alpha/2-\beta)\kappa^{2}}+m(\lambda)\kappa s
is increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and thus achieves its supremum for
\kappa=1
. The claim immediately follows as
h_{L}
is non decreasing on
[1,+\infty)
. Assume from now on that
\beta>\alpha/2
and
define for any
\kappa,s\geq 0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\varphi(\kappa,s):=\frac{(\mu-m(\lambda)\kappa s)^{2}}{4(\beta-\big(\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\kappa^{2})}+h_{L}(1+s).

With this notation
\widehat{\Phi}_{\lambda}(\mu)=\inf\big\{\varphi(\kappa,s):\kappa\in[0,1],0\leq m(\lambda)\kappa s\leq\mu\big\}
. We first show that we can restrict our attention to the set of parameters
(\kappa,s)
where
s\leq s_{\star}:=(\beta-\alpha/2)\mu/(\beta m(\lambda))
. Indeed, for any
\kappa\in[0,1]
and
s\geq 0
,

(6.60)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathrm{sg}\big(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\kappa}(\kappa,s)\big)=\mathrm{sg}\Big(\big(\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\frac{\kappa(\mu-m(\lambda)\kappa s)}{(\beta-\big(\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\kappa^{2}\big)}-m(\lambda)s\Big)=\mathrm{sg}\Big(\kappa\big(\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\mu-m(\lambda)s\beta\Big),

where we denote by
\mathrm{sg}
the sign of a real number. It yields that
\varphi(\cdot,s)
is non increasing on
[0,1]
for any
s\geq s_{\star}
, and as a consequence its infimum is achieved for
\kappa=1
.

This entails that it suffices to show that the infimum

\inf\big\{\varphi(\kappa,s):\kappa\in[0,1],0\leq s\leq s_{\star}\big\}
is achieved for
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
. To this end, for
\kappa\in[0,1]
let
s(\kappa)\in[0,s_{\star}]
be such that
\varphi(\kappa,\cdot)
, when viewed as a function on
[0,s_{\star}]
, is uniquely minimized at
s(\kappa)
. To show the existence of such a unique minima note that

\lx@nobreakspace\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial s}(\kappa,s)=h^{\prime}_{L}(1+s)-\frac{m(\lambda)\kappa}{2(\beta-\big(\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\kappa^{2})}(\mu-m(\lambda)\kappa s).

If
\kappa=0
, as
h_{L}^{\prime}
vanishes only at
1
and is non decreasing, and so the unique minima is at
s(0)=0
. Assume
\kappa\neq 0
. Then again using that
h_{L}^{\prime}
is non decreasing we find that
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa,.)
is strictly increasing, and thus the existence of a unique minima
s(\kappa)
follows. Moreover, as
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa,0)\leq 0
notice that

(6.61)

\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial s}(\kappa,s_{\star})\leq 0\Leftrightarrow s(\kappa)=s_{\star}\quad\text{ and }\quad s(\kappa)<s_{\star}\Rightarrow\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial s}(\kappa,s(\kappa))=0.

We next claim that the map
\kappa\mapsto s(\kappa)
is a non decreasing continuous function. The continuity follows from the fact that the map
\partial\varphi/\partial s
is continuous on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the characterization of
s(\kappa)
given in (
6.61
), and the fact that
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa,\cdot)
is strictly increasing for
\kappa>0
. To derive the non decreasing property of
s(\kappa)
observe that for any
\kappa\in[0,1]
and
s\geq 0
, we have
\mathrm{sg}((\partial^{2}\varphi/\partial\kappa\partial s)(\kappa,s))=-\mathrm{sg}(\psi(s,\kappa))
,
where
\psi(s,\kappa):=\big(\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\mu\kappa^{2}+(\mu-2m(\lambda)s\kappa)\beta
. Thus, for any
s\leq s_{\star}
and
\kappa\in[0,1]
,

\lx@nobreakspace\psi(\kappa,s)\geq\psi(\kappa,s_{\star})=\mu\big((\beta-\alpha/2)(\kappa^{2}-2\kappa)+\beta\big)\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}\geq 0,

showing that
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\cdot,s)
is non increasing. Now fix
\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. If
s(\kappa)=s_{\star}
then by (
6.61
), and as
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\cdot,s)
is non increasing, we obtain that
s(\kappa^{\prime})=s_{\star}
. On the other hand, if
s(\kappa)<s_{\star}
and
s(\kappa^{\prime})<s(\kappa)
then, using that
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa^{\prime},\cdot)
is strictly increasing, (
\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\cdot,s(\kappa))
is non increasing, and (
6.61
) we get

0=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial s}(\kappa^{\prime},s(\kappa^{\prime}))<\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial s}(\kappa^{\prime},s(\kappa))\leq\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial s}(\kappa,s(\kappa))=0,

yielding a contradiction. Observe that the above argument also gives a
\kappa_{\star}\in(0,1]
such that
s(\kappa)<s_{\star}
for
\kappa<\kappa_{\star}
and otherwise
s(\kappa)=s_{\star}
.

As
h_{L}^{\prime}
is differentiable, except potentially at
x_{\star}
and
h_{L}^{\prime}
is non decreasing (see Lemma
3.5
), we obtain that
(\partial^{2}\varphi/\partial s^{2})(\kappa,s)>0
for any
s\neq x_{\star}-1
and
\kappa\in[0,1]
. Besides, for any
s\geq 0
the equation
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa,s)=0
has at most two solutions in
\kappa
. Denoting
\mathcal{K}
to be the set of
\kappa
such that
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa,x_{\star}-1)=0
, by the implicit function theorem it follows that
\kappa\mapsto s(\kappa)
is differentiable on
(0,\kappa_{\star})\setminus\mathcal{K}
, and hence so is the map
\kappa\mapsto\Psi(\kappa):=\varphi(\kappa,s(\kappa))
. Furthermore

(6.62)

\lx@nobreakspace\Psi^{\prime}(\kappa)=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\kappa}(\kappa,s(\kappa))+s^{\prime}(\kappa)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial s}(\kappa,s(\kappa))=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\kappa}(\kappa,s(\kappa)),

where we used that
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa,s(\kappa))=0
for
\kappa<\kappa_{\star}
and trivially
s^{\prime}(\kappa)=0
for
\kappa\in(\kappa_{\star},1)\setminus\mathcal{K}
. Therefore by the first equality in (
6.60
) and (
6.62
), for any
\kappa\in[0,1]\setminus(\mathcal{K}\cup\{\kappa_{\star}\})
,

(6.63)

\mathrm{sg}(\Psi^{\prime}(\kappa))=\mathrm{sg}\Big(2\big(\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)h_{L}^{\prime}(1+s(\kappa))-m(\lambda)^{2}s(\kappa)\Big).

Using that
h_{L}^{\prime}
is concave by Lemma
3.5
(e) and the facts that
\kappa\mapsto s(\kappa)
is non decreasing and that
(\partial\varphi/\partial s)(\kappa,s(\kappa))\leq 0
for
\kappa\geq\kappa_{\star}
we deduce that the LHS of (
6.63
) is either nonpositive on
[0,1]\setminus\mathcal{K}
or nonnegative on
[0,\kappa_{\star\star}]\setminus\mathcal{K}
and nonpositive otherwise, for some
\kappa_{\star\star}\in(0,1]
. Since
\#\mathcal{K}
is
O(1)
, and
\kappa\mapsto\Psi(\kappa)
is continuous we find that either it is nonincreasing or it is nondecreasing on
[0,\kappa_{\star\star}]
and then nonincreasing. In both these cases
\inf\{\Psi(\kappa):\kappa\in[0,1]\}=\Psi(0)\wedge\Psi(1)
. This ends the proof. ∎

Finally, we add the linear term
t/(2\beta)
to the optimisation problem and show that the infimum is achieved for
t=0
. This will prove that the
\|\widecheck{B}_{J}{\bm{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|
term in the exponential equivalent of Proposition
6.11
leads indeed to a sub optimal large deviation scenario.

Lemma 6.26
.

For any
\mu\geq 0
,
\lambda\geq 2
.

(6.64)

\lx@nobreakspace\widehat{\Phi}_{\lambda}(1/m(\lambda))=\inf\big\{\widehat{\Phi}_{\lambda}(\mu)+\frac{t}{2\beta}:\mu+m(\lambda)t\geq 1/m(\lambda)\big\}.

Proof.

We will prove that on the one hand for any
w\geq 0
,

(6.65)

\lx@nobreakspace\inf\big\{h_{L}(1+s)+\frac{t}{2\beta}:s+t\geq w\big\}=h_{L}(1+w).

and on the other hand

(6.66)

\inf\big\{\frac{\mu^{2}}{4\beta}+\frac{\nu}{2\beta}:\mu+m(\lambda)\nu\geq 1/m(\lambda)\big\}=\frac{1}{4\beta m(\lambda)^{2}}.

Once these two claims are proven, it follows immediately by Lemma
6.25
that the infimum in (
6.64
) is achieved for
t=0
.

Now observe that as
h_{L}^{\prime}
is non decreasing and
\lim_{x\to+\infty}h_{L}^{\prime}(x)=1/(2\beta)
by Lemma
3.5
, the function
s\mapsto h_{L}(1+s)+(w-s)/(2\beta)
is non increasing for any
w\geq 0
. This yields (
6.65
). The second claim (
6.66
) follows from the fact that
\mu\mapsto\mu^{2}/(4\beta)+(1-m(\lambda)\mu)/(2\beta m(\lambda)^{2})
is non increasing on
[0,1/m(\lambda)]
.
∎

Proposition
6.23
is now obvious from Lemmas
6.24
,
6.25
, and
6.26
. We omit the details.

7.
Local laws for supercritical sparse Wigner matrices

In this section we provide the proofs of Propositions
3.2
and
5.11
, and Lemma
6.15
. A key to these proofs is an isotropic local law for supercritical Wigner matrices under Assumption
1.2
and in the whole regime
np\gg\log n
. Before stating our results we introduce the following notation.
Fixing
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1/2)
, we let

(7.1)

\Upsilon_{n}:=\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{(np)\wedge n^{\varepsilon_{0}}}}.

Set
\mathbb{H}:=\{z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}:{\rm Im}\,z^{\prime}>0\}
and for any
\kappa,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{H}_{\kappa}:=\{z\in\mathbb{H}:|{\rm Re}\,z|\leq\kappa^{-1}\text{ and }|{\rm Re}\,z\pm 2|\geq\kappa\},\quad\mathbb{H}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}:=\mathbb{H}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}^{n}:=\{z\in\mathbb{H}_{\kappa}:{\rm Im}\,z\in[n^{-1+\varepsilon},\kappa^{-1}]\}.

Fixing a sequence
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive numbers such that
\lim_{n\to+\infty}s_{n}=0
and
s_{n}\geq n^{-1+\varepsilon}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and a numerical constant
\mathfrak{C}>0
we further define

\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}:=\big\{z\in\mathbb{H}_{\kappa}:s_{n}\vee((np)\wedge n^{\varepsilon})^{-\mathfrak{C}}\leq{\rm Im}\,z\leq\kappa^{-1}\big\},\quad\mathcal{U}_{n}:=\big\{u\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}:\|u\|_{1}\leq s_{n}np\big\}.

For
W\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
z\in\mathbb{H}\cup(\mathbb{R}\setminus{\rm Spec}(W))
we define
\mathcal{R}(z,W):=(z-W)^{-1}
.
When the choice of
z
and/or
W
will be clear from the context we will suppress the dependencies of these variables in the notation
\mathcal{R}(\cdot,\cdot)
. For
\mathbb{T}\subset[n]
,
W\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, and
z\in\mathbb{H}\cup(\mathbb{R}\setminus{\rm Spec}(W))
we further let
\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{T})}:=\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{T})}(z,W):=(z-W^{(\mathbb{T})})^{-1}
.

To reach the isotropic local law for supercritical Wigner matrices we first prove the following entry wise local law.

Theorem 7.1

(Entry wise local law)
.

Let
p
be such that
\log n\ll np\ll n
. Fix
\kappa\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in(2\varepsilon_{0},1)
. Then under Assumption
1.2
, for any
M>0
there exists a constant
C<+\infty
such that

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}}\max_{\mathbb{T}\subset[n]:|\mathbb{T}|\lesssim 1}\max_{i,j\in[n]\setminus\mathbb{T}}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{i,j}(z,X)-m(z)\updelta_{i,j}|\geq C\Upsilon_{n}\bigg)\leq n^{-M},

for all large
n
. Moreover, the same result holds for
{\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np}
instead of
X
.

Building on Theorem
7.1
one can prove the following result.

Theorem 7.2

(Isotropic local law)
.

Under the same setup of Theorem
7.1
we have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\big|\mathbb{E}\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z,X)u\rangle-m(z)\big|=0.

Moreover, the same result holds for
{\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np}
instead of
X
.

As already mentioned in Section
2.2
the results available in literature do not yield Theorems
7.1
and
7.2
in the generality we need. These two results are proved using adaptations of the general strategies developed in
[
25
]
and
[
38
]
together with a careful counting argument.

Remark 7.3
.

A couple more comments regarding the isotropic local law result in Theorem
7.2
are in order. The reader can note from its proof that under the additional assumption that the entries of
G
have a symmetric distribution on
\mathbb{R}
the result would continue to hold for any
u\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
such that
\|u\|_{1}\leq(np)^{O(1)}
, and thus for
p
such that
\log(np)\gtrsim\log n
one does not need any upper bound
\|u\|_{1}
for the isotropic local law to hold. The proof will also indicate that it is amenable to yield a bound on
\mathbb{E}(|\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z)u\rangle-m(z)|^{2k})
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since we do not need such a bound we have not pursued it here.

7.1.
Proofs of Propositions
3.2
and
5.11
, and Lemma
6.15

In this section we use Theorem
7.2
to prove these results.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

We prove the required result only for
X
. The proof for
{\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np}
being identical is omitted. Let
\lambda>2
and
u\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Recall that
f_{\lambda,u}(K)=\langle u,(\lambda-K)^{-1}u\rangle
for
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\lambda}
. Fix
\delta>0
such that
\lambda\geq 2+2\delta
.
As
f_{\lambda,u}
is convex and
\delta^{-2}
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt norm on
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{2+\delta}
, there exists an extension
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}
of
f_{\lambda,u}
to
\mathcal{H}_{n}
such that
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}
is convex and
\delta^{-2}
-Lipschitz. Namely,
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(K^{\prime})=\sup\{f_{\lambda,u}(K)+\langle\nabla f_{\lambda,u}(K),K^{\prime}-K\rangle:K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{2+\delta}\}
for
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. In a first step we show that

(7.2)

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\big|\mathbb{E}\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)-m(\lambda)\big|=o(1).

Let
z=\lambda+{\rm i}\upeta
, where
\upeta=(\log\log n)^{-1}\vee s_{n}
implying that
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
. Since
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(K)=f_{\lambda,u}(K)
for any
K\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\delta}_{n}
, by triangle inequality we get that

(7.3)

\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)\big]-m(\lambda)\big|\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)\big|{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{E}^{c}}\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z,X)u\rangle\big|{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{E}^{c}}\big]+|m(z)-m(\lambda)|\\
+\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z,X)u\rangle-\langle u,\mathcal{R}(\lambda,X)u\rangle\big|{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{E}}\big]+\big|\mathbb{E}\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z,X)u\rangle-m(z)\big|,

where
\mathcal{E}:=\{X\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\delta}_{n}\}
. The third and fourth terms on the right-hand side of the above inequality are bounded by
\delta^{-2}\upeta
, while the last term goes to
0
as
n\to+\infty
by Theorem
7.2
uniformly in
u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
. For the two first terms, note that on the one hand as
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}
is convex and
\delta^{-2}
-Lipschitz and
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(0)=f_{\lambda,u}(0)=\lambda^{-1}
,
0
being the zero matrix, for any
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{2+\delta}
, we have

\lx@nobreakspace\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(K)\leq\lx@nobreakspace\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(0)+\delta^{-2}\|K\|_{2}=\lambda^{-1}+\delta^{-2}\|K\|_{2}.

We deduce by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\mathbb{E}\|X\|_{2}^{2}=n
, that
\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)\big|{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{E}^{c}}\big]\leq\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c})+\delta^{-2}\sqrt{n}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c})^{1/2}
. We claim that, for any
C>0
,

(7.4)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\log n}\log\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c})\leq-C.

Since
np\gg\log n
the bound (
7.4
) implies that

\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)\big|{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{E}^{c}}\big]=o(1)
uniformly in
u\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. On the other hand,
|\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z,X)u\rangle|\leq\upeta^{-1}
, so that by our choice of
\upeta
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z,X)u\rangle\big|{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{E}^{c}}\big]=o(1)
uniformly in
u\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

Thus to complete the proof of (
7.2
) we need to derive (
7.4
). Recall the matrices
A^{R}
and
B^{R}
from Section
6.1
. Since the entries of
A^{R}
are bounded, from
[
24
, Example 8.7]
,
[
15
, Theorem 2.7]
, and the fact that
\mathbb{E}(A_{1,2}^{R})^{2}=1+o_{R}(1)
, it follows that

(7.5)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}(\|A^{R}\|\geq 2+\delta/2)<0,

for
R
large enough.
By (
6.10
) and the integration by parts formula, for any
\theta>0
sufficiently small, we derive

(7.6)

\mathbb{E}\left[\|B_{i}^{R}\|^{N}\right]\leq\delta_{*}^{N}+\int_{\delta_{*}}^{+\infty}Nt^{N-1}\exp(-np\theta t^{2}+o_{R}(1)np)dt\\
\leq\delta_{*}^{N}+\int_{0}^{+\infty}Nt^{N-1}\exp(-np\theta t^{2}/2)dt\leq\delta_{*}^{N}+\sqrt{2\pi N}\left(\frac{N}{\theta np}\right)^{N/2},

for any
\delta_{*}>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and all large
R
(depending on
\delta_{*}
). By a symmetrisation argument and Seginer’s theorem
[
47
, Theorem 1.1]
we have
\mathbb{E}[\|B^{R}\|^{\log n}]\leq n\widetilde{C}^{\log n}\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\|B_{i}^{R}\|^{\log n}
, where
\widetilde{C}<+\infty
is some numerical constant. Therefore, as
np\gg\log n
, by (
7.6
) and Markov’s inequality

(7.7)

\limsup_{R\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\log n}\log\mathbb{P}(\|B^{R}\|\geq\delta/2)\leq-\log(\delta/2)+\log(\delta_{*})+\log(e\widetilde{C})\leq\frac{1}{2}\log(\delta_{*}),

where the last step follows upon choosing
\delta_{*}
sufficiently small (depending on
\delta
). Since
\delta_{*}>0
is can be chosen to be arbitrarily small the bound (
7.4
) follows from (
7.5
) and (
7.7
).

We now move on to prove (
3.22
).
Since
(G_{i,j}\xi_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
are independent sub-Gaussian random variables with uniformly bounded sub-Gaussian constants, by
[
39
, Theorem 1.3]
, there exists
c>0
such that for any
t>\sqrt{\log n/(cnp\delta^{4})}

(7.8)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)]\big|\geq t\Big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{c\delta^{4}t^{2}np}{\log n}\Big).

Using (
7.2
), we deduce that for any
t>0
and
\lambda\geq 2+2\delta
, and
n
large enough,

(7.9)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|{f}_{\lambda,u}(X)-m(\lambda)\big|\geq t,\lambda_{X}\leq 2+\delta\Big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{c\delta^{4}t^{2}np}{\log n}\Big),\quad u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}.

To obtain (
3.22
) from (
7.8
) we follow the following two step strategy:
As
\lambda\in(2+2\delta,+\infty)\mapsto f_{\lambda,u}(X)
and
m
are
\delta^{-2}
-Lipschitz on the event where
\lambda_{X}\leq 2+\delta
, using a net argument and a union bound (and possibly shrinking the constant
c
) we obtain (
3.22
) with
\sup_{\lambda\geq 2+2\delta}
replaced by
\sup_{\lambda\in(2+2\delta,2+2\delta+4t^{-1})}
.

In a second step we observe that on
\{\lambda_{X}\leq 2+\delta\}
we have
|f_{\lambda,u}(X)|\vee m(\lambda)\leq t/4
for any
\lambda\geq 2+\delta+4t^{-1}
. This together with the first step and a triangle inequality proves (
3.22
). ∎

Proof of Proposition
5.11
.

Recall the definition of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}
from (
5.11
).
Let
\delta\in(0,1)
such that
\lambda>2+2\delta
. We show in a first step that

(7.10)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\big[{\bf 1}_{\{\|X^{(\mathcal{J})}\|>2+\delta\}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\big]=o(1)(\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+1).

We claim that for any
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
,

(7.11)

\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(K^{\prime})\leq\lambda^{-1}\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+\|X_{\mathcal{J}}\|+r\|K^{\prime}\|_{2}.

Indeed, let
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
,
K\in E_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,r}
and
\zeta\in\Theta(K)
. This means that there exists
v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K)=\varphi_{\lambda}(K)
and
\zeta=\nabla\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K)\in r\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
. As
\varphi_{\lambda,v}
is convex,
\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K)\leq\varphi_{\lambda,v}(0)+\langle\zeta,K\rangle\leq\lambda^{-1}\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+\|X_{\mathcal{J}}\|+\langle\zeta,K\rangle
. Since
\varphi_{\lambda,v}(K)=\varphi_{\lambda}(K)
and
\|\zeta\|_{2}\leq r
, this gives the claimed bound (
7.11
). As the entries of
G
are bounded, by
[
24
, Example 8.7]
and
[
15
, Theorem 2.7]
, we obtain

(7.12)

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\|\widecheck{X}^{(\mathcal{J})}\|>2+\delta\big)<0.

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
\mathbb{E}\|X^{(\mathcal{J})}\|_{2}^{2}=O(n)
and the fact that
np\gg\log n
, we further get

(7.13)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\big[\lx@nobreakspace{\bf 1}_{\{\|X^{(\mathcal{J})}\|>2+\delta\}}\|X^{(\mathcal{J})}\|_{2}\big]=o(1).

Besides, as
\mathcal{J}
is of bounded size,
\|X_{\mathcal{J}}\|=o(1)
. Therefore, by (
7.11
)-(
7.13
) the bound (
7.10
) follows.
In a second step, we will prove that

(7.14)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\big[{\bf 1}_{\{\|X^{(\mathcal{J})}\|\leq 2+\delta\}}\psi_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\big]\leq m(\lambda)\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}(1+o(1)),

where for
K\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\delta}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}

\lx@nobreakspace\psi_{\lambda,v}(K):=\langle\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v,(\lambda-K)^{-1}\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v\rangle,\text{ for }v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}},\text{ and }\psi_{\lambda}(K):=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}\lx@nobreakspace\psi_{\lambda,v}(K).

As
\mathcal{J}
is of bounded size and the entries of
G
are bounded, on
\Omega_{\mathcal{J}}:=\{\|X^{(\mathcal{J})}\|\leq 2+\delta\}
we have
\varphi_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\leq\psi_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})+o(1)
. Since
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(K)\leq\varphi_{\lambda}(K)
for any
K\in\mathcal{H}^{\lambda}_{n}
we note that (
7.10
) and (
7.14
) yield the required result.

We now move on to the proof of (
7.14
). Since
\psi_{\lambda,v}
is convex and
\delta^{-2}\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt norm on
\mathcal{H}^{2+\delta}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
, there exists
\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda,v}
a convex extension of
{\psi}_{\lambda,v}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
that is also
\delta^{-2}\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt norm.
By
[
4
, Proposition 4.2]
, for any
t>0
, almost surely on
\Omega_{C}
we have

(7.15)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\Big({\psi}_{\lambda,v}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})-\sup_{v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda,v^{\prime}}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>t,X^{(\mathcal{J})}\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\delta}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\Big)\\
\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\big(\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda,v}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda,v}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>t\big)\leq e^{-\frac{t^{2}\delta^{4}np}{32(CR)^{2}}},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
denotes the probability with respect to the randomness of
X^{(\mathcal{J})}
.
Let
\kappa>0
such that
\#\mathcal{J}\leq\kappa
almost surely and set
\eta:=(np)^{-1/(4\kappa)}\ll 1
. Note that when
X^{(\mathcal{J})}\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\delta}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}
the map
v\mapsto\psi_{\lambda,v}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})
is
2\delta^{-1}\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}
-Lipschitz with respect to the
\ell^{2}
-norm.
By
[
3
, Corollary 4.1.15]
, there exists a
\delta\eta/2
–net for the
\ell^{2}
-norm of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
of size at most
(6(\delta\eta)^{-1})^{\kappa}
. By a union bound it follows that for any
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\Big({\psi}_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})-\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda,v}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})>t+\eta\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2},X^{(\mathcal{J})}\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\delta}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\Big)\leq\big(6(\delta\eta)^{-1}\big)^{\kappa}e^{-\frac{t^{2}\delta^{4}np}{32(CR)^{2}}},

almost surely on
\Omega_{C}
.
Integrating this inequality, by our choice of
\eta
,

(7.16)

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\lx@nobreakspace\big[{\bf 1}_{\{\|X^{(\mathcal{J})}\|\leq 2+\delta\}}\psi_{\lambda}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\big]\leq\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda,v}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})+o(1)(\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}+1),\quad\text{a.s.\penalty\ on $\Omega_{C}$}.

On the other hand note that on
\Omega_{C}
,

|\mathrm{supp}(\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v)|\leq Cnp
, and thus
\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{Cnp}\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}v\|_{2}
for any
v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}
. Therefore using (
7.2
) we get, a.s. on
\Omega_{C}
,

\lx@nobreakspace\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{J}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda,v}(X^{(\mathcal{J})})\leq m(\lambda)\|\widecheck{X}_{\mathcal{J}}\|^{2}(1+o(1)).

This together with (
7.16
) yields (
7.14
) completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.15
.

Applying Proposition
4.1
and using the same argument as in the proof of (
5.13
) we obtain that for any
t>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{np}\log\sup_{x\in 2\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathbb{P}\big(\widetilde{f}^{\varepsilon,r}_{\lambda,x}(Y)-\mathbb{E}\widetilde{f}^{\varepsilon,r}_{\lambda,x}(Y)>t\big)=-\infty.

Next note that for any
\varepsilon,r>0
,

\widetilde{f}^{\varepsilon,r}_{\lambda,x}\leq\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x}
, where
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x}
is as in the proof of Proposition
3.2
. Thus, by (
7.2
),
\mathbb{E}\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x}^{\varepsilon,r}(Y)\leq\mathbb{E}\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,x}(Y)\leq m(\lambda)\|x\|^{2}(1+o(1))
, for all large
n
. This completes the proof.
∎

7.2.
Proof of Theorem
7.2

The proof of Theorem
7.2
requires the following concentration bounds. Recall
\Upsilon_{n}
from (
7.1
). We recall also that the sub-Gaussian constant of a real random variable
Y
is defined as
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\{t>0:\mathbb{E}(e^{tY^{2}})\leq 1\}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
be a sequence of centered i.i.d. sub-Gaussian random variables and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
be a sequence of i.i.d.
\dBer(p)
random variables. Set
Y_{i}:=Z_{i}\xi_{i}/\sqrt{np}
,
i\in[n]
. Let
\{\widetilde{Y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
be an independent copy of
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Fix
\varepsilon\in(2\varepsilon_{0},1)
,
M>0
, and
C_{0}<+\infty
.

(a)

For any
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
deterministic with

\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|a_{i}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leq n^{-\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\max_{i\in[n]}|a_{i}|}\leq C_{0},

there exists some constant
C<+\infty
depending only the sub-Gaussian norm of
Z_{1}
,
C_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{0}
, and
M
such that, for all large
n
,

(7.17)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}Y_{i}\Big|\geq C\Upsilon_{n}\Big)\leq n^{-M}.

(b)

Let
np\gtrsim\log n
. For any
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
deterministic, bounded in moduli by
C_{0}
, there exists some constant
C<+\infty
depending only the sub-Gaussian norm of
Z_{1}
,
C_{0}
, and
M
such that, for all large
n
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(|Y_{i}|^{2}-\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{2})\Big|\geq C\Upsilon_{n}\Big)\leq n^{-M}.

(c)

For any deterministic
a_{i,j},i,j\in[n]
with

\Big(\max_{i}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|a_{i,j}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\bigvee\Big(\max_{j}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|a_{i,j}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leq n^{-\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\max_{i,j\in[n]}|a_{i,j}|}\leq C_{0},

there exists some constant
C<+\infty
depending only the sub-Gaussian norm of
Z_{1}
,
C_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
M
such that, for all large
n
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}Y_{i}\widetilde{Y}_{j}\Big|\geq C\Upsilon_{n}^{2}\Big)\leq n^{-M},\quad\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\sum_{i\neq j\in[n]}a_{i,j}Y_{i}Y_{j}\Big|\geq C\Upsilon_{n}^{2}\Big)\leq n^{-M}.

Proof.

We consider only the case
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R},i\in[n]
. The proof for the complex case does not require any new ideas except for the fact that it is notationally heavy compared to its real counterpart. So we omit it.
Set
\gamma:=n^{-\varepsilon}
and
\psi:=C_{0}/\sqrt{np}
.
We will prove that for any
r\in 2\mathbb{N}

(7.18)

\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}Y_{i}\Big\|_{r}\lesssim\bigg(\frac{r}{1+2\log_{+}(\psi/\gamma)}\bigvee 1\bigg)\cdot(\gamma\vee\psi)\cdot\sqrt{r}.

Observe that, for
r\asymp\log n
, the first term in the RHS of (
7.18
) can be trivially bounded by a
O(\log n)
term, while for
np\leq n^{2\varepsilon_{0}}
it is
O(1)
. Therefore, for
r\asymp\log n
, we deduce that
\|\sum_{i}a_{i}Y_{i}\|_{r}\lesssim\Upsilon_{n}
. Using this together with Markov’s inequality, applied with
r\asymp\log n
, we obtain (
7.17
).

Turning to prove (
7.18
) we let
\mathfrak{P}(r)
to be the set of partitions of
[r]
and
\mathfrak{P}_{\geq 2}(r)\subset\mathfrak{P}(r)
to be the subset that consists of partitions with all blocks having size at least two. For any
\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}(r)
we write
|\Pi|
to denote the number of blocks in
\Pi
. For a block
\pi\in\Pi
we use
|\pi|
to denote the number of elements in that block. Using the fact that
\mathbb{E}Y_{i}=0
we have that

\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}Y_{i}\Big\|_{r}^{r}=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}=1}^{n}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{r}a_{i_{j}}\Big)\mathbb{E}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{r}Y_{i_{j}}\Big)\leq\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{\geq 2}(r)}\sum_{s\in[n]^{|\Pi|}}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}|a_{s_{\pi}}|^{|\pi|}\mathbb{E}|Y_{s_{\pi}}|^{|\pi|}.

Using the fact that
Y_{i}=Z_{i}\xi_{i}/\sqrt{np}
, where
Z_{i}
is a sub-Gaussian random variable and
\xi_{i}
is a
\dBer(p)
random variable, for any
\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{\geq 2}(r)
we find that

(7.19)

\sum_{s\in[n]^{|\Pi|}}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}|a_{s_{\pi}}|^{|\pi|}\mathbb{E}|Y_{s_{\pi}}|^{|\pi|}\leq\mathbb{E}|Y_{1}|^{r}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{s\in[n]}|a_{s}|^{|\pi|}\\
\leq\|Y_{1}\|_{\psi_{2}}^{r}r^{r/2}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\Big(\sum_{s\in[n]}|a_{s}|^{2}\Big)\big(\max_{s\in[n]}|a_{s}|\big)^{|\pi|-2}\frac{1}{n(np)^{\frac{1}{2}|\pi|-1}}\leq\|Y_{1}\|_{\psi_{2}}^{r}r^{r/2}\gamma^{2|\Pi|}\psi^{r-2|\Pi|},

where the first step uses Hölder’s inequality. We point out to the reader that in the case when
\sqrt{np}Y_{i}
is a centred
\dBer(p)
random variable a bound analogous to (
7.19
), without the factor
r^{r/2}
was obtained in the proof of
[
38
, Proposition 3.1]
. Therefore repeating the rest of the arguments of that proof one obtains (
7.18
).

To prove part (c) one is required to do similar adaptations of those of
[
38
, Proposition 3.3]
and
[
38
, Proposition 3.4]
to obtain

(7.20)

\Big\|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}Y_{i}\widetilde{Y}_{j}\Big\|_{r}\lesssim\bigg(\frac{r}{1+2\log_{+}(\widetilde{\psi}/\gamma)}\bigvee 1\bigg)^{2}\cdot(\gamma\vee\widetilde{\psi})\cdot{r}.

and

(7.21)

\Big\|\sum_{i\neq j\in[n]}a_{i,j}Y_{i}Y_{j}\Big\|_{r}\lesssim\bigg(\frac{r}{1+2\log_{+}(\widetilde{\psi}/\gamma)}\bigvee 1\bigg)^{2}\cdot(\gamma\vee\widetilde{\psi})\cdot{r},

for any
r\in 2\mathbb{N}
, where
\widetilde{\psi}:=C_{0}/np
. These bounds together with Markov’s inequality yield the desired results. Further details are omitted. Finally, part (b) follows from
[
51
, Theorem 3.1]
and the fact that
np\gtrsim\log n
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

First we prove the required result for
X
.
To ease the notation, we drop the dependency of
\mathcal{R}(z,X)
in
X
, and write
\mathcal{R}(z)
instead, and similarly for
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{T}}(z)
, where
\mathbb{T}\subset[n]
.
We begin by noting that

(7.22)

\mathbb{E}\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z)u\rangle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i\in[n]}u_{i}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z)\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i\neq j\in[n]}u_{i}u_{j}\mathcal{R}_{i,j}(z)\Big].

Since
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
implies that
\upeta:={\rm Im}\,z\geq n^{-1}
, using the trivial bound
\|\mathcal{R}(z)\|\leq\upeta^{-1}
and applying Theorem
7.1
, as
u\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we find that

(7.23)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i\in[n]}u_{i}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z)\Big]\to m(z),\qquad\text{ as }n\to+\infty,

uniformly for
u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
. Next for
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[n]
we let
\mathbb{I}:=\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\}
and set

\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}:=\bigg\{\max_{i,j\in[n]\setminus\mathbb{I}}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{i,j}(z)-m(z)\updelta_{i,j}|\leq C\Upsilon_{n}\bigg\},

where
C
is as in Theorem
7.1
with
M=4\mathfrak{f}
, and
\mathfrak{f}
some large integer to be determined below. By Theorem
7.1
, as
\|u\|_{1}\leq n^{1/2}
and
\|\mathcal{R}(z)\|\leq\upeta^{-1}
we observe that

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},i_{2}}(z)\mathcal{R}_{i_{3},i_{4}}(z){\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}^{c}}\Big]\bigg|\leq n^{4}\max_{\mathbb{I}}\mathbb{P}(\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}^{c})\to 0

as
n\to+\infty
.
Hence, by (
7.22
), (
7.23
), and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality it suffices to show that

(7.24)

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},i_{2}}(z)\mathcal{R}_{i_{3},i_{4}}(z){\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]\to 0,

as
n\to+\infty
. To compute the sum in the RHS of (
7.24
) we split the sum according to
|\mathbb{I}|
. Observe that, when the sum is restricted over all those indices for which
|\mathbb{I}|=2
the claim (
7.24
) is immediate due to Theorem
7.1
. So for the rest of the proof we will focus only on summing those indices for which
|\mathbb{I}|>2
.

Fix
i_{1}\neq i_{2},i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]
. Using the Schur complement formula, and the fact that
m(z)+m(z)^{-1}=z
we obtain

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}}=(\mathsf{D}-\mathsf{E})^{-1},

where
\mathsf{D}:=\mathsf{D}(z):=m(z)^{-1}{\rm Id}-\mathsf{E}_{1}
,
\mathsf{E}:=\mathsf{E}_{2}+\mathsf{E}_{3}
,
