\mathsf{E}_{1}:=(m_{n}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z)-m(z)){\rm Id},\quad\mathsf{E}_{2}:=X_{\mathbb{I}},\quad\mathsf{E}_{3}:=\widecheck{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{\sf T}\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}\widecheck{X}_{\mathbb{I}}-m_{n}^{(\mathbb{I})}{\rm Id},

and
m_{n}^{(\mathbb{I})}:=m_{n}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z):=n^{-1}{\rm Tr}\,\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z)
. Note that, as
np\gg\log n
we have
\Upsilon_{n}\ll 1
, and
|m(z)|\asymp 1
for any
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
(see
[
32
, Lemma 3.2]
) we obtain that
\mathsf{D}
is invertible on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
for
n
large enough. Therefore, by the resolvent expansion, on the same event, upon writing
\widehat{\mathsf{E}}:=\mathsf{D}^{-1}\mathsf{E}
, we obtain that

(7.25)

\mathcal{R}=\mathsf{D}^{-1}+\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2},

where

(7.26)

\mathcal{R}_{1}:=\mathcal{R}_{1}(z):=\sum_{a=1}^{\mathfrak{f}}\widehat{\mathsf{E}}^{a}\mathsf{D}^{-1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{R}_{2}:=\mathcal{R}_{2}(z):=\widehat{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathfrak{f}+1}(\mathsf{D}-\mathsf{E})^{-1},

for some
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbb{N}
to be determined below. Notice that
\mathsf{D}^{-1}=\widehat{m}(z){\rm Id}
, where
\widehat{m}(z):=m(z)/(1-(m_{n}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z)-m(z))m(z))
. On the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
we find that
|\widehat{m}(z)|\asymp 1
(use that
|m(z)|\asymp 1
) for
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
. Now fix any
a\in[\mathfrak{f}]
and observe that

(7.27)

(\widehat{\mathsf{E}}^{a}\mathsf{D}^{-1})_{i_{1},i_{2}}\cdot(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{i_{3},i_{4}}=\widehat{m}(z)^{a+\mathfrak{f}+2}\sum_{i_{0}\in\mathbb{I}}\Big[\sum_{\widetilde{\uppi},\uppi_{i_{0}}}\prod_{i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}}\mathsf{E}_{i,j}^{h_{i,j}}\Big]\cdot(\mathsf{D}-\mathsf{E})^{-1}_{i_{0},i_{4}},

where the second sum in the RHS is over all possible paths
\widetilde{\uppi}
of length
a
in the complete graph over
\mathbb{I}
with starting and ending points
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, respectively, and paths
\uppi_{i_{0}}
of length
\mathfrak{f}+1
in the complete graph over
\mathbb{I}
with starting and ending points
i_{3}
and
i_{0}
, respectively. Here
h_{i,j}:=h_{i,j}(\widetilde{\uppi},\uppi_{i_{0}})
denotes the number of times the edge between
i
and
j
is traversed by paths
\widetilde{\uppi}
and
\uppi_{i_{0}}
. Notice that for any
\widetilde{\uppi}
and
\uppi_{i_{0}}
as above

(7.28)

\sum_{i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}}h_{i,j}=a+\mathfrak{f}+1.

Next observe that, on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}

using that
|m(z)|\asymp 1
we obtain that

\max_{i,j\in[n]\setminus\mathbb{I}}|\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z)|\lesssim 1,

and hence by Ward identity (see
[
38
, Eqn. (4.4)]
we further derive that on
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
, as
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
,

\bigg(\max_{i}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\notin\mathbb{I}}|\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z)|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigvee\bigg(\max_{j}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\notin\mathbb{I}}|\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z)|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}=\sqrt{\frac{\max_{i\notin\mathbb{I}}{\rm Im}\,\mathcal{R}_{i,i}^{(\mathbb{I})}(z)}{n\upeta}}\leq n^{-\varepsilon},

for any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Therefore, the bounds (
7.20
)-(
7.21
), and an argument analogous to that in
[
38
, Proposition 3.2]
yield the bound

(7.29)

\max_{i,j\in\mathbb{I}}\mathbb{E}\Big[((\mathsf{E}_{3})_{i,j})^{2h}\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\lesssim((np)\wedge n^{\varepsilon})^{-h},

for any
h\in\mathbb{N}
with
h\asymp 1
on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Furthermore, due to independence we have that

(7.30)

\max_{i,j\in\mathbb{I}}\mathbb{E}\Big[((\mathsf{E}_{2})_{i,j})^{2h}\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\lesssim n^{-1}\cdot(np)^{1-h}\ll(np)^{-h},

as
p\ll 1
, again for any
h\in\mathbb{N}
with
h\asymp 1
. Thus by (
7.28
), (
7.29
)-(
7.30
), Hölder’s inequality, and Theorem
7.1
we deduce that

(7.31)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\prod_{i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}}\mathsf{E}_{i,j}^{2h_{i,j}}\Big){\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}}\mathsf{E}_{i,j}^{2h_{i,j}}\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\bigg]\lesssim((np)\wedge n^{\varepsilon})^{-\mathfrak{f}}.

Hence, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (
7.27
), and the fact that
|\widehat{m}(z)|\lesssim 1
on
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
and
\|\mathcal{R}(z)\|\leq\upeta
, and the lower bound
\upeta
we further see that

(7.32)

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[(\widehat{\mathsf{E}}^{a}\mathsf{D}^{-1})_{i_{1},i_{2}}\cdot(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{i_{3},i_{4}}{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]\Big|\\
\lesssim\max_{i_{0}\in\mathbb{I}}\max_{\widetilde{\uppi},\uppi_{i_{0}}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\prod_{i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}}\mathsf{E}_{i,j}^{2h_{i,j}}\Big){\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big[|\widehat{m}(z)|^{2(a+\mathfrak{f}+2)}\|\mathcal{R}(z)\|{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]}\\
\lesssim((np)\wedge n^{\varepsilon})^{-\mathfrak{f}/2}\cdot\upeta^{-1/2}\ll(np)^{-4},

for
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, any
a\in[\mathfrak{f}]
. Fix this choice of
\mathfrak{f}
for the rest of the proof. Since
\|u\|_{1}\ll np
, the bound (
7.32
) in turn implies that

(7.33)

\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}(\mathcal{R}_{1})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{i_{3},i_{4}}{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]\bigg|\\
\lesssim\|u\|_{1}^{4}\max_{a\in[\mathfrak{f}]}\max_{\mathbb{I}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[(\widehat{\mathsf{E}}^{a}\mathsf{D}^{-1})_{i_{1},i_{2}}\cdot(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{i_{3},i_{4}}{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]\Big|\to 0,

as
n\to+\infty
. Note that a similar argument further yields that

(7.34)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}(\mathcal{R}_{\ell})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathcal{R}_{\ell^{\prime}})_{i_{3},i_{4}}{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]\to 0,

as
n\to+\infty
, for
\ell=2
and
\ell^{\prime}=1,2
. Since
i_{2\ell-1}\neq i_{2\ell}
for
\ell=1,2
and
\mathsf{D}
is a diagonal matrix we also have that
\mathcal{R}_{i_{2\ell-1},i_{2\ell}}=(\mathcal{R}_{1})_{i_{2\ell-1},i_{2\ell}}+(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{i_{2\ell-1},i_{2\ell}}
(see (
7.25
)). Therefore, by (
7.33
)-(
7.34
) and recalling (
7.26
) we deduce that it is enough to prove the following for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in[\mathfrak{f}]
and
k=3,4
:

(7.35)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}\widehat{m}(z)^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}(\mathsf{E}^{a_{1}})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathsf{E}^{a_{2}})_{i_{3},i_{4}}{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}{\bf 1}_{|\mathbb{I}|=k}\bigg]=0.

We will prove that

(7.36)

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[(\mathsf{E}^{a_{1}})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathsf{E}^{a_{2}})_{i_{3},i_{4}}\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\Big|{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}{\bf 1}_{|\mathbb{I}|=k}\lesssim{(np)^{-\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}\cdot(n\upeta)^{-\frac{1}{2}\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}}\qquad\text{ for }k=3,4.

Notice that
u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\subset\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
implies that
\|u\|_{1}\leq n^{1/2}\wedge(s_{n}np)
and hence
\|u\|_{1}^{4}\leq n\cdot(s_{n}np)^{2}
. The latter bound on
\|u\|_{1}
together with (
7.36
), the bound
on
|\widehat{m}(z)|
on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
, and the lower bound on
\upeta
for
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
yield (
7.35
).

We now move on to the proof of (
7.36
). This requires some careful counting arguments. We will represent the LHS of (
7.36
) as a sum of ‘weights’ of a collection of graphs. It needs some notation.

To this end, we let
\mathfrak{P}
to be the set of all finite sequence of paths on
\mathbb{I}
(self loops and backtracking allowed). For a sequence of paths
\mathfrak{G}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}
we set
e(i,j)
to be the number of times the edge
(i,j)
appears in
\mathfrak{G}
. By a slight abuse of notation we write
(i,j)\in\mathfrak{G}
to denote that the edge
(i,j)
belongs to the sequence of paths
\mathfrak{G}
. Further let
{\bm{n}}(\mathfrak{G})
to be the number of distinct edges in
\mathfrak{G}
and write
|\mathfrak{G}|
to denote the total length of the paths in
\mathfrak{G}
. Let

\varpi_{\ell}(\mathfrak{G}):=\prod_{i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}}(\mathsf{E}_{\ell})_{i,j}^{e(i,j)},\qquad\ell=2,3\text{ and }\mathfrak{G}\in\mathfrak{P}.

Since
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{2}+\mathsf{E}_{3}
, expanding the LHS of (
7.36
) and using the joint independence of
\mathsf{E}_{2}
and
\mathsf{E}_{3}
, conditioned on
X^{(\mathbb{I})}
, we see that

(7.37)

\mathbb{E}\Big[(\mathsf{E}^{a_{1}})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathsf{E}^{a_{2}})_{i_{3},i_{4}}\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]=\sum_{\mathfrak{G},\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\varpi_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})\big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\varpi_{3}(\mathfrak{G})\big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big],

where the sum is over those
\mathfrak{G},\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that the alternating concatenation of paths in
\mathfrak{G}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
is the union of a path from
i_{1}
to
i_{2}
and a path from
i_{3}
to
i_{4}
, and
|\mathfrak{G}|+|\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}|=a_{1}+a_{2}
. To bound the LHS of (
7.36
) it suffices to bound each of the summand appearing in the RHS of (
7.37
).

The bound on the first term in the summand is easy. Indeed, by the definition of
X
, as its entries are centred, we have that

(7.38)

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\varpi_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\Big|\lesssim\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{{\bm{n}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})}\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{np}\Big)^{|\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}|/2-{\bm{n}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}\\
(i,j)\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\end{subarray}}{\bf 1}(e(i,j)\geq 2).

Obtaining the bound on the second term in the summand needs additional work. Turning to get such a bound, for any
\mathfrak{G}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
{\bm{\upbeta}}=(\upbeta_{1},\upbeta_{2},\ldots,\upbeta_{2|\mathfrak{G}|})\in([n]\setminus\mathbb{I})^{2|\mathfrak{G}|}
we define
\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
to be a bipartite multigraph with partitions
\mathbb{I}
and
[n]\setminus\mathbb{I}
as follows:
For every
k\in[|\mathfrak{G}|]
replace the
k
-th edge
(i,j)
in
\mathfrak{G}
with the pair of edges
(i,\upbeta_{2k-1})
and
(j,\upbeta_{2k})
. The resulting graph is
\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
. Next for any edge
(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
we further let
e(i,\upbeta)
to be the number of times that edge appears in
\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
,

e_{d}(i,\upbeta):=\Big|\Big\{k\in[|\mathfrak{G}|]:(i,i)\text{ is the }k\text{-th edge in }\mathfrak{G}\text{ and }\upbeta_{2k-1}=\upbeta_{2k}=\beta\Big\}\Big|,

e_{o}(i,\upbeta):=e(i,\upbeta)-2e_{d}(i,\upbeta)
, and
\Delta(i,\upbeta):=e(i,\upbeta)/2-1
. Define
{\bm{n}}(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))
to be the number distinct edges in
\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
. Recalling the definition of
X
and the fact that it is centred we find that

(7.39)

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})}X_{i,\upbeta}^{e_{o}(i,\upbeta)}\big(X_{i,\upbeta}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\big)^{e_{d}(i,\upbeta)}\Big]\Big|\lesssim\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{{\bm{n}}(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))}\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{np}\Big)^{\Delta(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))}\cdot\mathfrak{I}({\bm{\upbeta}}),

where

(7.40)

\mathfrak{I}({\bm{\upbeta}}):=\prod_{(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})}{\bf 1}(e_{o}(i,\upbeta)+e_{d}(i,\upbeta)\geq 2)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Delta(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})):=\sum_{(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta})}}\Delta(i,\upbeta).

On the other hand, as
\widecheck{X}_{\mathbb{I}}
is independent of
X^{(\mathbb{I})}
, upon recalling the definition of
\mathsf{E}_{3}
we find that

(7.41)

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\varpi_{3}(\mathfrak{G})\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\Big|\leq\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}}\varpi(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})}X_{i,\upbeta}^{e_{o}(i,\upbeta)}\big(X_{i,\upbeta}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\big)^{e_{d}(i,\upbeta)}\Big]\Big|,

where

\varpi(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})):=\prod_{k=1}^{|\mathfrak{G}|}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta_{2k-1},\upbeta_{2k}}|.

To obtain the necessary bound on the RHS of (
7.41
) we need to find the same when
\varpi(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))
is summed over various subsets of
{\bm{\upbeta}}
’s. We will iteratively sum over different coordinates of
{\bm{\upbeta}}
that are determined by a sequence of graphs described by ‘partitions’ induced by
{\bm{\upbeta}}
which we define below. For any
{\bm{\upbeta}}
as above we set
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}})
to be the graph with vertex set
{\bm{\upbeta}}
and with edge set
\{(\upbeta_{2k-1},\upbeta_{2k}),k\in[|\mathfrak{G}|]\}
. Obtain
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}({\bm{\upbeta}})
from
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}})
by deleting all self loops, removing the possible resulting isolated vertices, and keeping one edge per each set of parallel edges.
Having defined
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})
for some
\ell\geq 1
we proceed as follows: If the graph is empty or all vertices are of degree one we terminate the procedure. Otherwise, consider the vertex with the largest degree (choose any if there is more than one). Remove that vertex, all edges incident to it, and all degree one neighbours of it (if any). The resulting subgraph is set to be
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell+1}({\bm{\upbeta}})
. Use
{\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell)}
to denote the vertex set of
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})
. Given
{\bm{s}}:=(s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{L})
we let
\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})
to be the indicator function of the subset
{\bm{\upbeta}}
for which the sequence of graphs (described above) is terminated for
\ell=L
and
|{\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell)}|=s_{\ell}
for
\ell=0,1,\ldots,L
. For
\ell\geq 0
, by an abuse of notation, we let
\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell)})=\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})
.

We claim that, on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
, for any indicator function
\mathfrak{I}({\bm{\upbeta}})
we have

(7.42)

\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}}\varpi(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})\mathfrak{I}({\bm{\upbeta}})\leq\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}}\varpi(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})\lesssim n^{s_{0}}\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{n\upeta}\Big)^{s_{1}/4}.

Since
\varpi(\cdot)
is nonnegative the first inequality in (
7.42
) is obvious. To prove the second inequality define the ‘weights’ of the graphs
\{\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})\}_{\ell=0}^{L}
as follows:

\widehat{\varpi}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell)}):=\widehat{\varpi}_{\ell}(\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})):=\prod_{(\upbeta,\upbeta^{\prime})\in\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta,\upbeta^{\prime}}|.

In words, for every edge
(\upbeta,\upbeta^{\prime})\in\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}})
we set its weight to be
|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta,\upbeta^{\prime}}|
. The weight of the graph
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})
,
\ell\geq 0
, is set to be the product of its edge weights (note
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}})
).

Observe that for any
{\bm{\upbeta}}
such that
\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})=1
, when moving from
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}})
to
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}({\bm{\upbeta}})
, the number of vertices lost is
s_{0}-s_{1}
. These are the vertices that participate only in the self loops in
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}})
, and each such vertex has at most
n
choices. Therefore, recalling that on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
one has
\max_{i,j\in[n]\setminus\mathbb{I}}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{i,j}|\lesssim 1
we obtain that

(7.43)

\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}^{(0)}}\widehat{\varpi}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(0)})\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(0)})\lesssim n^{s_{0}-s_{1}}\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}^{(1)}}\widehat{\varpi}_{1}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(1)})\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(1)}).

Next we aim to argue that for any
\ell=1,2\ldots,L-1
we have that

(7.44)

\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell)}}\widehat{\varpi}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell)})\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell)})\lesssim\bigg(\frac{n}{(n\upeta)^{1/4}}\bigg)^{s_{\ell}-s_{\ell+1}}\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell+1)}}\widehat{\varpi}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell+1)})\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(\ell+1)}).

To prove (
7.44
) we need to consider two cases. Without loss of generality let us assume that
\upbeta_{1}
is the vertex with the maximum degree in
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})
. By definition, the maximal degree is at least two. Further assume that all its neighbours have degree at least two, and thus
s_{\ell}-s_{\ell+1}=1
. Pick any two such neighbours, say
\upbeta
and
\upbeta^{\prime}
. By Ward identity (see
[
38
, Eqn. (4.4)]
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
, we find that

\sum_{\upbeta_{1}}\prod_{(\widehat{\upbeta},\upbeta_{1})\in\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\widehat{\upbeta},\upbeta_{1}}|\lesssim\sum_{\upbeta_{1}}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta,\upbeta_{1}}||\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta^{\prime},\upbeta_{1}}|\leq\sqrt{\frac{{\rm Im}\,\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta,\upbeta}}{\upeta}}\sqrt{\frac{{\rm Im}\,\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta^{\prime},\upbeta^{\prime}}}{\upeta}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\upeta}\ll\bigg(\frac{n}{(n\upeta)^{1/4}}\bigg)^{s_{\ell}-s_{\ell+1}},

yielding (
7.44
). Next consider the case when
\upbeta_{1}
has
k
neighbours of degree one, to be denoted by
\widetilde{\upbeta}_{1},\widetilde{\upbeta}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{\upbeta}_{k}
, for some
k\geq 1
. Using Ward identity and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again, on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
, we obtain that

\sum_{\widetilde{\upbeta}_{1},\widetilde{\upbeta}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{\upbeta}_{k}}\sum_{\upbeta_{1}}\prod_{(\widehat{\upbeta},\upbeta_{1})\in\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}({\bm{\upbeta}})}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\widehat{\upbeta},\upbeta_{1}}|\lesssim\sum_{\upbeta_{1}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg(\sum_{\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i}}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta_{1},\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i}}|\bigg)\leq\sum_{\upbeta_{1}}\bigg(\frac{n{\rm Im}\,\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\upbeta_{1},\upbeta_{1}}}{\upeta}\bigg)^{k/2}\lesssim\frac{n^{k+1}}{(n\upeta)^{k/2}}\\
\leq\bigg(\frac{n}{(n\upeta)^{1/4}}\bigg)^{s_{\ell}-s_{\ell+1}},

where in the last step we used that
s_{\ell}-s_{\ell+1}=k+1
and
k\geq 1
. Thus we have (
7.44
) also in this case.

Finally, recalling that
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{L}({\bm{\upbeta}})
is a collection of disjoint edges, denoting
\{(\widehat{\upbeta}_{i},\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i}),i\in[s_{L}/2]\}
to be its set of edges, and applying Ward identity and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yet again we derive that, on the event
\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}
,

(7.45)

\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}^{(L)}}\widehat{\varpi}_{L}({\bm{\upbeta}}^{(L)})\leq\Big(\sum_{\widehat{\upbeta},\widetilde{\upbeta}}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{I})}_{\widehat{\upbeta},\widetilde{\upbeta}}|\Big)^{s_{L}/2}\lesssim\bigg(n\sqrt{\frac{n}{\upeta}}\bigg)^{s_{L}/2}=\bigg(\frac{n}{(n\upeta)^{1/4}}\bigg)^{s_{L}}.

Combining the bounds (
7.43
), (
7.44
), and (
7.45
) we obtain (
7.42
).

Equipped with the necessary bounds we now proceed to combine these bounds and complete the proof of this theorem. By (
7.38
), (
7.39
), and (
7.41
) we have

(7.46)

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\varpi_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\Big|\cdot\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\varpi_{3}(\mathfrak{G})\Big|X^{(\mathbb{I})}\Big]\Big|\lesssim\sum_{\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1}}\sum_{{\bm{s}}}T(\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}})\cdot\widetilde{T},

where

(7.47)

T(\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}}):=T(\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}},\mathfrak{G}):=\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{\upgamma_{0}}\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{np}\Big)^{\upgamma_{1}}\cdot\sum_{{\bm{\upbeta}}}\varpi(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}({\bm{\upbeta}}),

(7.48)

\widetilde{T}:=\widetilde{T}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}):=\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{{\bm{n}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})}\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{np}\Big)^{|\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}|/2-{\bm{n}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq j\in\mathbb{I}\\
(i,j)\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\end{subarray}}{\bf 1}(e(i,j)\geq 2),

and
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}({\bm{\upbeta}}):={\bf 1}({\bm{n}}(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))=\upgamma_{0},\Delta(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))=\upgamma_{1})\cdot\mathfrak{I}({\bm{\upbeta}})
(recall (
7.40
)). We consider two cases:
k=4
and
k=3
, separately. First we consider the case
|\mathbb{I}|=4
.

Recall that the concatenation of paths alternating in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
and
\mathfrak{G}
yield two paths with end points
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, and
i_{3}
and
i_{4}
, hereafter to be denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, respectively. Since
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
are paths and
|\mathbb{I}|=4
we observe that
\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(i)
is odd for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
, where
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\deg_{{\mathcal{P}}}(i)
denotes the degree of
i
in the path
{\mathcal{P}}
, meant as the number of edges in
\mathcal{P}
with one endpoint being
i
. Furthermore, as
\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(i)=\deg_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}}(i)+\deg_{\mathfrak{G}}(i)
we see that either
\deg_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}}(i)
or
\deg_{\mathfrak{G}}(i)
is odd for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
. Let
\mathbb{J}:=\mathbb{J}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}},\mathfrak{G}):=\{i\in\mathbb{I}:\deg_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}}(i)\text{ is odd}\}
. Hence, by the pigeonhole principle there must be at least
\lceil|\mathbb{J}|/2\rceil
distinct edges in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
which appear an
odd
number of times.
By (
7.48
) this means that

(7.49)

\widetilde{T}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})\leq n^{-\big\lceil\frac{|\mathbb{J}|}{2}\big\rceil}\cdot(np)^{-\frac{1}{2}\big\lceil\frac{|\mathbb{J}|}{2}\big\rceil}.

On the other hand, recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
we note that
\deg_{\mathfrak{G}}(i)=\sum_{\upbeta\in{\bm{\upbeta}}}e(i,\upbeta)
. This in turn yields that for any
i\in\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J}
there must exist some
\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i}\in{\bm{\upbeta}}
such that
e(i,\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i})
is odd and so is
e_{o}(i,\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i})
. For
{\bm{\upbeta}}
such that
\mathfrak{I}({\bm{\upbeta}})=1
(recall (
7.40
)) we therefore have that
\Delta(i,\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i})\geq 1/2
for any
i\in\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J}
, and thus
\Delta(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))\geq\frac{1}{2}|(\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J})|
. Further let
\widehat{\mathbb{J}}:=\{\widetilde{\upbeta}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J}\}
. Observe that, by definition, for any
{\bm{\upbeta}}
such that
\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})=1
we have that
s_{1}\geq|\widehat{\mathbb{J}}|
.

We also claim that for any
{\bm{\upbeta}}
such that
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}({\bm{\upbeta}})=\mathfrak{J}_{{\bm{s}}}({\bm{\upbeta}})=1
we must have that

(7.50)

\upgamma_{0}-s_{0}\geq|(\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J})|-|\widehat{\mathbb{J}}|\geq 0.

The second inequality is obvious by the definition of
\widetilde{\mathbb{J}}
.
To obtain the other inequality, recalling the definition of
\upgamma_{0}
and
s_{0}
for any
{\bm{\upbeta}}
such that
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}({\bm{\upbeta}})=1
we note that

\upgamma_{0}-s_{0}=\Big(\sum_{(i,\upbeta):(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})}1\Big)-\Big(\sum_{\upbeta\in{\bm{\upbeta}}}1\Big)=\sum_{\upbeta\in{\bm{\upbeta}}}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}:(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})}1\Big)-1\Big]\geq\sum_{\upbeta\in\widehat{\mathbb{J}}}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}:(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})}1\Big)-1\Big]\\
\geq\sum_{\upbeta\in\widehat{\mathbb{J}}}\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J}:(i,\upbeta)\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})}1\Big)-|\widehat{\mathbb{J}}|\geq|(\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J})|-|\widehat{\mathbb{J}}|,

where the third last step follows from the fact that the summands are nonnegative.

Using the bounds (
7.42
),
\Delta(\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}}))\geq|(\mathbb{I}\setminus\mathbb{J})|/2
,
s_{1}\geq|\widehat{\mathbb{J}}|
, and (
7.50
), denoting
|\mathbb{J}|=\upgamma_{2}
, and splitting the sum in the RHS of (
7.47
) according to the value of
|\widehat{\mathbb{J}}|=\widehat{\upgamma}_{2}
we deduce that

(7.51)

T(\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}})\cdot\widetilde{T}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})\lesssim n^{-|\mathbb{I}|}(np)^{-|\mathbb{I}|/2}\max_{\upgamma_{2},\widehat{\upgamma}_{2}:\upgamma_{2}+\widehat{\upgamma}_{2}\leq|\mathbb{I}|}\exp(F(\upgamma_{2},\widehat{\upgamma}_{2})),

where

F(\upgamma_{2},\widehat{\upgamma}_{2}):=\frac{\upgamma_{2}}{4}\log(np)-\frac{\widehat{\upgamma}_{2}}{4}\log(n\upeta)+(\widehat{\upgamma}_{2}+\upgamma_{2}/2)\log n

Since
\widehat{\upgamma}_{2}\mapsto F(\cdot,\widehat{\upgamma}_{2})
is increasing, for
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
, using that
|\mathbb{I}|=4
, we obtain from above that

(7.52)

T(\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}})\cdot\widetilde{T}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}})\lesssim n^{-4}(np)^{-2}\max_{\upgamma_{2}=0}^{4}\exp(F(\upgamma_{2},4-\upgamma_{2}))=(np)^{-2}\cdot(n\upeta)^{-1}\max_{\upgamma_{2}=0}^{4}\bigg(\frac{np\cdot n\upeta}{n^{2}}\bigg)^{\upgamma_{2}/4}\\
=(np)^{-2}\cdot(n\upeta)^{-1},

for all large
n
, where in the last step we used that
p\upeta\ll 1
. Since the choices of
\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
, and
\mathfrak{G}
are
O(1)
the bound together with (
7.37
) and (
7.46
) yield (
7.36
) for
k=4
.

It now remains to consider the case
k=3
. he proof splits into a few sub cases.

Since the concatenation of paths alternating in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
and
\mathfrak{G}
yield two paths with end points
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, and
i_{3}
and
i_{4}
, we see that it must contain two distinct edges, to be denoted by
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, that are not self loops.

Therefore, if
e_{1},e_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
then
\widetilde{T}\lesssim n^{-2}
.
As
T(\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}})\lesssim 1
(see (
7.47
), use (
7.42
) and the fact that
\upgamma_{0}\geq s_{0}
) we get the desired bound.

Next we assume
e_{1}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathfrak{G}
and
e_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
. By definition there must exist
\upbeta_{1},\upbeta_{2}
such that
(j_{1},\upbeta_{1}),(j_{2},\upbeta_{2})\in\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
.
If
\upbeta_{1}\neq\upbeta_{2}
then
s_{1}\geq 2
. Observe that either there is an edge that appears in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
an odd number of times, or else repeating the proof for the case
k=4
it follows that
\upgamma_{1}\geq 1
. By (
7.48
), in the first sub case we have
\widetilde{T}\lesssim n^{-1}(np)^{-1/2}
, while in the second sub case
\widetilde{T}\lesssim n^{-1}
. Hence, from (
7.47
), (
7.42
), and (
7.50
) the desired bound follows.

If
\upbeta=\upbeta_{2}
, as
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
, from the definition of
\mathfrak{G}({\bm{\upbeta}})
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}({\bm{\upbeta}})
it follows that
\upgamma_{0}\geq s_{0}+1
In this case, using the bound
\widetilde{T}\lesssim n^{-1}
, and the bounds (
7.47
) and (
7.42
) we obtain the required bound.

Now we assume
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathfrak{G}
. Similar to
e_{1}
, as above, we can associate some
\upbeta_{3}
and
\upbeta_{4}
to
e_{2}
. If
\upbeta_{1}\neq\upbeta_{2}
and
\upbeta_{3}\neq\upbeta_{4}
then
s_{1}\geq 4
. If
\upbeta_{\ell},\ell=1,\ldots,4
, are all same then, as three distinct vertices participate in
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, it follows that
\upgamma_{0}-s_{0}\geq 2
. Moreover, for
\upbeta_{1}\neq\upbeta_{2}
and
\upbeta_{3}=\upbeta_{4}
we have
s_{1}\geq 2
and
\upgamma_{0}-s_{0}\geq 1
. Furthermore,
\upgamma_{1}\geq 1
. Therefore, in all these cases, from (
7.47
) and (
7.42
), we find that
T(\upgamma_{0},\upgamma_{1},{\bm{s}})\lesssim(np)^{-1}(n\upeta)^{-1}
.

Hence combining all sub cases we have the required bound for
k=3
and thus the proof of the theorem for
X
is finally complete.

The reader can note that the above proof does not use the product structure of the entries of
X
. Therefore, repeating the exact same arguments one derives the required result for
{\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np}
. We omit further details.
∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
7.1

The required result for
{\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np}
follows from
[
38
, Theorem 2.2]
. The proof for
X
follows a similar strategy.
The difference is that we need to use the concentration bounds proved in Lemma
7.4
instead of the concentration bounds in
[
38
]
. We provide a short proof for the convenience of the reader.

Set
\widetilde{M}:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{N}:1-kn^{-3}\geq n^{-1+\varepsilon}/2\}
. Fix
E_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|E_{0}\pm 2|\geq\kappa
and
|E_{0}|\leq\kappa^{-1}
. Let
\upeta_{k}:=1-kn^{-3}
and define
z_{k}:=E_{0}+{\rm i}\upeta_{k}
for
k=0,1,\ldots,\widetilde{M}
. Since the maps
z\mapsto\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{i,j}(z,X)
and
z\mapsto m(z)
are
n^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
- Lipschitz functions on the set
\mathbb{H}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
we observe that it suffices to prove that for any
M>0
there exists
C<+\infty
such that

(7.53)

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\max_{i,j\in[n]\setminus\mathbb{T}}|\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbb{T})}_{i,j}(z_{k},X)-m(z_{k})\updelta_{i,j}|\geq C\Upsilon_{n}\bigg)\leq n^{-M},

for all large
n
and
k=0,1,\ldots,\widetilde{M}
, uniformly for all
\mathbb{T}\subset[n]
such that
|\mathbb{T}|\lesssim 1
and
E_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|E_{0}\pm 2|\geq\kappa
and
|E_{0}|\leq\kappa^{-1}
. Since, for
i,j,k\notin\mathbb{T}
and
i,j\neq k

(7.54)

\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(\mathbb{T}\cup\{k\})}=\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(\mathbb{T})}-\frac{\mathcal{R}_{i,k}^{(\mathbb{T})}\mathcal{R}_{k,j}^{(\mathbb{T})}}{\mathcal{R}_{k,k}^{(\mathbb{T})}},

(see
[
16
, Lemma 3.5]
) and
|m(z)|\asymp 1
on
\mathbb{H}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
(see
[
32
, Lemma 3.2]
) we deduce that it is enough to prove (
7.53
) for
\mathbb{T}=\emptyset
.

Turning to prove (
7.53
) when
\mathbb{T}=\emptyset
, using the Schur complement formula (see
[
16
, Eqn. (4.1)]
) it follows that

(7.55)

\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}_{i,i}}=z-X_{i,i}-\sum_{k,\ell\neq i}X_{i,k}\mathcal{R}_{k,\ell}^{(i)}X_{\ell,i}\\
=z-m_{n}(z)+(m_{n}(z)-m_{n}^{(i)}(z))-X_{i,i}-\sum_{k\neq\ell\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}X_{i,k}\mathcal{R}_{k,\ell}^{(i)}X_{\ell,i}-\sum_{k\neq i}(X_{i,k}^{2}-n^{-1})\mathcal{R}_{k,k}^{(i)},

where we recall that
m_{n}^{(i)}(z)=n^{-1}{\rm Tr}\,\mathcal{R}^{(i)}(z)
. Multiplying both sides of (
7.55
) by
\mathcal{R}_{i,i}
and taking an average over
i\in[n]
we obtain from above

(7.56)

\mathfrak{p}_{z}(m_{n}(z))=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{Y}_{i}(z)\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z),

where

\widehat{Y}_{i}:=\widehat{Y}_{i}(z):=(m_{n}(z)-m_{n}^{(i)}(z))-X_{i,i}-\sum_{k\neq\ell\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}X_{i,k}\mathcal{R}_{k,\ell}^{(i)}X_{\ell,i}-\sum_{k\neq i}(X_{i,k}^{2}-n^{-1})\mathcal{R}_{k,k}^{(i)}

and
\mathfrak{p}_{z}(w):=1-zw+w^{2}
for
w\in\mathbb{C}
. For convenience further let, for
k=0,1,\ldots,\widetilde{M}
,

\Upomega_{k}:=\Big\{|m_{n}(z_{k})-m(z_{k})|\leq\frac{C}{4}\Upsilon_{n}\Big\},\quad\widehat{\Upomega}_{k}:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\Upomega_{i,k},\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{\Upomega}_{i,k}:=\big\{\Upgamma_{i}(z_{k})\leq\frac{3}{2}\big\},

where
\Upgamma_{i}(z):=\max_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\neq i}|\mathcal{R}^{(i)}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)|
. We also set
\Upgamma^{\prime}(z):=\max_{i\in[n]}|\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z)|
and
\Upomega_{k}^{\prime}:=\{\Upgamma^{\prime}(z_{k})\leq 3/2\}
.
Note that by Assumption
1.2
, for any
M>0
there exists some
C<+\infty
such hat

\max_{i\in[n]}|X_{i,i}|\leq\frac{\sqrt{C}}{8}\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{np}},

with probability at least
1-n^{-4M}
. Hence on the event
\widehat{\Upomega}_{k}
, by Lemma
7.4
(b) and (c), the Ward identity, and the fact that
|m_{n}^{(i)}(z_{k})-m_{n}(z_{k})|\leq(n{\rm Im}\,z_{k})^{-1}
we obtain that

(7.57)

\max_{i\in[n]}|\widehat{Y}_{i}(z_{k})|\leq\frac{\sqrt{C}}{4}\Upsilon_{n},

for some sufficiently large constant
C
, with probability at least
1-n^{-2M}
, i.e. the probability of the intersection of
\widehat{\Upomega}_{k}
and the event such that (
7.57
) does not hold is at most
n^{-2M}
. Thus, by (
7.56
) on the event
\widehat{\Upomega}_{k}\cap\Upomega_{k}^{\prime}
the event
\widetilde{\Upomega}_{k}
holds with probability at least
1-n^{-2M}
, where

\widetilde{\Upomega}_{k}:=\big\{|\mathfrak{p}_{z_{k}}(m_{n}(z_{k}))|\leq\frac{\sqrt{C}}{2}\Upsilon_{n}\big\},\qquad k=0,1,2,\ldots,\widetilde{M}.

Observe that
\mathfrak{p}_{z}(m_{n}(z))=(w_{+}(z)-m_{n}(z))(w_{-}(z)-m_{n}(z))
, where
w_{\pm}(z):=\frac{1}{2}(z\pm\sqrt{z^{2}-4})
are the roots of quadratic polynomial
\mathfrak{p}_{z}
. As
|w_{+}(z)-w_{-}(z)|\geq\sqrt{\kappa}
for any
z\in\mathbb{H}_{\kappa}
we find that on the event
\widetilde{\Upomega}_{k}
one has that

(7.58)

|m_{n}(z_{k})-w_{-}(z_{k})|\wedge|m_{n}(z_{k})-w_{+}(z_{k})|\leq\frac{C}{2}\Upsilon_{n},

where we enlarge
C
if necessary. Notice that, by the trivial bound
\|\mathcal{R}(z_{0})\|\leq\upeta_{0}^{-1}=1
we have
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\Upomega}_{0}\cap\Upomega_{0}^{\prime})=1
. As
{\rm Im}\,m_{n}(z_{0})\leq 0
,
{\rm Im}\,w_{+}(z_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}{\rm Im}\,z_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and
w_{-}(z)=m(z)
we deduce from above that
\widetilde{\Upomega}_{0}\subset\Upomega_{0}
. Thus we have shown that
\mathbb{P}(\Upomega_{0}^{c})\leq n^{-2M}
.

We next proceed to argue that on the event
\Upomega_{k}
the required bounds on the individual entries of
\mathcal{R}(z_{k})
(recall (
7.53
)) hold. To this end, by (
7.55
) and the fact that
m(z_{k})+m(z_{k})^{-1}=z
we see that

1-m(z_{k})^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z_{k})=1-(z_{k}-m(z_{k}))\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z_{k})=(m(z_{k})-m_{n}(z_{k}))\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z_{k})+\widehat{Y}_{i}(z_{k})\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z_{k}).

Therefore, using
|m(z_{k})|\leq 1
and (
7.57
) we obtain that

\max_{i\in[n]}|\mathcal{R}_{i,i}(z_{k})-m(z_{k})|\leq\frac{3}{2}\big(|m(z_{k})-m_{n}(z_{k})|+\max_{i\in[n]}|\widehat{Y}_{i}(z_{k})|\big)\leq C\Upsilon_{n},

on the event
\Upomega_{k}\cap\widehat{\Upomega}_{k}\cap\Upomega_{k}^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-n^{-2M}
. To treat the off-diagonal entries we use the identity

\mathcal{R}_{i,j}(z)=-\mathcal{R}_{j,j}(z)\sum_{k\neq j}\mathcal{R}_{i,k}^{(j)}X_{k,j},\quad i\neq j.

This identity together with Lemma
7.4
(a) and the Ward identity imply that the desired bound on the off-diagonal entries hold with probability at least
1-n^{-2M}
on the event
\widehat{\Upomega}_{k}\cap\Upomega_{k}^{\prime}
. Now applying the union bound we derive (
7.53
) for
k=0
.

To complete the proof we do an induction on
k
. So, assume that the bound (
7.53
) holds for
k=0,1,2,\ldots,k_{0}
for some
k_{0}<\widetilde{M}
and let
\Upomega_{k}^{*}
be the complement of the event on the LHS of (
7.53
). Since the maps
z\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{i,j}(z)
and
z\mapsto m(z)
are
n^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
- Lipschitz functions on
\mathbb{H}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
, using
|m(z)|\leq 1
and (
7.54
) we find that
\Upomega_{k_{0}}^{*}\subset\widehat{\Upomega}_{k_{0}+1}\cap\Upomega_{k_{0}+1}^{\prime}
. Hence, we find that on
\Omega_{k_{0}}^{*}
the event
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{0}+1}
, and thus (
7.58
) for
k=k_{0}+1
, hold with probability at least
1-n^{-2M}
. The Lipschitz continuity of
m_{n}
and
m
further implies on the event
\Upomega_{k_{0}}^{*}
, that
|m_{n}(z_{k_{0}+1})-m(z_{k_{0}+1})|=o(1)
, which in turn implies that
|m_{n}(z_{k_{0}+1})-w_{+}(z_{k_{0}+1})|\gtrsim 1
. As we saw, this entails that on the same event
\Upomega_{k_{0}}^{*}
the event
\Omega_{k_{0}+1}
holds with probability at least
1-n^{-2M}
. As a consequence the desired bounds on the entries of
\mathcal{R}(z_{k_{0}+1})
hold on
\Omega_{k_{0}}^{*}
with high probability.
This completes the induction and the proof of the theorem.
∎

7.4.
Isotropic local law for the centred adjacency matrix

In this short section we prove Proposition
3.7
. We need the following isotropic law.

Theorem 7.5
.

Assume
\log(np)\gtrsim\log n
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
be as in Theorem
7.2
. Fix
\kappa,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{z\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}_{n}}\big|\mathbb{E}\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z,{\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np})v\rangle-m(z)\langle u,v\rangle\big|=0,

where
\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}:=\{z\in\mathbb{H}_{\kappa}:s_{n}\vee(np)^{-1+\varepsilon}\leq{\rm Im}\,z\leq\kappa^{-1}\}
.

Notice the differences between Theorems
7.2
and
7.5
. Theorem
7.5
allows us consider
\langle u,\mathcal{R}(z)v\rangle
without putting any restriction on
\|v\|_{1}
. However, it imposes a stronger lower bound on
p
.

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.

For
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
f_{\lambda,u}
defined on
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\lambda+\delta}
be as in the proof of Proposition
3.2
and
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u}
its convex and
\delta^{-2}\|u\|^{2}
-Lipschitz extension to
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. By
[
24
, Theorem 8.6]
for any
t>0
,

(7.59)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,w}({\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np})-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,w}({\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np})]\big|\geq t\Big)\leq\exp\big(-{c\delta^{4}t^{2}np}\big),

uniformly for any
w\in 2\mathbb{B}^{n}
, where
c>0
is some numerical constant. For
u,v\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, let
\widetilde{g}_{\lambda,u,v}(K)=\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u+v}(K)-\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,u-v}(K)
. By the polarization identity,
\widetilde{g}_{\lambda,u,v}(K)=4\langle u,(\lambda-K)^{-1}v\rangle
for any
K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\lambda+\delta}
. Arguing as in the proof of (
7.2
) we find that

(7.60)

\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}|\mathbb{E}\widetilde{g}_{\lambda,u,v}({\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np})-4m(\lambda)\langle u,v\rangle|=o(1).

By a union bound, (
7.59
) entails a similar concentration inequality for
\widetilde{g}_{\lambda,u,v}
instead of
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda,w}
. Using the same net argument as in the proof of proof of Proposition
3.2
, we finally get the claim.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.5
.

The proof is a minor modification of that of Theorem
7.2
. We reuse the bounds obtained during the proof of Theorem
7.2
. Proceeding as in the steps leading to (
7.24
) we find that

\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}v_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}v_{i_{4}}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},i_{2}}(z)\mathcal{R}_{i_{3},i_{4}}(z){\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]\to 0,\quad\text{ as }n\to+\infty,

where
\mathcal{R}(z)=\mathcal{R}(z,{\rm Adj}_{o}/\sqrt{np})
. Since
\log(np)\gtrsim\log n
, one can choose
\mathfrak{f}
large enough so that (
7.32
) holds with
(np)^{-4}
replaced by
n^{-3}
. Therefore, arguing as in (
7.33
), as
\|u\|_{1}\vee\|v\|_{1}\leq n^{1/2}
, we find that

\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}v_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}v_{i_{4}}(\mathcal{R}_{1})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{i_{3},i_{4}}{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}\Big]\bigg|\to 0,\quad\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

Hence, similar to (
7.35
), we deduce that it is enough to prove that for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in[\mathfrak{f}]
and
k=3,4
:

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}u_{i_{1}}v_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}v_{i_{4}}\widehat{m}(z)^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}(\mathsf{E}^{a_{1}})_{i_{1},i_{2}}(\mathsf{E}^{a_{2}})_{i_{3},i_{4}}{\bf 1}_{\widecheck{\Omega}_{\mathbb{I}}}{\bf 1}_{|\mathbb{I}|=k}\bigg]=0.

Since
\|u\|_{1}\leq s_{n}np
and
\|v\|_{1}\leq n^{1/2}
, for
z\in\widehat{\mathbb{H}}_{\kappa}
the above follows from the bound (
7.36
). We omit the details. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Proofs of Lemmas
3.5
,
4.4
, and
4.5

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

It is trivial to note that
(-\infty,0]\subset\mathcal{D}_{L}
. Fix any
\theta\in(0,1/(2\beta))
and
\widehat{R}>0
. Let
\Gamma
be an independent standard Gaussian random variable. Observe that, by Assumption
1.2
,

(A.1)

L(\theta)=\mathbb{E}\big[\exp({\sqrt{2\theta}G_{1,2}\Gamma})\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\exp\big({\Lambda_{1,2}(\sqrt{2\theta}\Gamma)}\big)\big]\\
\leq\exp(\upsigma\widehat{R}^{2})+\mathbb{E}\big({\bf 1}_{|\Gamma|\geq\widehat{R}/\sqrt{2\theta}}e^{\beta(1+o_{\widehat{R}}(1))\theta\Gamma^{2}}\big)<+\infty,

where the last step follows from the fact that, as
\theta<1/(2\beta)
, one can choose
\widehat{R}
sufficiently large so that
\beta\theta(1+o_{\widehat{R}}(1))<1/2
. On the other hand, fixing any
\theta>1/(2\beta)
(assume
\beta>0
) and arguing as in (
A.1
) we obtain that

(A.2)

L(\theta)\geq\mathbb{E}\big({\bf 1}_{|\Gamma|\geq\widehat{R}/\sqrt{2\theta}}e^{\beta(1-o_{\widehat{R}}(1))\theta\Gamma^{2}}\big)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\widehat{R}/\sqrt{2\theta}}^{+\infty}\exp\Big(\Big\{\beta(1-o_{\widehat{R}}(1))\theta-\frac{1}{2}\Big\}x^{2}\Big)dx=+\infty,

where the last step follows upon choosing
\widehat{R}
large so that
\beta(1-o_{\widehat{R}}(1))\theta>1/2
. Combining (
A.1
)-(
A.2
) we have part (a). The proof of part (b) being standard is omitted.

Since
L
is convex and infinitely differentiable on
{\rm Int}(\mathcal{D}_{L})
, fixing
\theta,\theta_{0}<1/(2\beta)
we find that

L(\theta)\leq L(\theta_{0})+(\theta-\theta_{0})L^{\prime}(\theta).

Now, letting
\theta\uparrow 1/(2\beta)
above part (c) follows. Turning to prove (d), provided
x_{\star}<+\infty
, by parts (a) and (c) we find that

(A.3)

h_{L}(x)=\sup_{\theta\in(-\infty,1/(2\beta)]}\{\theta x-L(\theta)+1\},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

By parts (b) and (c), for any
x\geq x_{\star}
, the map
\theta\mapsto\theta x-L(\theta)
is strictly increasing on
(-\infty,1/(2\beta))
. Therefore, by (
A.3
) and part (c) we have part (d).

It remains to prove part (e).
Upon denoting
\partial(L-1)(\cdot)
to be the sub differential of
L(\cdot)-1
and using the fact that
\lim_{\theta\uparrow 1/(2\beta)}L^{\prime}(\theta)=x_{\star}
we note that

\lx@nobreakspace\partial(L-1)(\theta)=\begin{cases}\{L^{\prime}(\theta)\}&\text{ if }\theta<1/(2\beta),\\
[x_{\star},+\infty)&\text{ if }\theta=1/(2\beta),\\
\emptyset&\text{\lx@nobreakspace if }\theta>1/(2\beta).\end{cases}

From
[
46
, Corollary 23.5.1]
we know that the sub differentials of
L-1
and
h_{L}
are inverse from each other in the sense of multivalued functions.
As
L^{\prime}
is strictly increasing and strictly convex on
{\rm Int}(\mathcal{D}_{L})
we therefore have from above that the function
h_{L}^{\prime}
exists on
(0,x_{\star})
. Moreover, on the same interval
h_{L}^{\prime}
is the inverse of the map
L^{\prime}
restricted to
{\rm Int}(\mathcal{D}_{L})
, and therefore is strictly increasing, strictly concave, and differentiable on
(0,x_{\star})
. By part (d) this implies that
h_{L}^{\prime}
is differentiable except possibly at
x_{\star}
.

Furthermore,
h_{L}^{\prime}(x)=\theta(x)
for
x\in(0,x_{\star})
where
\theta(x)\in(-\infty,1/(2\beta))
is the unique solution of the equation
L^{\prime}(\theta(x))=x
. Using that
L^{\prime}
is increasing on
{\rm Int}(\mathcal{D}_{L})
it can be argued that
\theta(x)\uparrow 1/(2\beta)
as
x\uparrow x_{\star}
which together with the fact that
h_{L}
is convex imply that the left derivative of
h_{L}
at
x=x_{\star}
is bounded below by
1/(2\beta)
. On the other hand, by part (d)
h_{L}^{\prime}(x)=1/(2\beta)
for
x>x_{\star}
and so the right derivative at
x=x_{\star}
is bounded above by
1/(2\beta)
. Thus
h_{L}
is differentiable on
(0,+\infty)
. As
h_{L}^{\prime}
is strictly increasing and concave on
(0,x_{\star})
,
h_{L}^{\prime}(x)=1/(2\beta)
, and
h_{L}^{\prime}
is continuous on
(0,+\infty)
it is straightforward to see that it is concave and non decreasing on entire
(0,+\infty)
.

To prove that
h_{L}
is increasing on
(1,+\infty)
and decreasing on
(0,1)
we recall that
h_{L}^{\prime}(x)=\theta(x)
for
x\in(0,x_{\star})
and
h_{L}^{\prime}(x)=1/(2\beta)
for
x\geq x_{\star}
. The requirement
L^{\prime}(\theta(x))=x
and the fact that
\mathbb{E}G_{1,2}^{2}=1
implies that
\theta(x)
is either positive or negative depending on whether
x\in(1,x_{\star})
or
x<1
proving the desired property for
h_{L}
. Finally, as
L^{\prime}(0)=1
, we have
h_{L}^{\prime}(1)=0
which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

By
[
24
, Theorem 15.5]
, we know that for any
q\geq 2
,
\|(Z-\mathbb{E}Z)_{+}\|_{q}\leq\sqrt{2\kappa q}\|\sqrt{V^{+}}\|_{q}
,
where
\kappa
is some positive numerical constant.
Using the fact that
V^{+}\leq\varepsilon^{2}
on
E_{\varepsilon}
, and that
V^{+}\leq 1
on
E_{\varepsilon}^{c}
, we get

\lx@nobreakspace\|\sqrt{V^{+}}\|_{q}\leq\|{\bf 1}_{E_{\varepsilon}}\sqrt{V^{+}}\|_{q}+\|{\bf 1}_{E_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\sqrt{V^{+}}\|_{q}\leq\big(\varepsilon+\mathbb{P}(E_{\varepsilon}^{c})^{1/q}\big).

For any
q\leq q_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}(E_{\varepsilon}^{c})^{1/q}\leq\varepsilon
, and therefore
\|\big(Z-\mathbb{E}Z\big)_{+}\|_{q}\leq 2\sqrt{2\kappa q}\varepsilon
.
By Markov inequality, we have for any
t>0
and
2\leq q\leq q_{0}
,

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbb{P}\big(Z-\mathbb{E}Z>t\big)\leq(c\varepsilon^{2}q)^{q/2}t^{-q},

where
c
is some positive numerical constant. If
2ce\varepsilon^{2}\leq t^{2}\leq ce\varepsilon^{2}q_{0}
then one applies the above inequality for the optimal moment order
q=(ce\varepsilon^{2})^{-1}t^{2}
which indeed gives (
4.10
).
This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

The first claim can be read off
[
44
, Lemma 24]
using the Legendre duality.
By definition, for any
x\in(-p,1-p)
,

\lx@nobreakspace\Lambda_{p}^{*}(x)=(x+p)\log\frac{x+p}{p}+(1-x-p)\log\frac{1-x-p}{1-p}.

Thus, for any
x\in(-p,1-p)
,

(\Lambda^{*}_{p})^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{x+p}+\frac{1}{1-x-p},\ (\Lambda_{p}^{*})^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{(1-x-p)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(x+p)^{2}}.

This yields that
(\Lambda_{p}^{*})^{\prime\prime}
is non increasing on
[-p,1/2-p]
.
Therefore noting that
\Lambda_{p}^{*}(0)=(\Lambda_{p}^{*})^{\prime}(0)=0
, we get for any
-p\leq x\leq Cp
,

\lx@nobreakspace\Lambda_{p}^{*}(x)\geq(\Lambda^{*}_{p})^{\prime\prime}(Cp)\frac{x^{2}}{2}\geq\frac{x^{2}}{2(C+1)p}.

For the third claim, the concavity of the
\log
yields for any
x\in(-p,1-p)
,

\lx@nobreakspace\Lambda_{p}^{*}(x)\geq x\log\frac{x}{p}-(1-x-p)\frac{x}{1-p}\geq x\log\frac{x}{p}-x\geq\Big(1-\frac{1}{\log C}\Big)x\log\frac{x}{p}.

where the last step holds for
x\geq Cp
, with
C\geq e
.
∎

Appendix B
Measurable selection of sub differentials

Lemma B.1
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{N}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a convex function and
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
a closed set such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
F\cap\partial f(x)\neq\emptyset
. Then, there exists
\zeta:\mathbb{R}^{N}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{N}
a Borel function such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\zeta(x)\in\partial f(x)\cap F
.

Proof.

As
\partial f(x)
is closed for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
(see
[
27
, Corollary 4.7]
), it follows that
x\mapsto\partial f(x)\cap F
is a mapping with values in the set of nonempty closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We will show that for any open set
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\partial f(x)\cap F\cap U\neq\emptyset\}
is a Borel subset. Using
[
21
, Theorem 6.9.3]
this will give the claim. Since every open set of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
can be written as a countable union of compact subsets, it is sufficient to prove that for any compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
g(K):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\partial f(x)\cap K\neq\emptyset\}
is a Borel set. Let
\varphi(x,\zeta):=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\big(f^{\prime}(x;v)-\langle\zeta,v\rangle\big)\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
for any
\zeta,x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, where
f^{\prime}(x;v)
denotes the directional derivative of
f
at
x
in the direction
v
. As
f
is convex and finite valued on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
it follows from
[
27
, Corollary 2.35]
that
f
is locally Lipschitz from which we get that
v\mapsto f^{\prime}(x;v)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
Therefore so is the map

v\mapsto f^{\prime}(x;v)-\langle\zeta,v\rangle
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Thus the infimum defining
\varphi(x,\zeta)
can be taken in a dense countable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Since
x\mapsto f^{\prime}(x;v)
is measurable for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, it yields that
x\mapsto\varphi(x,\zeta)
is measurable for any
\zeta
. Moreover, note that
\zeta\mapsto\varphi(x,\zeta)
is continuous for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Now, using that
f^{\prime}(x;.)
is homogenous and
[
27
, Proposition 4.3]
, we can write
\partial f(x)=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\varphi(x,\zeta)\geq 0\}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Taking
D
to be a countable dense subset of
K
, it follows from the stated properties of
\varphi
that

\lx@nobreakspace g(K)=\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\bigcup_{\zeta\in D}\{x:\varphi(x,\zeta)>-1/k\},

which shows that
g(K)
is a Borel set and therefore the proof ends.
∎

Appendix C
Upper tail large deviations of sum of independent variables

The following proposition is well known in the literature. For the sake of completeness, we state it in the generality that we need in this article and provide a short proof.

Proposition C.1
.

Fix
L\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
\delta>0
let
(X_{n,\delta}^{(k)})
,
k\in[L]
be sequences of nonnegative random variables. Assume that there exist
I_{k}
,
k\in[L]
non decreasing good rate functions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and an increasing positive sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, diverging to infinity, such that for any
k\in[L]
and
t>0
,

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{\delta\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{v_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(X_{n,\delta}^{(k)}>t\big)\leq-I_{k}(t).

Then, for any deterministic sequence
(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in a compact metric space
(\mathcal{K},d)
, any continuous functions
f_{k}:\mathcal{K}\to[0,+\infty)
,
k\in[L]
, and
t>0
,

\limsup_{\delta\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{k=1}^{L}f_{k}(\kappa_{n})X_{n,\delta}^{(k)}>t\big)\leq-\Phi(t),

where
J
is the lower semicontinuous function defined by

\lx@nobreakspace\Phi(t):=\inf\big\{\sum_{k=1}^{L}I_{k}(x_{k}):\sum_{k=1}^{L}f_{k}(\kappa)x_{k}\geq t,x_{k}\geq 0,k\in[L],\kappa\in\mathcal{K}\big\}.

Proof.

For convenience we only prove the case
L=2
. The general case is a straightforward extension. Let
0<t<M
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, let
\varepsilon=t_{1,\varepsilon}<t_{2,\varepsilon}<\cdots<t_{m,\varepsilon}=M
be a sequence of real number such that
\max_{i}(t_{i+1,\varepsilon}-t_{i,\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon
. One can find such a mesh with
m\leq M/\varepsilon+1
. As
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are uniformly continuous, we can find a
\delta>0
such that, for any
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
,

(C.1)

d(\kappa,\kappa^{\prime})<\delta\Longrightarrow|f_{1}(\kappa)-f_{1}(\kappa^{\prime})|<\varepsilon,\ |f_{2}(\kappa)-f_{2}(\kappa^{\prime})|<\varepsilon,

Let then
s_{1},\ldots,s_{q}\in\mathcal{K}
,
q\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathcal{K}
is covered by the open balls
B_{d}(\kappa_{i},\delta)
of center
\kappa_{i}
and radius
\delta
for the metric
d
on
\mathcal{K}
. Let
L>M\vee\|f_{1}\|_{\infty}\vee\|f_{2}\|_{\infty}
. Set
Z_{n,\delta}:=f_{1}(\kappa_{n})X_{n,\delta}^{(1)}+f_{2}(\kappa_{n})X_{n,\delta}^{(2)}
. Using (
C.1
),

\lx@nobreakspace\{Z_{n,\delta}>t,X_{n,\delta}^{(1)}\vee X_{n,\delta}^{(2)}\leq M\}\subset\bigcup_{(i,j)\in T}\{X_{n,\delta}^{(1)}>t_{i,\varepsilon},X_{n,\delta}^{(2)}>t_{j,\varepsilon}\},

where
T:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{q}\{(i,j)\in[m]^{2}:f_{1}(s_{k})t_{i,\varepsilon}+f_{2}(s_{k})t_{j,\varepsilon}>t-3\varepsilon L\}
.
Using a union bound and the independence of
X_{n,R}^{(1)}
and
X_{n,R}^{(2)}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{v_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{n,\delta}>t,X_{n,\delta}^{(1)}\vee X_{n,\delta}^{(2)}\leq M\big)

\displaystyle\leq-\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\min_{(i,j)\in T}\big(I_{1}(t_{i,\varepsilon})+I_{2}(t_{j,\varepsilon})\big)

(C.2)

\displaystyle\leq-\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Phi(t-3\varepsilon L)\leq-\Phi(t),

where the last step follows from the fact that
\Phi
is a good rate function which is a consequence of the facts that
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are good rate functions,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are continuous, and
\mathcal{K}
is compact.

On the other hand as
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are good rate functions we deduce that

\lx@nobreakspace\limsup_{M\to+\infty}\limsup_{\delta\to+\infty}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{v_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}\big(X_{n,\delta}^{(1)}\vee X_{n,\delta}^{(2)}>M\big)=-\infty.

This together with (
C
) end the proof.
∎
